The waived fee removes the joining barrier, the annual option gives cash up front (12 × €800 = €9,600, minus two months = €8,000), and the quarterly review is a continuity bonus that recurs.

##### Gym (local business)

A gym charges €60 per month. New members who sign up today get a free body-composition scan and a nutrition plan (continuity bonus). The €99 joining fee is waived for anyone who commits to six months, and charged if they leave before. Those who prepay a year pay €600 instead of €720, which is two months free.

##### Bakery (local business)

A bakery sells a bread subscription: one loaf every Friday for €18 per month. Subscribers get a free pastry on their first pickup and a 10% discount on all cakes. The bakery knows its Friday baking volume in advance, and the customers come in every week, which also drives extra purchases.

#### Common mistakes

- **No recurring value.** Charging every month for something delivered once leads to cancellations. The value has to repeat.
- **Discounting without commitment.** A cheaper monthly price with no longer term gives away margin and keeps churn the same.
- **Hiding the terms.** Commitment periods and early-cancellation fees must be clear at signup, or they damage trust and invite disputes.
- **Ignoring retention.** Most of the value of continuity comes from how long people stay. Track churn as closely as signups.
- **Bonuses unrelated to the outcome.** A bonus should help them succeed with the core offer, which also helps them stay.

#### How Alex would apply it

- **He looks for continuity in every business.** His default question is what the customer could keep receiving, because recurring revenue makes acquisition cheaper over time.
- **He rewards joining and staying.** He stacks a bonus for joining with a discount or waived fee tied to a commitment.
- **He pushes toward longer terms.** As a rule of thumb, he prefers annual or longer commitments where the market allows, because they bring cash up front and tend to churn less.
- **He uses upfront cash to fund acquisition.** Prepaid continuity is one of the ways a business can pay for new customers with customers' money.
- **He keeps the terms clean and visible.** Fees and commitments are explained up front so nobody feels trapped.
- **Questions he would ask:** What value can you deliver every month? Why do people cancel, and when? What does it cost you to onboard a customer? What would make a customer commit for a year?

#### Worksheet

Answer for your business, even if you sell one-off projects today.

1. **Recurring value:** What could a customer receive from you every month or quarter?
2. **Bonus:** What member-only bonus would help them get results faster?
3. **Discount:** What would you give for a 12-month commitment or prepayment?
4. **Fee:** What setup cost could you waive in exchange for a minimum term?
5. **Churn:** When and why do customers leave today, and what would make them stay?

## Building Your Money Model

- id: build-your-money-model
- book: $100M Money Models / Putting it together
- original term: Money Model
- key: Core offer first, then attraction, upsell, downsell, continuity. Add one at a time until 30-day gross profit ≥ 2 × CAC.
- related: money-models, client-financed-acquisition, attraction-offers, upsell-offers, continuity-offers

How to assemble a money model piece by piece, starting from your core offer, so that each new customer pays for their own acquisition within 30 days.

#### The gist

A money model is the sequence of offers a customer meets from first contact onwards. You don't design it in one sitting. You start with the thing you already sell well, wrap one offer around it, prove it works, and only then add the next.

<div class="formula" markdown="1">
**Core offer** → **Attraction** (gets them in, covers CAC) → **Upsell** (at the yes) → **Downsell** (at the no) → **Continuity** (keeps them)

**Target:** 30-day gross profit per customer ≥ **2 × CAC**
</div>

The yardstick is cash, measured early. What matters is how much gross profit the average new customer produces in the first 30 days compared with what it cost to get them. When that number beats CAC, growth funds itself. When it beats CAC twice, each customer pays for the next two. The aim is a few products sold through many offers. You change how you package and present the products and leave the products themselves mostly alone.

#### How it works

##### Step 1. Start from the core offer

Write down what you deliver best and what it costs you to deliver. Every other offer in the model borrows its credibility from this one. If the core offer is weak, fix it first (see *$100M Offers*). Then measure three numbers you will need for every later decision:

- **CAC:** all marketing and sales cost in a period ÷ new customers in that period. Include your own selling time.
- **Gross profit per sale:** price minus direct delivery cost.
- **30-day gross profit today:** what an average new customer brings in the first month with your current setup.

##### Step 2. Add one attraction offer

The attraction offer exists to bring the right customer in at the lowest net cost. Its first job is to cover as much of CAC as possible on day one. It must lead naturally into the core offer: a paid diagnostic before an implementation, a short challenge before a membership. Pick one form and stop there. Common forms include a low-priced entry product, "buy X get Y", a decoy next to a premium option, or a challenge where people earn their money back by hitting a target.

##### Step 3. Add one upsell at the moment of the yes

The best moment to offer more is right after someone has said yes, because the decision to trust you is already made. A good upsell solves the next problem the first purchase reveals. The audit shows what to fix, so the upsell is fixing it. Make it a natural next step in the same conversation or the same checkout.

##### Step 4. Add a downsell for the no

Many buyers who say no to the upsell are saying no to the price, size or risk. Give them a smaller step: less scope, a payment plan, or a trial. Take something away or change the terms. Cutting the price of the identical thing teaches people to wait for discounts and punishes those who paid in full.

##### Step 5. Add continuity for retention

Continuity is anything that bills again: a retainer, a membership, a subscription. It smooths cash flow and raises lifetime value. Offer it when the customer has just felt a result, typically near the end of the first engagement. A bonus for joining, or a waived setup fee, helps people decide.

##### Step 6. Sequence and timing

Order matters as much as content. Put the offers that collect cash early inside the first 30 days, because that window decides whether you can afford to acquire the next customer. A useful layout:

| When | Offer | Purpose |
|---|---|---|
| Day 0 | Attraction | Get in, cover most of CAC |
| Day 0 to 14 | Upsell, then downsell for the no | Push 30-day gross profit past CAC |
| Day 21 to 30 | Continuity | Start recurring revenue before the first result fades |

##### Step 7. Test one piece at a time

Change one offer, run it long enough to get a stable take rate, then decide. If you change two things at once you can't tell which one moved the number. Rule of thumb: get the attraction offer working before adding an upsell, and keep adding pieces only while each one raises 30-day gross profit.

##### Step 8. Keep it simple

More products means more delivery, more training and more ways to fail. Most businesses need only a handful of products. You create new offers by changing price, terms, bundling and timing around the same products.

#### Examples

*Examples are invented for illustration.*

Expected gross profit = gross profit per sale × share of new customers who take it. Take rates below are shares of all new customers.

##### AI automation and BI consultancy (B2B)

<div class="ba" markdown="1">
<div class="before" markdown="1">
**Before.** One offer: an automation build at €6,000, gross profit €3,600. Cold prospects rarely commit to a large first purchase, so CAC per client is €4,000. 30-day gross profit ÷ CAC = 3,600 ÷ 4,000 = 0.9×. Every new client drains cash.
</div>
<div class="after" markdown="1">
**After.** A paid Process Audit becomes the entry point. Far more prospects say yes to it, so CAC per audit client drops to €1,400.
</div>
</div>

| Step | Offer | Price | Gross profit | Take rate | Expected GP (30 days) |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Attraction | Process Audit, 2 weeks | €1,500 | €1,000 | 100% | €1,000 |
| Upsell (at audit readout) | First automation build, paid upfront | €6,000 | €3,600 | 40% | €1,440 |
| Downsell (for the 60% who say no) | One quick-win automation | €2,500 | €1,500 | 18% (30% of 60%) | €270 |
| Continuity (for the 58% who bought a build) | Run and improve retainer, billed from signing the build | €900/month | €600 | 29% (half of 58%) | €174 |
| **Total** | | | | | **€2,884** |

30-day gross profit ÷ CAC = 2,884 ÷ 1,400 ≈ **2.06×**. The audit alone covers 1,000 ÷ 1,400 ≈ 71% of CAC. The upsell is what pushes the model past break-even. The retainer barely matters in month one but adds €600 of gross profit every month after.

##### Gym (local business)

<div class="ba" markdown="1">
<div class="before" markdown="1">
**Before.** Membership at €59/month, gross profit €45. CAC from ads €100. 30-day ratio 45 ÷ 100 = 0.45×. Payback takes 100 ÷ 45 ≈ 2.2 months, assuming nobody leaves.
</div>
<div class="after" markdown="1">
**After.** Ads promote a 28-day Kickstart. Membership is offered in week 3, once members see their first results.
</div>
</div>

| Step | Offer | Price | Gross profit | Take rate | Expected GP (30 days) |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Attraction | 28-day Kickstart | €199 | €150 | 100% | €150.00 |
| Upsell (at checkout) | Nutrition coaching and meal plan | €99 | €75 | 40% | €30.00 |
| Downsell (for the 60% who say no) | Meal plan only | €29 | €25 | 18% (30% of 60%) | €4.50 |
| Continuity (offered week 3, first charge day 28) | Membership | €69/month | €52 | 50% | €26.00 |
| **Total** | | | | | **€210.50** |

30-day gross profit ÷ CAC = 210.50 ÷ 100 ≈ **2.1×**. Each new member now returns enough gross profit within a month to pay for about two more, so the gym can spend more on ads without waiting months for the money to come back.

Both models work for the same reason: the first purchase is easy to say yes to, the next offer arrives when trust is highest, and the recurring revenue starts before the first result fades.

#### Common mistakes

- **Building everything at once.** Four new offers launched together give you no idea which one works. Add and test them one by one.
- **Measuring revenue instead of gross profit.** Only gross profit can pay for the next customer. A high-revenue, low-margin offer can look great and still drain cash.
- **Missing the 30-day window.** An upsell sold in month four helps lifetime value but does nothing for funding acquisition today.
- **A downsell that is just a discount.** It trains buyers to say no first. Remove scope or change terms instead.
- **Too many products.** Every new product adds delivery complexity. New offers around existing products are cheaper to test and easier to deliver.

#### How Alex would apply it

- **He starts with the 30-day number.** Before discussing any offer, he wants CAC and 30-day gross profit per customer. Everything else is judged by whether it moves that ratio.
- **He fixes the biggest gap first.** If the first sale covers little of CAC, he works on the attraction offer. If the first sale is fine but nothing follows, he adds an upsell. He builds in that order and adds one piece at a time.
- **He puts the upsell where the yes is.** He would move the next offer into the same call, the same checkout or the same readout meeting where the customer just agreed.
- **He would rather collect cash upfront.** Upfront payments, setup fees and first months bundled into the entry offer pull gross profit into the first 30 days.
- **He cuts products and adds offers.** When a business has many products and weak economics, he would trim the product list and package the best few in different ways.
- **He treats the 2× CAC target as the point where growth stops being capped by cash** (a rule of thumb). Below 1×, he would hold back on scaling ads until the model is fixed.
- **Questions he would ask:** What does a customer cost you, all in? How much gross profit do they bring in the first 30 days? What do people buy right after saying yes? What do the ones who say no actually object to? What keeps a customer paying after the first result? Which one offer would you test next?

#### Worksheet

Fill this in for your own business, with numbers.

1. **Baseline:** What are your CAC and 30-day gross profit per customer today? What is the ratio?
2. **Attraction:** Which easy first purchase leads naturally into your core offer, and how much of CAC could it cover?
3. **Upsell:** What is the next problem your customer faces right after buying? What would you offer at that moment, and at what price?
4. **Downsell:** When people say no, is it price, scope or risk? What smaller step removes that objection?
5. **Continuity:** What value could recur every month? When would you offer it, and with what bonus?
6. **Table:** Build the 30-day table with honest take rates. Which single offer, added next, would move the ratio most?

# Selling & Scaling — From Alex's talks, trainings and podcast

**Close the conversations your offers and leads create, then find and fix the one constraint that limits growth.**

### This collection in one page {#overview}

This is not a book. Alex has not published a book on these topics, so this collection gathers ideas he teaches repeatedly in his talks, trainings and podcast episodes. They fill the gap the three books leave: what happens once a good offer and a steady flow of leads are in place.

The first idea is the **CLOSER framework** for sales conversations. A sales call works best as a diagnosis: the buyer explains why they came, confirms the problem, admits what hasn't worked, and only then hears about the destination your offer leads to. Concerns are handled by returning to the buyer's own words, and the decision is reinforced right after the purchase.

The second idea is **the constraint**, borrowed from Eliyahu Goldratt's Theory of Constraints. At any moment one part of the business limits growth: leads, sales, delivery, retention, and sometimes cash or people. Effort spent anywhere else has little effect. Fix the constraint, and it moves to the next place.

The flow:

1. **Make the offer and get the leads** (the three books).
2. **Close the conversations** with CLOSER, so leads become clients.
3. **Find the constraint** with the doubling test and your funnel numbers.
4. **Fix only that,** saying no to everything else for now.
5. **Repeat** when the constraint moves.

### How Alex would use these ideas {#alex}

- He checks the constraint before any other advice, because the right next step depends on where growth is stuck.
- When sales is the constraint, he reviews recorded calls against CLOSER and trains the weakest step.
- He fixes conversion, delivery or retention before buying more leads, so each new lead is worth more.
- He keeps the owner's time on the constraint and pauses projects that don't touch it.
- He re-runs the diagnosis regularly and treats a moved constraint as progress.

## The CLOSER Framework

- id: closer
- book: Selling & Scaling / Sales
- original term: CLOSER Framework
- key: Clarify, Label, Overview, Sell the vacation, Explain away concerns, Reinforce
- related: value-equation, grand-slam-offer, guarantees, constraint, charge-what-its-worth

Six steps that turn a sales call into a diagnosis the buyer walks through themselves. Understand first, show the destination second, clear doubts last.

#### The gist

A good sales call is a **diagnosis**. The buyer arrives with a problem, a reason it matters now, and a history of failed attempts to fix it. Your job is to make all three visible to them, then show them where they could be, then help them deal with what still holds them back.

<div class="formula" markdown="1">
**C**larify why they're here · **L**abel them with a problem · **O**verview past pain · **S**ell the vacation · **E**xplain away concerns · **R**einforce the decision
</div>

The order matters more than any single line. The first three letters are entirely about the buyer. Only once they have confirmed the problem in their own words and admitted that past attempts didn't work does it make sense to talk about your offer. A seller who jumps straight to S is pitching to someone who hasn't yet bought the problem.

#### How it works

##### C: Clarify why they're here

People don't book a call for no reason. Something happened: a lost customer, a new demand from the board, another missed deadline. That trigger drives the whole conversation. Open with a question about it ("What made you reach out now?") and keep asking until you hear the real reason, which is rarely the first polite answer. If the buyer doesn't know why they came, they won't know why they should buy.

##### L: Label them with a problem they confirm

When you have heard enough, sum the problem up in one sentence and end with a question: "So the issue is that you have the data but can't trust it in time for board meetings. Is that right?" The buyer must confirm it. Once they say "yes, exactly", the problem belongs to them. If they correct you, that is useful too: you now have a sharper picture. A label is a mirror you hold up. It is never a diagnosis you push on them.

##### O: Overview past pain

Ask what they have already tried, how long it took, what it cost and why it didn't work. This does three things:

1. The buyer admits, to themselves, that their current approaches won't solve it.
2. You learn what not to offer, because it has already failed.
3. You can separate the problem from blame: the past solution failed because it was the wrong fit, which keeps the buyer's pride intact.

##### S: Sell the vacation, not the plane flight

Nobody books a holiday for the flight. They want the beach. Describe what the buyer's week or month looks like once the problem is solved, in their own words from C and L. Mention the process only as far as it makes the promise believable. Every extra detail about the plane (methodology, meetings, architecture) is another thing that can feel tiring or risky, which raises effort and lowers perceived likelihood in the Value Equation.

##### E: Explain away concerns

Concerns are normal and usually mean "I'm not sure yet". In Alex's teaching they commonly come down to a few groups:

| Group | What it sounds like |
|---|---|
| Circumstances | "I don't have time right now", "It's too expensive" |
| Other people | "I need to ask my partner / the director" |
| Self-doubt | "It won't work for us", "I'm not sure I can do it" |

A practical sequence: repeat the concern back, ask whether it is the only thing holding them back, then return to what they told you in C, L and O. Their reason, their problem and their failed attempts are your best arguments, because they are theirs. Don't argue. Arguing makes people defend their position.

##### R: Reinforce the decision

Right after buying, many people feel buyer's remorse. The sale doesn't end at payment. Congratulate them, tell them exactly what happens next and when, and get them to a first contact or a first small win as fast as possible. Reinforcement cuts cancellations and refunds and starts the experience that later produces referrals.

#### Examples

*Examples are invented for illustration.*

##### Call with a manufacturer's CEO (B2B analytics)

<div class="dialogue" markdown="1">
**Seller (Clarify):** What made you get in touch this month in particular?

**Client:** At the last board meeting I couldn't say which customers actually make us money. It was embarrassing.

**Seller (Label):** So the issue isn't a lack of data. You don't get it in time, in a form you trust. Is that right?

**Client:** Exactly. We have numbers, but every department has its own.

**Seller (Overview):** What have you tried so far?

**Client:** Last year we bought a reporting tool. We pay for licences, one person used it, and she's now on maternity leave.

**Seller (Sell):** Picture the next board meeting. You open one page, see margin by customer for last month, and answer every question in seconds without calling accounting.

**Client (concern):** Sounds good, but we'll pay again for something nobody uses.

**Seller (Explain):** That's exactly what happened last year, so it's a fair worry. Is it the only thing holding you back? In the first 30 days we build the report and review it with you twice before the meeting. Nobody on your side has to learn the tool.

**Seller (Reinforce, after signing):** Good decision. Our analyst calls you tomorrow at nine, and you'll have the first version of the report by Friday.
</div>

The concern was self-doubt ("it won't work for us"). The seller didn't argue. He used the client's own story from the Overview step and tied it to a specific part of the offer that removes the effort that caused the last failure.

##### Adult braces consultation (dental clinic)

<div class="ba" markdown="1">
<div class="before" markdown="1">
**Before.** Reception explains bracket types, materials, treatment length in months and the price. The patient says she'll think about it and never comes back.
</div>
<div class="after" markdown="1">
**After.** First the question why now: her sister's wedding in a year. Label: "You don't like smiling in photos, is that right?" Then what she has tried. Then the destination: smiling in the wedding photos. The money concern is handled with instalments, and the clinic calls that same week to confirm the first appointment.
</div>
</div>

Same product, same price list. The first version sold the plane (materials and months). The second sold the vacation, and the follow-up call was the R step.

##### Concerns in quote automation (AI consulting)

| Group | Client says | Possible response |
|---|---|---|
| Circumstances (time) | "We're in the middle of the season." | "The season is when quotes cost you the most hours. How many will you write this spring?" |
| Circumstances (money) | "The budget is spent." | Compare the fee with the monthly cost of manual work they named in Overview. |
| Other people | "I need to ask the director." | "What will the director ask? Let's prepare the answers, or invite them to a short call." |
| Self-doubt | "Our processes are too specific." | "Step one is a working prototype on three of your real enquiries." |

#### Common mistakes

- **Jumping straight to the pitch.** Without C, L and O there is nothing to build on. The buyer hears a solution to a problem they haven't voiced, and everything sounds like cost.
- **Stating the problem yourself.** If the buyer doesn't confirm it in their own words, they don't own it. Every label ends with a question.
- **Selling the plane.** Technical sellers love explaining architecture and methodology. Each detail is a new place for a concern to appear.
- **Arguing with the concern.** It hardens the buyer's position. Isolate it and go back to their own words.
- **Going silent after the signature.** A few days without contact is enough for doubt to grow. Plan the first 48 hours before the call.

#### How Alex would apply it

- **He treats the call as the buyer's conversation.** The seller asks most of the questions and the buyer does most of the talking, especially in the first half.
- **He makes the buyer say the problem.** A problem the seller states is an opinion. A problem the buyer confirms is a reason to buy.
- **He uses past pain as leverage.** What they tried and why it failed becomes the case for doing something different now.
- **He keeps the pitch short and outcome-first.** If the offer needs a long explanation, he fixes the offer before he fixes the pitch.
- **He sees most concerns as avoidance of a decision,** and helps the buyer make one, yes or no, instead of leaving it open. As a rule of thumb, a clear no is better than a vague maybe.
- **He reviews recorded calls against the six steps** to find where deals are lost, then trains that step.
- **Questions he would ask:** Why did they reach out now? Did they confirm the problem in their own words? What have they already tried, and why did it fail? Did you describe the destination or the process? Which concern did they raise, and did you isolate it? What happened in the first 48 hours after they paid?

#### Worksheet

Take your last two sales conversations, or one coming up, and answer in writing.

1. **Clarify:** What is your opening question that reveals why the buyer got in touch right now?
2. **Label:** Write the last buyer's problem as one sentence that ends with "is that right?".
3. **Overview:** Which three solutions do your buyers usually try before you, and why don't they work?
4. **Sell:** Describe the vacation of your offer in two sentences without a single technical word.
5. **Explain:** List the concerns you hear most. Which group does each belong to, and what is your response?
6. **Reinforce:** What happens in the first 48 hours after purchase, and what should the buyer feel by the end of them?

## The Constraint

- id: constraint
- book: Selling & Scaling / Scaling
- original term: Constraint (bottleneck)
- key: Find the one constraint, fix it, find the next one. Test: if leads doubled tomorrow, what breaks first?
- related: core-four, closer, money-models, continuity-offers, rule-of-100

At any moment a business has one main constraint that limits its growth. Work on anything else has little effect until that one is fixed, and then the constraint moves.

#### The gist

A business works like a pipe with several sections: leads come in, some become sales, sales become delivered work, and delivered clients stay or leave. Only as much flows through the whole pipe as the **narrowest section** lets through. Widening any other section changes nothing, because the flow still waits at the same point.

<div class="formula" markdown="1">
**One constraint at a time** → fix it → the constraint **moves** → find the next one
</div>

The idea comes from Eliyahu Goldratt's **Theory of Constraints**, set out in his book *The Goal*. Alex uses it as a simple operating rule for founders: find the one thing that limits growth right now, put your effort there, and say no to good ideas elsewhere until the constraint has moved.

#### How it works

##### There is always one main constraint

A business always has many problems, but usually one of them matters most: the one whose improvement would raise the result of the whole business the most. The common places are:

- **Leads.** Too few of the right people hear about you.
- **Sales conversion.** People come, but they don't buy.
- **Delivery or capacity.** You sell more than you can fulfil well.
- **Retention.** Clients leave too soon (churn).
- **Sometimes cash or people.** Growth stalls on cash flow, or on one key person who is missing or overloaded.

##### Work elsewhere is low leverage

If delivery is the constraint, another ad campaign brings more disappointed clients and longer queues. If retention is the constraint, a new website fills a leaking bucket. This work looks productive but doesn't move the result, and it can make it worse. Hence the rule: say no to anything that isn't the constraint, good ideas included.

##### Find it with a thought experiment

The quickest test is to ask: **"If my leads doubled tomorrow, what would break first?"**

- "Nothing, we'd handle them happily" means leads are the constraint.
- "We wouldn't have time for all the calls" or "most wouldn't buy" means sales.
- "We couldn't deliver on time" means delivery or capacity.
- "We'd sign them, but they'd be gone in a few months" means retention.
- "We couldn't fund the extra work" or "it all goes through me" means cash or people.

##### Confirm it with funnel numbers

Write down the numbers for each step of the last quarter: leads, calls held, new clients, onboarded clients, clients still active after a set period. Calculate the conversion at each step and compare it with a reasonable expectation for your market. The step that falls furthest short, or where work and people pile up in a queue, is the candidate. The thought experiment gives you a hypothesis. The numbers confirm or reject it.

##### Fix it, then it moves

When you remove a constraint, it doesn't disappear. It moves. More leads make sales the new bottleneck. Better sales overload delivery. Better delivery reveals that clients don't stay. That is progress, not failure. Finding the constraint is a standing habit, for example a monthly review, rather than a one-off project.

##### Goldratt's five focusing steps, simplified

1. **Identify** the constraint.
2. **Exploit** it: get the most out of it with what you already have, so it never sits idle.
3. **Subordinate** everything else to it.
4. **Elevate** it with new people, systems or money.
5. **Repeat** once it has moved.

#### Examples

*Examples are invented for illustration.*

##### One quarter's funnel (B2B analytics SaaS)

A small company sells an analytics platform to manufacturers. The founder wants to spend more on ads, but first looks at the numbers.

| Step | Count | Conversion | Expected |
|---|---|---|---|
| Leads | 240 | | |
| Calls held | 150 | 63 % | 60 % |
| New clients | 30 | 20 % | 20 % |
| Onboarded within 30 days | 27 | 90 % | 85 % |
| Active after 6 months | 12 | 44 % | 80 % |

Every step meets expectations except retention. More ads would bring more clients, and the company would lose more than half of them within six months. The constraint is retention. The first move is a monthly usage review with each client and an alert when someone hasn't opened the platform for two weeks. Only once retention rises does it pay to scale ads.

##### Full calendar, flat revenue (car repair shop)

<div class="ba" markdown="1">
<div class="before" markdown="1">
**Before.** The owner thinks he needs more customers and pays for social ads. Calls go up, but the wait for an appointment grows to three weeks and some regulars go elsewhere.
</div>
<div class="after" markdown="1">
**After.** The "if leads doubled" test shows it at once: the workshop would break. The constraint is one lift and one diagnostic technician. The owner pauses the ads, adds a second shift on Thursdays and Fridays, and pre-orders parts so cars don't sit on the lift waiting.
</div>
</div>

He had been improving a step that wasn't the constraint and made the customer experience worse. Once the workshop can handle more, leads become the constraint again, and that is when ads make sense.

##### Diagnostic table (AI consultancy)

A general aid for locating the constraint. Compare the symptoms with your business.

| Constraint | Symptoms | Number to watch | First move |
|---|---|---|---|
| Leads | Empty calendar, sellers waiting | New qualified leads per week | More volume on one channel |
| Sales | Many calls, few signatures | Close rate | Call reviews, a script, a stronger offer |
| Delivery | Delays, queues, overloaded team | Time to first result | Standardise, automate, hire |
| Retention | Clients leave after a few months | Monthly churn | Fast first win, regular check-ins |
| Cash or people | Growth stalls on cash or one person | Months of runway, owner hours in delivery | Upfront payments, delegation |

An AI consultancy has enough leads and a good close rate, but the founder runs every project personally. The symptoms point to delivery and people. Instead of new campaigns, the founder writes a standard process for the first project and brings in a project lead.

#### Common mistakes

- **Working on everything at once.** Ten small improvements move little, because the constraint gets only a tenth of the attention.
- **Defaulting to "we need more clients".** Leads are the most common answer and often the wrong one. More clients into leaky retention or overloaded delivery does harm.
- **Choosing by gut instead of numbers.** Founders like working on what they enjoy. Without funnel numbers, picking the constraint often means picking a hobby.
- **Forgetting that it moves.** After one constraint is solved, some keep doing the same work. Re-run the test after every improvement.

#### How Alex would apply it

- **He asks what the constraint is before giving any advice.** Most business questions have a different right answer depending on where the bottleneck sits.
- **He runs the doubling test first,** then checks it against the funnel numbers.
- **He puts the owner's time on the constraint** and cuts or pauses projects that don't touch it, even promising ones.
- **He is wary of adding leads to a broken funnel.** Fixing conversion, delivery or retention first makes every future lead worth more.
- **He exploits before he elevates:** squeeze more out of the current team, process and offer before hiring or spending.
- **He expects the constraint to move** and reviews it regularly, treating a new bottleneck as a sign of progress.
- **Questions he would ask:** If leads doubled tomorrow, what breaks first? What are your numbers at each step? Where do people or work queue up? How much of delivery still depends on you? What would you stop doing this month if you took the answer seriously?

#### Worksheet

Take the last three months of your business and answer in writing.

1. **Test:** If your leads doubled tomorrow, what would break first?
2. **Funnel:** Write down the numbers for each step (leads, calls, deals, onboarding, retention). Where is the biggest gap against what you'd expect?
3. **Queues:** Where do work or clients pile up and wait?
4. **Focus:** Which three projects that don't touch the constraint will you pause this month?
5. **Action:** What one action widens the constraint within 30 days, and how will you measure it?
6. **Next:** Where is the constraint likely to move once you fix it?

# Alex's Playbook — How Alex diagnoses and decides, distilled from 100+ videos

**Alex solves most business problems the same way, by finding the one constraint, checking the numbers and choosing two or three moves in the right order.**

### The playbook in one page {#overview}

The four books describe Alex's frameworks: how to build an offer, get leads, structure a money model and close. This layer captures something different: how he thinks when a real owner tells him about a real problem. It is distilled from 103 of his videos (hotseats, live Q&As, lectures and sales trainings). The transcripts stay private. What you read here is paraphrased, organised by theme, and every entry lists the videos it comes from, with timestamps.

The books answer "what is the framework?". The playbook answers "what would he do first, what would he ignore, and why?". It favours patterns that recur across many videos, marks single-video points as such, and keeps his numbers as rules of thumb with their context. Where the notes say nothing, the playbook says nothing.

Across all of the videos, the same operating principles keep coming back:

1. **Find the one constraint.** First triage is supply versus demand: if customers doubled, what would break? Then attack the funnel stage furthest below benchmark and ignore the rest.
2. **The stated problem is rarely the real one.** "Leads" often turns out to be the offer or the sales process, "ads" turns out to be LTV, "people" turns out to be a broken model.
3. **Numbers before advice.** He asks in a fixed order: what you sell, to whom, at what price, how you acquire, close rate, LTV, CAC, churn, gross margin, capacity. Lifetime gross profit to CAC is the core lens.
4. **More, then better, then new.** Squeeze what already works before trying anything new. Never switch off a working channel to test another.
5. **Focus and subtract.** One business, one avatar, one channel, one priority, roughly until $1M a year. Kill side ventures and losing units.
6. **Price is the first and cheapest lever.** Most owners are underpriced; very high close rates and thin margins are warning signs. Add bonuses instead of discounting.
7. **Pull cash forward.** Solve cash problems through offer design (prepay, up-front fees, quick upsells) before debt or capital, so CAC comes back within about 30 days.
8. **Fix the leaky bucket before buying traffic.** Retention and lifetime value are where the advantage lives, and churn is usually an onboarding or communication problem.
9. **Talent is the long-term constraint.** Recruit like you acquire customers, pay more for people who have done it before, and judge people by the metrics they track.
10. **Move the founder from doer to teacher to leader.** Reducing founder dependence is the route from income to enterprise value.
11. **Nail it before you scale it.** No second location, product or business until the first is profitable and runs without the owner. Better before bigger.
12. **Change is costly, so choose few moves.** Two or three highest-impact moves, sequenced by impact, confidence and ease, one change at a time.

### How Alex would use these ideas {#alex}

- **Start with the goal and the numbers.** Ask what the owner actually wants (cash, freedom or an exit), then collect the key metrics from the diagnose section before forming any opinion.
- **Name the constraint as one question.** Decide whether the business is supply- or demand-constrained, find the weakest stage against benchmark, and phrase it as "how do I…?".
- **Place the business on its stage.** Revenue and headcount decide which rules apply; the scale section shows what the founder should give up next.
- **Pick two or three moves from the levers in his order:** price and cash first, then retention and the sales process, then more of the working channel, and only then something new.
- **Check who will execute.** If the owner or a key person is the bottleneck, the people section comes before any growth tactic.
- **Calibrate against a similar case.** Find the closest business in the casebook and compare its diagnosis with yours.
- **Say what not to do.** End with a short sequenced plan for the next 90 days and the distractions to drop.

## The Diagnostic Sequence

- id: diagnostic-sequence
- book: Alex's Playbook / How he diagnoses
- original term: Diagnostic Sequence
- key: Numbers → supply or demand → weakest stage vs benchmark → one "how do I…" question → 2–3 moves
- related: constraint, business-math, prioritising-moves, focus-and-subtraction, stages-of-growth

The fixed order of questions Alex runs on any business before he gives advice. Numbers first, then the one constraint, then two or three moves.

#### The gist

Alex diagnoses almost every business the same way. He asks for the numbers before he listens to the story, because the problem the owner brings is rarely the problem the numbers show. "We need more leads" turns out to be an offer problem, "ads stopped working" turns out to be a lifetime value problem, and "my team is the issue" turns out to be a broken model.

Then he sorts the business into one of two buckets: it cannot get enough customers (demand) or it cannot serve more customers (supply). Inside that bucket he finds the single stage that sits furthest below a healthy benchmark, names it as a "how do I…" question, and limits his advice to the two or three moves that attack it.

<div class="formula" markdown="1">
**Numbers** → **Supply or demand?** → **Weakest stage vs benchmark** → **One "how do I…" question** → **2–3 moves**, then the owner's real goal decides the plan
</div>

#### How it works

##### 1. The fixed order of questions

In his live hotseats the questions come in nearly the same order every time: what do you sell, to whom, at what price, how do customers find you, how many sales a month, what is the close rate, what is a customer worth over their lifetime, what does it cost to get one, how many leave, what is the gross margin, how much capacity is left. Only then does he ask what the owner actually wants.

He expects owners to know these by heart. In one walkthrough he reduces it to six numbers: new sales per month, current revenue, price, churn, lifetime value and gross margin, and notes that most owners cannot name the last three. In another he says plainly that he breaks down unit economics before asking about payroll or anything else, because that is where the problem shows up.

##### 2. Distrust the stated problem

A recurring pattern across many videos: the owner names a symptom, the numbers point somewhere else. A practice with three "separate" problems (low margin, pricing set by insurers, one channel) had one model problem. A founder listing six problems was usually missing around three senior people. An owner who wants to start a new business after a plateau usually does not know how to grow the current one. Alex treats the stated problem as a hypothesis and checks it against the numbers.

He also separates a complaint from a constraint: if you know more spend would double the business and you are not doing it, your chores are not the constraint.

##### 3. Supply or demand triage

His first real question in most hotseats: is this business supply-constrained or demand-constrained? The test is concrete. If leads, ad spend or outreach doubled tomorrow, would sales double, or would you create a mess? A mess means supply. He says you can only have one of the two at a time, and the constraint swings back and forth like an accordion. Owners stay stuck for years because they keep working on the side they already solved.

| If the answer is… | The work is… | What he refuses |
|---|---|---|
| Demand (capacity is free) | Offer, price, conversion, creative, then more spend | Fixing delivery or hiring ahead of growth |
| Supply (doubling would break delivery) | Raise price, change the delivery ratio, productise, hire | More marketing; a second business "to market more" |

On the supply side he goes further: tolerate mediocre marketing while capacity is full, because improving it would make the business worse.

##### 4. Compare each stage to a benchmark

Inside the funnel he walks the stages (click, opt-in, booking, show, close, repeat purchase) and compares each to the numbers he knows. He fixes the stage furthest below its benchmark, because that is where a 2–4x is possible. Another version of the same idea: add the same small percentage to every stage and see which one moves output the most. That is usually the weakest stage.

He normally optimises acquisition front to back, since early stages touch the most people. The exception is one stage far below the norm, such as a 5% show rate where 60% is normal. A 60% show rate cannot triple; a 1% click-through can.

##### 5. Turn the constraint into a "how do I…" question

Once he has the constraint, he rewrites it as a solvable skill question: "how do I recruit, train and manage a sales team", "how do I get customers from a channel I am allowed to use". In one story he refused to discuss pricing, messaging or lifetime gross profit with an owner until the named constraint was solved.

##### 6. Limit the advice to two or three moves

Hotseats end with a short, ordered list. He writes the easy, high-impact moves first and the big bets last, and repeatedly leaves alone the parts that already work (a converting offer, a sales process with healthy close rates). In one case he trimmed his plan to two steps the owner would actually carry out. He also orders the fixes so that cheap conversion gains come before more spend, since they may already push the business into a supply constraint.

##### 7. Then the owner's real goal

Before he commits to a plan he asks what the owner wants: double it, sell it, or simply make money. The answer changes the advice. If the owner only wants cash, he avoids complexity and repeats the working playbook. If they want to sell, concentration risk and owner dependence move up the list. In some talks he asks the goal first, together with current state, desired state and the obstacle between them.

#### Examples

*Cases are paraphrased and anonymised from Alex's videos. Any example marked as invented is for illustration only.*

##### Duct-cleaning service, about $1.25M revenue

<div class="ba" markdown="1">
<div class="before" markdown="1">
**Before.** The owner asks for more and better leads. Numbers: 38% net margin, 99% show rate, 82% close rate, ads returning roughly 13 to 1, one active referral partner.
</div>
<div class="after" markdown="1">
**After.** Triage: demand-constrained, nothing badly broken, so incremental moves. A 10% price rise first (an 82% close rate signals underpricing), then a dedicated landing page, more ad creative and spend, reactivation emails, and outbound last.
</div>
</div>

Price and conversion fixes come before spend, so the new van and staff are not swamped.

##### Online weight-loss program, about $4M, webinar funnel

The owner was nervous about launching a big new lead magnet. Alex compared each stage to his numbers: show rate at 30% was already above benchmark, opt-in sat at 5% where 20% or more is possible. All testing went to the opt-in page and the lead magnet's name, because the stage furthest below benchmark held the largest multiple.

##### Business at about $150K a month

Many things could have been improved, including the pricing model and messaging. Alex named one constraint (he had never built a sales team), rewrote it as "how do I recruit, manage and train salespeople", and declined to talk about anything else on the follow-up call.

##### Qlik consultancy, invented

A two-person BI consultancy says it needs more leads. Numbers: 6 proposals a month, 5 signed (83% close), both consultants booked for the next four months. Triage answer: doubling leads would break delivery, so it is supply. The question becomes "how do I deliver the same result with less senior time?" Two moves: raise the day rate on new work, and split delivery so a junior builds the data model while seniors handle design and sign-off.

#### Common mistakes

- **Starting from the story.** Advice given before the numbers usually treats a symptom.
- **Working the solved side.** Buying more leads while delivery is full, or hiring while capacity sits idle.
- **Fixing everything at once.** A list of ten fixes gets none of them done; he keeps it to two or three.
- **Touching what works.** Rewriting an offer or script that converts well, instead of attacking the weak stage.
- **Skipping the goal.** The right plan for a cash-flow owner differs from the plan for someone building to sell.

#### How Alex would apply it

- **Numbers before opinions.** He asks for price, volume, close rate, churn, lifetime value, acquisition cost and gross margin before he says anything about strategy.
- **Supply or demand first.** If doubling customers would break delivery, he works on capacity and price. If not, it is a pure demand problem, and he works on offer and traffic.
- **The weakest stage gets everything.** He benchmarks each funnel stage and puts all testing on the one furthest below its norm.
- **One constraint, phrased as a skill.** He turns it into a "how do I…" question and refuses side topics until it is solved.
- **Short ordered list.** Two or three moves, cheapest and highest impact first, working parts left alone.
- **A new venture is a red flag.** If the owner would drop the new idea once the current business tripled, it is an escape from the constraint.
- **Questions he would ask:** What do you sell, to whom, at what price? Where do customers come from? What are your show and close rates? What is a customer worth and what does one cost? If customers doubled, what breaks? Why aren't you spending ten times more on ads? What do you want: double it, sell it, or take cash out?

#### Worksheet

Answer with numbers, not impressions.

1. **The numbers:** Write down new sales per month, price, close rate, monthly churn, gross margin and acquisition cost. Mark the ones you had to guess.
2. **Triage:** If leads doubled next month, what would break first? Is that a supply or a demand answer?
3. **Benchmarks:** List your funnel stages with today's rate next to each. Which is furthest below a healthy level?
4. **The question:** Rewrite your constraint as one "how do I…" sentence.
5. **The moves:** Pick at most three moves that attack it, in order. What are you deliberately leaving untouched?
6. **The goal:** Do you want to grow it, sell it, or take cash out? Does the plan still fit?

- [How to Grow Your Business So FAST it Makes Your Accountant Nervous](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=sGv2BTUCcCM&t=47s) — the doubling test, supply vs demand triage and the accordion (00:47)
- [Building a $2,500,000 Business for a Stranger in 36 Minutes](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=OQf2Ba-Lp_4&t=234s) — full hotseat run: triage, ordered list of fixes (03:54)
- [Entrepreneurs, stop starting new businesses. Fix the one you've got.](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=s59jbVBprn0&t=432s) — naming one constraint as a "how do I…" question (07:12)
- ["He Makes $1.5M a Year... and Keeps 1.3% of It" | Alex Hormozi Answers Live](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=lidFtb1Ga0o&t=2010s) — funnel stages vs benchmarks, attack the furthest-below step (33:30)
- [I Reverse Engineered the Perfect Business](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=HKbFUWJwEG0&t=2824s) — theory of constraints applied to funnel stages (47:04)
- ["My business has stopped growing..what should I do?"](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=f_ceDZqhKbw&t=49s) — the six numbers he asks for first (00:49)
- [Business Owners: You NEED to Know This Number](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jzKpAtzKQ54&t=143s) — unit economics before anything else; the front-to-back exception (02:23)
- [13 Years Of Brutally Honest Business Advice in 90 Mins](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KhFlD54nQrY&t=624s) — goal, current state, desired state, obstacle; "why not 10x on ads?" (10:24)
- [Building a $12,000,000 Business for a Stranger in 25 Minutes](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=MH-IMJxbUY4&t=286s) — supply or demand first, then double, sell or cash out (04:46)
- ["Should He Walk Away From His $1.4M Business To Start Over?" | Alex Hormozi Answers Live](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0RjIw9M1j28&t=4590s) — the fixed question order ending in "what do you want?" (76:30)

## Business Math & Benchmarks

- id: business-math
- book: Alex's Playbook / How he diagnoses
- original term: Business Math (LTGP, LTV:CAC, payback)
- key: LTGP = price ÷ churn × gross margin; 30-day cash > CAC + delivery cost
- related: diagnostic-sequence, client-financed-acquisition, pricing-decisions, retention-and-ltv, cash-forward

The handful of numbers Alex runs in his head on every business, and the benchmarks he compares them to. Choosing between options becomes a math problem.

#### The gist

Alex treats business math as a language: you can only think as clearly about a business as your vocabulary for its numbers allows. In conversation he runs the same small set of calculations before he advises anyone: what a customer is worth in gross profit, what it costs to get one, how fast that cost comes back, and what the goal implies in leads, calls and budget.

There are only two ways to grow: more customers, or more value per customer. He keeps finding that the second is easier. Since acquisition cost for a given customer is roughly the same for every competitor in a market, the lasting advantage goes to whoever makes a customer worth the most.

<div class="formula" markdown="1">
**LTGP** = (price ÷ monthly churn) × gross margin  ·  **Max CAC** = LTGP  ·  **Client-financed** when 30-day cash collected > CAC + cost to deliver
</div>

#### How it works

##### Two growth levers

Every improvement should move one of a few levers: more customers, more lifetime gross profit per customer, or lower risk that the earnings continue. In another talk he names four: traffic, conversion, price and churn, and says a task that does not ladder up to one of them should not be done.

His "genie" question makes the point. If you could double new customers, price, or purchases per customer, which would you pick? At a 20% margin, doubling customers doubles profit, while doubling price gives about six times the profit, because the extra revenue is all margin. He also prefers doubling retention over doubling new customers: profit ends up similar, but the higher lifetime value lets you outspend competitors for the next customer.

##### Lifetime gross profit, not revenue

He insists that lifetime value means lifetime gross profit (LTGP): price minus the cost to deliver, times the number of purchases or months. Revenue overstates it. Fixed overhead such as rent and non-delivery staff stays out. One example he uses: a $10 meal that costs $9 to deliver, bought with $5 of acquisition cost, looks like 2:1 on revenue and is really $5 paid to earn $1.

His back-of-napkin version:

1. Churn = cancellations this month ÷ active customers.
2. LTV = price ÷ churn.
3. LTGP = LTV × gross margin.
4. Revenue cap = new sales per month × LTV. Growth flattens as churn eats new sales.

A young business can estimate it from one month: if 9 of 10 customers stay, churn is 10% and LTV is price ÷ 0.10. He tells owners to calculate it conservatively and round down.

##### Acquisition cost and the ratio

CAC is marketing payroll plus media plus sales commissions over a period, divided by new customers, ideally over a full year. The ratio he wants depends on how many humans sit in lead generation, sales and delivery: about 3:1 if all three are automated, rising to 6:1, 9:1 and 12:1 or more as people are added, because new hires ramp slowly and CAC only rises over time. He optimises the ratio rather than the cheapest lead: $5,000 LTV at $500 CAC beats $1,000 at $200.

##### 30-day cash vs CAC

His payback target is 30 days. That is about as long as a small business can carry the cost, and it matches interest-free card terms. When gross profit collected in the first 30 days exceeds the cost to acquire and deliver, growth stops being limited by cash. If payback is longer, he looks for setup fees, prepaid terms, bundled products or upsells inside the first month.

##### Backward math from the goal

He works targets backwards. In one worked example: 300 members at 10% monthly churn means 30 replacements a month just to stay flat. At 50% front-end conversion that is 60 front-end sales, at a 50% close rate 120 conversations, at a 50% show rate about 240 bookings, which took roughly 15 leads a day. At about $10 a lead the budget comes to around $4,500 a month, and growth means doubling it.

##### Benchmarks he states

These are his rules of thumb from many videos, with the context they came in. He also warns against industry averages as targets and asks instead whether physics forbids a better result.

| Metric | His rule of thumb |
|---|---|
| Close rate | 80%+ means underpriced (he says by 3–4x); 30–40% is about right with pre-call education; under 30% means fix the avatar and sales process before touching price |
| Show rate | ~25%+ for a cold webinar; a small paid upfront fee often gives 70–80%; ~80% means lead handling is fine |
| Opt-in | ~30% on a lead-magnet page; qualified pages 20–50% |
| Webinar booking | ~20% of attendees with a book-a-call CTA |
| Gross margin | 80%+ for services; he will not invest below that; delivery cost about a fifth of price |
| Retention | monthly churn 20%+ in months 1–3, about 10% after day 90, about 2% after month six; B2B annual retention above 80% |
| Payback | CAC back within 30 days; let a new ad campaign spend up to 2x allowable CAC before cutting it |

##### ROAS vs absolute profit

He optimises for absolute profit. A lower return ratio on much larger spend can net far more money, so a falling ratio is no reason to stop. In one hotseat with search ads returning 9 to 24 times spend, he said he would keep raising spend until it reached about 5:1.

#### Examples

*Cases are paraphrased and anonymised from Alex's videos. Any example marked as invented is for illustration only.*

##### Subscription service at $1,000 a month

The owner had 380 clients, 100 new sales a month and 50 cancellations, and did not know churn. Alex: 50 ÷ 380 is about 13% churn, LTV about $7,700, LTGP about $7,000 at a 90% margin. Cap: 100 × $7,700 is about $770,000 a month. Cutting churn to about 3% lifts LTV to roughly $33,000 and the cap to about $3.3M a month, which is far easier than ten times the lead flow.

##### Duct-cleaning service, 38% net margin

<div class="ba" markdown="1">
<div class="before" markdown="1">
**Before.** About $1.25M revenue, about $479,000 profit, 82% close rate.
</div>
<div class="after" markdown="1">
**After.** A 10% price rise adds about $125,000 that falls straight to profit: roughly 25% more net profit, held as long as close rate stays above 65%.
</div>
</div>

Thin or middling margins make price the strongest lever, which is why low margins excite him.

##### Analytics retainer, invented

A BI firm sells a €3,000/month retainer with €900 delivery cost (70% gross margin, below his 80% rule). Churn is 4% a month: LTV €75,000, LTGP €52,500, rounded down to €45,000. CAC is €9,000, so the ratio is 5:1, while human sales and human delivery call for 9–12:1. First-month gross profit is €2,100 against €9,000 CAC. Moves: a €6,000 onboarding fee plus a prepaid quarter brings in €15,000 within 30 days, above CAC plus first-quarter delivery (€9,000 + €2,700 = €11,700), and cutting delivery to €600 lifts margin to 80%.

#### Common mistakes

- **Using revenue as LTV.** The ratio looks healthy while each sale loses money.
- **Judging spend by ROAS alone.** Stopping a channel because the ratio fell, while absolute profit was still rising.
- **Not knowing churn.** Without it you cannot see where revenue will cap.
- **Cutting price to fix sales.** A 20% cut on a 40% margin needs twice the sales just to break even.
- **Copying industry averages.** He benchmarks against winners and limits, not against the median.

#### How Alex would apply it

- **He calculates in the conversation.** Churn, LTV, LTGP, cap and payback are worked out on the spot, and he expects the owner to know them without a finance report.
- **Value per customer before more customers.** Price, churn and upsells usually move profit more, and with less effort, than doubling acquisition.
- **Gross margin is the first health check.** A service under 80% gets fixed through price or delivery before anything is scaled.
- **Cash forward until CAC pays back in 30 days.** Setup fees, prepay and early upsells come before debt or investors.
- **Decisions by math.** Pricing and offer choices are settled by conversion × price × LTV per option, never by opinion.
- **Spend while the math holds.** If a dollar reliably returns several, the budget is "as much as possible", judged on absolute profit.
- **Questions he would ask:** What do you charge, and what does it cost to deliver? How many cancel each month? What is a customer worth in gross profit? What does one cost to acquire, and how fast do you get it back? What is your close rate against your price? How many leads a day does your goal need?

#### Worksheet

Use real numbers from the last 12 months.

1. **LTGP:** Price ÷ monthly churn × gross margin. Round it down.
2. **CAC:** Marketing payroll + media + commissions ÷ new customers. What ratio do you have, and what does your human count call for?
3. **Payback:** What gross profit does a new customer bring in the first 30 days? Is it above CAC plus delivery cost?
4. **Benchmarks:** Put your close, show and opt-in rates, gross margin and churn next to his rules of thumb. Which is furthest off?
5. **Backward math:** From your target customer count, work out replacements per month, then sales, calls, bookings, leads per day and budget.

#### Sources

- [The Mathematics of Business, Explained](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=A_tx40lNpf8&t=49s) — close-rate table, LTV:CAC by humans in the loop, 30-day payback, 80% margin (00:49)
- [Business Owners: You NEED to Know This Number](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jzKpAtzKQ54&t=47s) — LTV as lifetime gross profit, how to compute CAC (00:47)
- ["My business has stopped growing..what should I do?"](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=f_ceDZqhKbw&t=144s) — churn, LTV, LTGP and the revenue cap (02:24)
- [KEYNOTE: Small To BIG. The Big 4 Customer Acquisition Models.](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=XwZH-lOKG9c&t=3822s) — the genie question, round LTGP down, client-financed acquisition (63:42)
- [Your Inflow Is Your Bottleneck (ALEX HORMOZI)](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=XC_lklN9KmE&t=585s) — backward math from members to leads and budget (09:45)
- [How I Would Build a Business in 2026 (If I Had to Start Over)](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=i7bLRKwKSms&t=685s) — back-of-napkin CAC and LTV, 30-day recovery (11:25)
- [If You Don't Understand Margin, You Don't Understand Business](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=9xAMe0QBFhU&t=245s) — 80% gross margin minimum for services (04:05)
- [6 Hacks To Make More Money [Increase LTV] Without More Customers](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Q2VUuUGfpNY&t=47s) — LTGP instead of revenue LTV (00:47)
- [How to Start a Business From Nothing (Thank Me Later)](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=unshZobTt6Q&t=3860s) — absolute profit over ROAS (64:20)
