## Focus & Subtraction

- id: focus-and-subtraction
- book: Alex's Playbook / How he decides
- original term: Focus, 1-1-1, subtraction
- key: 1-1-1 = one product, one avatar, one channel until $1M a year. Subtract before you add.
- related: prioritising-moves, constraint, more-better-new, niche, risk-and-bets

Alex's default fix for a stuck or overwhelmed owner is to take things away. One business, one avatar, one channel and one priority, held long enough to compound.

#### The gist

Across the hotseats, the most common thing Alex prescribes is removal. Owners arrive with two businesses, three offers, five channels, a side software product or a partner deal on the side, and describe the result as overwhelm or a plateau. He reads both as self-made complexity. Focus, in his usage, is something you get by subtracting, and he judges it by the number and quality of things you say no to.

<div class="formula" markdown="1">
**1-1-1:** one product, one avatar, one channel until about **$1M a year**. Then: **one priority** at a time, and **subtract before you add**.
</div>

The logic is economic. An owner's attention is the scarcest resource in a small company, and he treats strategy as the allocation of limited time, money and people across unlimited options. Every split of attention and every change has a cost, usually higher than the owner thinks.

#### How it works

##### 1-1-1 until the first $1M

In at least two talks he states the rule directly: one product, one avatar and one channel until roughly $1M a year. He ranks avatar clarity in one Q&A: one product for one avatar is best, one product for many people is next, everything for everyone is worst. He only accepts a new avatar once the current one is exhausted, and when an owner itches for something new, he would rather add a channel than a customer type.

##### Subtract first, then add

Before anyone adds a venture, he wants a list of what can be removed from the current one, and he only lets them proceed if the hours left over are realistic. In one live session he estimates that halving attention to hedge against downside can push year-two results out to year four, because you compete against people who are fully committed.

The recurring subtractions:

- **Kill the vampire.** An unprofitable line that drains cash and founder time from the cash cow gets cut (a side software product, a money-losing course, a second retail location that barely breaks even).
- **Exit side deals and partnerships** as fast as possible. You can own several companies, but he treats being CEO of more than one as the usual failure. His litmus test: if a problem in it rings your phone, you are running it.
- **Fire-sell the weaker business** when the other one is an order of magnitude better, so you get full attention back.
- **Keep passive investments passive.** Other holdings only distract if they change what you do each day.

##### The woman in the red dress

He uses this image (also "girl in the red dress") for the shiny new opportunity that looks greener only because you have not seen its problems yet. It appears in at least four videos: a wholesale owner who wants to build salon software, a B2C coaching company eyeing enterprise clients, a whale pulling a firm off its avatar, and founders who start another business to avoid a new kind of problem. His diagnosis is the same each time: the new venture is an escape from a solvable problem, and the skill gap that caused the plateau will follow you.

##### Every change costs a dip

In one hotseat he gives his own anecdotal estimate: any change to a process (a sales script, onboarding, a leadership routine) causes a temporary drop of about 20% while the team adapts. So he only makes a change when the risk-adjusted upside is about 20% or more, for example 40% upside at 50/50 odds. He treats changes like a limited punch card: roughly one test a week on automated assets such as landing pages, and perhaps one real process change per department per quarter. Ideas go onto a private list with estimated upside and probability, and are pulled only when the team has bandwidth and the idea beats everything else in the queue.

##### One priority, and let small fires burn

He asks for the one thing that, if true twelve months from now, would change everything (an owned lead channel, a talent pipeline, faster cash conversion). If the CEO cannot name it, he says the CEO has failed. Once chosen, the bet gets patience: no second solution because the first has not paid off in 30 days. Small fires are allowed to burn, because very little kills a business within hours, and the first four to six hours of the day go to the highest-leverage work.

##### Simplify offers and features

The same subtraction runs through the product. He calls overwhelm the main churn driver after price: paying for five things and using one feels like 20% value, while paying the same for one thing feels like 100%. His moves are to remove deliverables, ask members what they would fight to keep and what they would not miss, and design a simple start-here path. In marketing he looks for the single most compelling point. In sales he usually cuts about half of a script. His shorthand is that simple scales and fancy fails.

#### Examples

*Cases are paraphrased and anonymised from Alex's videos. Any example marked as invented is for illustration only.*

##### Solo legal practice with two side products

<div class="ba" markdown="1">
<div class="before" markdown="1">
**Before.** A solo attorney serving creative professionals sells hourly services, a low-priced course and a software subscription off one video audience. The services make money, the software burns cash, and together the side lines lose about $100K a year.
</div>
<div class="after" markdown="1">
**After.** Kill the software or sell it outright. Make the education free and use it as nurture. Route every call to action to one channel and one sales call, and sell an outcome package at a flat fee instead of hours.
</div>
</div>

The services already had more demand than supply. Removing the vampire stopped the losses, and pointing all traffic at one monetisation path made the remaining business simpler to grow.

##### Founder with four ventures

A founder running writing retreats, a publishing house, a TV show and a business school keeps starting things because she gets bored. Alex tells her to follow where the market already pays (the books), stop pushing the school, use the show as media to sell the core offer, and put her appetite for novelty into marketing, where change is cheap.

##### Contractor with a better second business

An owner runs an established construction firm (about $11M at roughly 18% margin) and a young elevator-service company (about $3M at roughly 30% in year one, recurring). The safe route is to spend profit on leaders to run construction. Alex's own route is to sell the construction firm quickly, even at a discount, and go all in on the recurring business, because in his view one focused quarter of recurring growth there could equal the enterprise value of the old firm.

##### Analytics consultancy (invented)

| Before | After |
|---|---|
| Dashboard projects, an AI chatbot product, an ERP training course | Dashboard projects for mid-size manufacturers only |
| LinkedIn posts, events, partner referrals, cold email | Partner referrals, worked properly, with one weekly cadence |
| Three half-finished priorities | One: a repeatable two-week margin-report offer |

The chatbot and the course are paused and go on the idea list. They come back only when the core offer has passed $1M and the team has spare capacity.

#### Common mistakes

- **Hedging by splitting.** Running the old and new business side by side feels safe. He sees it as the default way to fail at both.
- **Calling an escape a strategy.** A new product line, avatar or company started at a plateau usually avoids the skill gap that caused the plateau.
- **Changing many things at once.** Each change costs a dip and attribution is lost. Five parallel changes stack five dips.
- **Adding to fix churn.** More features and more content widen the gap between what is paid for and what is used.
- **Keeping a losing unit out of loyalty.** A location or line that drains cash and attention from the winner gets closed unless something concrete requires it.

#### How Alex would apply it

- **He asks which unit actually makes money**, and where sales really happen, then points everything there and closes the other doors.
- **He makes the owner list subtractions before any addition** and checks whether the freed hours are enough for the new thing.
- **He names the red dress out loud** when a new venture, avatar or whale shows up at a plateau, and redirects to the constraint in the current business.
- **He treats change as expensive**: one real change at a time, a risk-adjusted 20% bar, real-time metrics to see the dip, and a queue for every other idea.
- **He picks one priority for the year** and protects it, letting small fires burn rather than starting parallel fixes after 30 days.
- **He simplifies the product and the message**: fewer deliverables, one core promise, the shortest script that works.
- **Questions he would ask:** Which line makes the money? If a friend had one business everyone wanted plus four slower side ventures, what would you tell them? If you only did this one thing, would you hit your goal? What can we delete and still have it be as good or better? Would you still want this new venture if the current business tripled?

#### Worksheet

Answer in writing, with numbers where you have them.

1. **Units:** List every product, avatar and channel. Next to each, write revenue, profit and the hours it takes from you each week.
2. **Vampires:** Which unit loses money or attention to the cash cow? What would happen if you closed it this month?
3. **1-1-1:** Write your one product, one avatar and one channel for the next twelve months.
4. **One thing:** What single result, if true in twelve months, would make most other problems irrelevant?
5. **Change budget:** Which one change will you make this quarter, and what upside and odds justify the dip?
6. **Simplify:** What can you remove from your offer that customers would not miss?

#### Sources

- [I Blew Up a Business Through 3 Small Changes (Just Copy Me)](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=3yAiVjcImQ4&t=47s) — the 1-1-1 rule and "kill the vampire" applied to a solo legal practice (00:47)
- [If I Wanted to Make My First $100K in 2026, I'd Do This](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jfW6gL6hKhk&t=475s) — focus by subtraction and 1-1-1 restated for beginners (07:55)
- [Why Doing More Is Actually Making You Less Money](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=hfwZwPGsbIo&t=623s) — the ~20% dip per change, the punch card and the private idea list (10:23)
- [Less is More: The Magic of a Simple Business](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=-JLN1d1ZKUU&t=290s) — one priority for twelve months and letting small fires burn (04:50)
- [Helping E-Commerce Business Owners Scale](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8C_6qojTA78&t=1534s) — the "girl in the red dress" diagnosis for a planned software side venture (25:34)
- ["Should He Walk Away From His $1.4M Business To Start Over?" | Alex Hormozi Answers Live](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0RjIw9M1j28&t=576s) — subtract before adding and the cost of split focus (09:36)
- [Helping 6 Business Owners Scale in 33 Minutes](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0EqJD2o-Mnk&t=1843s) — fire-selling the weaker business to reclaim attention (30:43)
- [$100M Investor Answers The Most Important Business Questions for 1 Hour](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=--9kOXNCUdE&t=2319s) — chasing multiple rabbits against focused competitors (38:39)
- [I Confronted a Stranger With a Failing Business...](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6Fg4VXjRphQ&t=589s) — shutting the money-losing unit and routing all traffic to one path (09:49)
- [How to Get Your Customers to Stay FOREVER](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=-j8_YCWZ05Q&t=571s) — fewer features as a churn fix (09:31)
- [Business Was Hard Until I Understood These 4 Concepts](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=F84olnKkseM&t=1365s) — commitment as eliminating alternatives (22:45)
- [13 Years Of Brutally Honest Business Advice in 90 Mins](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KhFlD54nQrY&t=2627s) — let fires burn and protect the first hours for leverage (43:47)

## Prioritising Moves

- id: prioritising-moves
- book: Alex's Playbook / How he decides
- original term: More, Better, New; ICE (impact, confidence, ease)
- key: More → Better → New, ranked by Impact × Confidence × Ease
- related: more-better-new, constraint, diagnostic-sequence, focus-and-subtraction, pricing-decisions

How Alex orders the work once he knows the constraint. Squeeze what already works, rank everything else by impact, confidence and ease, and give the owner only two or three moves.

#### The gist

Most hotseats end the same way. Alex writes a short numbered list, with quick, likely, high-impact moves at the top and heavy strategic bets at the bottom. Before anything new appears on that list, he asks why the owner cannot simply do more of what already works. New ideas have to prove they beat spending more on the existing winner.

<div class="formula" markdown="1">
**More** of what works → make it **better** → only then something **new**.
Rank candidates by **Impact × Confidence × Ease**, and hand over **two or three**.
</div>

The ordering is how he controls risk. A move that adds volume to a proven channel has known economics. A move that changes the offer, the avatar or the channel resets the learning curve. He picks up the obvious money first and lets the cash and confidence from those wins fund the harder bets later.

#### How it works

##### More, then better, then new

He states the default order in several talks: first ask why you cannot do more of what works; if you cannot do more, ask whether you can make it better; only then try something new. He uses it as a quarterly plan too ("more, better", repeated until something blocks it). In one Q&A he explains why small owners stall: they find something that works, stop doing it, and chase several new things.

Typical "more" moves from the cases: going from a few networking events a week to several a day, from one stage talk a year to one a week, doubling the ad spend on a funnel that already returns about 4x, or sending 20 partner emails a day instead of a handful. A channel that works but has never been scaled is, in his view, usually the biggest win in the business.

##### Rank by impact, confidence and ease

He names ICE (impact, confidence, ease) as his prioritisation tool and in one hotseat splits ease into speed and effort, so the ranking mirrors the value equation. The practical rules he attaches to it:

- **Light first, heavy later**, unless a later step depends on an earlier one.
- **Cheapest to heaviest.** In one case the sequence was better creative, then the right pixel event, then a high-ticket one-time offer, and only then a sales team selling a large back-end package.
- **Low operational risk when execution is weak.** If the team is unlikely to execute a complex change well, he prefers the move that still works when done badly.
- **Low operational drag.** Price is his favourite example: you change a number or a sentence, and nothing in delivery has to change.
- **Can these people execute it?** He checks the team before finalising the list.

##### His order of operations

When several areas need work, he follows a fairly stable sequence across videos:

| Step | What he fixes | Why it comes here |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Offer and price | Highest leverage, lowest drag; frees cash |
| 2 | Sales motion and conversion | Fixes leaks before you pay for more traffic |
| 3 | Demand on existing channels | Spend and volume on what is proven |
| 4 | New channels, avatars, products | Highest risk, funded by steps 1–3 |

He puts it as: do not push more demand through thin margins, and do not buy traffic into a leaky bucket. For a stalled local practice he ordered it as pricing and packaging, then attribution, then ads, then content. He adds that cheap conversion fixes can double leads on their own and push a business into a supply constraint. The supply-versus-demand triage itself comes before all of this (see the diagnostic sequence).

##### Never switch off what works

He repeats this rule often. Do not turn off a channel that makes money to test a new one; add the new one on top. Do not let the main channel drop while building a second. Do not put a new person on a new channel: hand the existing channel to someone, confirm it holds, then put a proven person on the new one. When testing a new lead source, route it to one proven closer so the only variables are the source, the offer and the process. If budget has to move, he moves it gradually (for example 30%, then 50%).

##### Do not change what customers love

If demand exceeds capacity, he raises price or adds capacity rather than changing the product, because pricing is easy to change and delivery is hard. He warns that products get worse through addition, that a working offer in a large company touches billing, sales, ads and fulfilment at once, and that a sales process with healthy close rates should be left alone. He also refuses to fix problems that are not showing up in the metrics: a narrative about a threat, with no rise in churn, is no reason to rebuild.

##### The biggest obvious pile of money first

In a keynote he says most businesses grow most by picking up the biggest obvious pile of money in front of them. Elsewhere he asks owners which single thing would double the business this year, and tells them to drop whatever is not that. In one case he refused to discuss anything except the main constraint until the owner stopped asking about secondary optimisations.

##### Two or three moves only

He deliberately limits advice to the two or three highest-impact moves so the owner actually executes them, and in one hotseat simplifies a plan to two steps.

#### Examples

*Cases are paraphrased and anonymised from Alex's videos. Any example marked as invented is for illustration only.*

##### Home-service company with nothing broken

A duct-cleaning business with about $1.25M revenue, 38% profit, a 99% show rate and an 82% close rate asked for more leads. Alex's ordered list:

| # | Move | Logic |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Raise price about 10%, hold while close rate stays above ~65% | About +25% net profit, no operational change |
| 2 | Keep paying down debt | Lower risk |
| 3 | Dedicated ad landing page, all buttons to it | Cheap conversion fix |
| 4 | More creative, organic winners as ads, then more spend | Ads already returned about 13:1 |
| 5–7 | Retargeting, reactivation emails, partner offer | More from the existing base and partners |
| 8 | Outbound to new segments | Newest and heaviest, so last |

##### Coach about to switch away from a working funnel

<div class="ba" markdown="1">
<div class="before" markdown="1">
**Before.** A weight-loss coach at about $288K needs $600K. National ads to DMs run at about 4x return. She is considering a local in-person model as well.
</div>
<div class="after" markdown="1">
**After.** Double the ad spend on the 4x funnel and add DM setters. Do not run both models.
</div>
</div>

Alex first suggested the local hybrid, then corrected himself on the call: when something works, put more into it.

##### BI consultancy (invented)

<div class="ba" markdown="1">
<div class="before" markdown="1">
**Before.** Most clients come from one ERP partner. The owner plans a new AI product, a webinar series and a price review, all this quarter.
</div>
<div class="after" markdown="1">
**After.** 1) Raise the project price on new quotes. 2) Turn the partner referrals into a fixed monthly routine with a report-back after each project. 3) Ask the partner for introductions to its other regional offices. The AI product waits on the idea list.
</div>
</div>

Steps 1 and 2 are fast, likely and nearly free. Step 3 is still "more" of the proven channel. The new product carries the most risk, so it comes last.

#### Common mistakes

- **Starting with "new".** A new channel or product feels like progress, while the working channel stays under-exploited.
- **Fixing the loudest problem instead of the biggest one.** He looks for the move that changes throughput most and ignores secondary optimisations until it is done.
- **Turning off a winner to test.** Replacing a working channel removes the cash and the benchmark you need to judge the test.
- **Handing over a long list.** Ten good ideas executed badly lose to two executed fully.
- **Improving what customers already love.** Changing the core product or a healthy sales process risks the part that works.

#### How Alex would apply it

- **He asks "why can't you just do more of this?"** before discussing anything new, and treats the answer as the next constraint.
- **He writes a numbered list in ICE order**: quick, likely, high-impact items first, structural bets last, dependencies respected.
- **He fixes offer and price first**, then conversion, then volume on proven channels, and only then new channels.
- **He keeps every working channel running** while testing, and isolates new variables (one closer, one source, one change).
- **He leaves alone what customers love** and what the metrics say is healthy.
- **He cuts the plan to two or three moves** the owner will actually carry out.
- **Questions he would ask:** Where do your best customers come from, and have you scaled that? If you could do only one thing this year and it had to double the business, what would it be? What happened when you increased spend? What breaks if we do more? Is this new idea better than simply spending more on what works?

#### Worksheet

List every move you are considering, then work through these.

1. **More:** Which channel or offer already works, and what would 2x or 10x of it look like?
2. **Better:** Which step of that working path is furthest below where it should be?
3. **Score:** Give each move 1–10 for impact, confidence and ease. Multiply and sort.
4. **Order:** Re-sort so offer and price come before conversion, conversion before volume, and volume before anything new.
5. **Protect:** Write what you will not touch (working channels, the core product, a healthy sales process).
6. **Cut:** Keep the top two or three moves. Put the rest on a dated idea list.

#### Sources

## Risk & Bets

- id: risk-and-bets
- book: Alex's Playbook / How he decides
- original term: Risk-adjusted bets; push vs pivot
- key: Bet value = payoff × probability. At equal value, take the higher probability.
- related: prioritising-moves, focus-and-subtraction, constraint, client-financed-acquisition, enterprise-value

Alex prices every move as payoff times probability, prefers the surer bet, keeps pushing while the core assumptions hold, and treats fear-driven under-spending, debt and premature hiring as the risks that actually hurt owners.

#### The gist

Alex talks about risk in two directions at once. He thinks owners overestimate the downside of the right moves (raising price, spending more on a channel that pays, hiring salespeople when leads exist) and underestimate the downside of the wrong ones (starting a second business, taking on debt, adding staff ahead of growth, changing course on a bad week).

<div class="formula" markdown="1">
**Bet value** = **payoff** × **probability**
At equal value, choose the **higher probability**, then string sure wins together.
**Push** while the first-principles assumptions hold. **Pivot** only when data disproves one.
</div>

He frames risk as personal and usually overestimated: for most founders the real downside is bankruptcy and starting over. The scarce resource is time, since a founder only gets a handful of multi-year attempts in a career.

#### How it works

##### Payoff × probability, and the career punch card

In a short lecture on risk he runs a thought experiment: about 35 working years at about five years per serious attempt gives roughly seven bets. Big-bet logic (10% odds at 100x) suits venture funds with unlimited capital and attempts. A founder who loses three such bets in a row is fifteen years in with nothing. So when two options have similar risk-adjusted value, he takes the more probable one and compounds a chain of sure wins. He adds a test: assume none of the good things you expect happen; if you still win, take it.

In another talk he stresses the other side: you are paid for the risk you are seen to take, and risk is often mispriced because people overestimate downside. The two fit together: seek bets that look riskier to others than they are for you.

For changes inside a running business he uses a concrete bar in one hotseat: make a change only when the risk-adjusted upside is about 20% or more (for example 40% upside at 50/50 odds), because every change costs a dip.

##### Idea risk versus execution risk

He separates two kinds of risk and says he only takes the second. Idea risk asks whether people want it. Execution risk asks whether you can run it well. He picks businesses with proven demand, so the only variable is execution. With proven demand, a failure tells you the problem was you. With idea risk you cannot tell, which makes push-or-pivot decisions nearly impossible.

##### Push or pivot

His rule, repeated in several videos: pivot only when a core assumption about demand or monetisation has been disproven by data. If demand is real and things are simply hard, keep solving one problem at a time. He calls this "good hard". Write the assumptions down before investing (the buyers are on this platform, they can be reached profitably), so you know what would count as disproof. Losses from testing an assumption that turned out false are the price of learning, which he calls an ignorance tax. In one lecture he puts the ratio bluntly: push nine times out of ten, and pivot when the market structurally does not allow repeat purchase.

##### Do not start a new venture to escape a plateau

His test for the urge: would you still want the new venture if the current business tripled? If not, it is an escape. A new business caps at the founder's current skill level, so changing the vehicle does not lift the ceiling. Years in one business pay down your ignorance debt; a new field puts you back at the bottom.

##### Owners self-capping spend out of fear

Often the owner's fear is the limiter: capped budgets on profitable funnels, too few creatives, no new salespeople despite lead flow. His counter-arguments:

- **If a dollar reliably returns several, the right budget is as much as the channel will take.**
- **Testing spend is investment.** Budget a known loss to find the few keywords, creatives or channels that scale. One rule of thumb he gives: let a new campaign spend up to about 2x the allowable acquisition cost before shutting it off. A new channel costing $10K a month for six months before its first sale can still be a good investment if it then returns 5–10x.
- **Reversible downside.** If spending more fails, you can go back to what you did before.
- **Check fear against cash flow.** If cash is fine, the hesitation is irrational.

He also sizes bets. In one turnaround he notes that even a high-expected-value bet is dangerous when you only have enough capital for two hands, and that most founders are under-capitalised relative to the odds.

##### Debt, expenses and hiring ahead of growth

His view of debt is conservative: it adds risk, and over a long horizon risk tends to come due, so he leans toward paying it off. He prefers growing debt-free through offer design (upfront cash, prepay, anchors). Expenses are downside risk, so he does not hire ahead of growth: get the growth and let it pay for the people. He recalls hiring 25 support reps in anticipation of growth, and later four people handled more volume. His exception is commission-based sales reps. He also takes profit out regularly, since being asset rich and cash poor degrades decisions.

##### Slow down when emotional

In a lecture on what nearly killed his business he names the signal: you flip-flop on the same data from day to day. When he senses emotion or urgency, he slows down, sleeps on big decisions for 24 hours, treats "must decide today" as usually fictitious, and only makes big multivariable calls from a rested state. Once decided, he sticks with the call through the predictable difficulties. This sits alongside his view that a company grows at the speed its leader makes decisions. One way to hold both: decide small things promptly and big emotional ones slowly.

#### Examples

*Cases are paraphrased and anonymised from Alex's videos. Any example marked as invented is for illustration only.*

##### Telehealth owner planning a consultancy

<div class="ba" markdown="1">
<div class="before" markdown="1">
**Before.** A telehealth and clinic business at about $550K a month has stalled because the product cannot be advertised on the major social platforms. The owner wants to start a consultancy teaching other physicians.
</div>
<div class="after" markdown="1">
**After.** Drop the new venture. Restate the problem as "how do I acquire customers from a channel I am allowed to use", and put all effort into that.
</div>
</div>

The consultancy would enter a saturated market with no track record. The existing engine already works, and its real constraint would follow the owner into any new business.

##### Education business capping its own ads

An online craft-education membership (about $800K a year, 30–35% profit) ran four ads and limited spend at about $37 per acquisition. Alex told the owner to make 80 or more ads and accept up to about $100 per acquisition, because back-end value recovered it within the first month.

##### Consumer brand with a defined last test

A founder of a premium drinks brand had about $1.5M in sales across many stores, zero profit and no repeat-purchase data. Alex proposed one focused bet on travel-retail contracts, where the small can was an advantage. If she could not close one, she should shut it down and put her time into the property development she had already proven on the side. The rule: learn fast rather than spend three more years on an unproven assumption.

##### Data consultancy weighing a SaaS product (invented)

| Question | Answer |
|---|---|
| Demand proven? | Clients pay for the consulting; nobody has paid for the planned SaaS |
| Type of risk | Idea risk on the SaaS, execution risk on the consulting |
| Escape test | The owner would drop the SaaS idea if consulting tripled |
| Decision | Push consulting; write down what data would justify the SaaS later |

#### Common mistakes

- **Treating test spend as lost money.** The search for a scalable channel always includes losers.
- **Pivoting on a bad month.** Without written assumptions, frustration gets mistaken for disproof.
- **Betting the career on long odds.** A few multi-year attempts cannot absorb a string of 10% bets.
- **Hiring for the growth you expect.** Fixed costs taken on early become the risk that sinks you if growth is late.
- **Deciding while upset.** Flip-flopping on the same data is the sign to wait.

#### How Alex would apply it

- **He asks for the payoff and the odds** and prefers the surer path when the products are close.
- **He chooses execution risk over idea risk** and wants proven demand before any new venture.
- **He pushes while the written assumptions hold** and pivots only on data, not on fatigue.
- **He runs the escape test** before discussing any new business: would you still want it if the current one tripled?
- **He removes fear-based caps** on spend and sales hiring when unit economics and cash flow support it, and frames testing as an investment with a budget.
- **He keeps fixed costs and debt low**, pays down debt, takes profit out, and does not hire ahead of growth (commission reps excepted).
- **Questions he would ask:** Has a fundamental assumption about demand been disproven? How much are you spending, can you spend more, and why don't you? If your return were 10:1 instead of 3:1, would you spend more? What is your monthly break-even and debt service? Would you still want this if the current business tripled?

#### Worksheet

Pick one decision you are facing and work through it.

1. **Value:** Estimate payoff and probability for each option. Which has the higher product, and which is surer?
2. **Risk type:** Is demand proven, or are you betting on whether people want it?
3. **Assumptions:** Write the two or three assumptions that must hold. What data would disprove each?
4. **Escape test:** Would you still choose this if your current business tripled?
5. **Spend:** Where are you capping spend or hiring out of fear? What do unit economics and cash flow say?
6. **State:** Are you deciding rested and calm? If not, set a date 24 hours out.

## Pricing Decisions

- id: pricing-decisions
- book: Alex's Playbook / Money levers
- original term: Pricing (raise, test, never discount the core)
- key: Raise price until conversion × price (or total gross profit) starts to fall, not until unit sales fall.
- related: charge-what-its-worth, value-equation, cash-forward, retention-and-ltv, business-math

Price is the lever Alex reaches for first: it costs nothing to change, falls straight to profit, and most owners are underpriced. He tests it by conversion × price and almost never lowers it.

#### The gist

Across his hotseats, Alex treats price as the first and cheapest lever. Changing it means changing a number or a sentence, with almost no operational drag, yet owners fear it more than anything else. His recurring diagnosis is that small owners price from their own wallet: they cannot imagine anyone paying more than they would, and they discount whatever comes easily to them.

The math is why he cares. On thin margins, a small price rise multiplies profit, while more customers only add to it. In one keynote he walks through a business with a 20% margin: doubling the number of customers doubles profit, but doubling the price makes profit six times larger, because the extra revenue carries no extra cost.

<div class="formula" markdown="1">
**Test price by** close rate × price (revenue per sales conversation), or total gross profit. **Stop raising** when that number falls, not when the first buyer says no.
</div>

#### How it works

##### Signals that you are underpriced

These come up again and again in the notes:

- **Very high close rates.** In his business-math lecture he gives a rule-of-thumb table: closing 80% or more means you are probably 3–4x underpriced, 60–80% means 2–3x, 50–60% means 1.5–2x, 40–50% means 1.25–1.5x. Around 30–40% is about right if prospects are educated before the call. Below 30% he looks at the avatar and the sales process before ever touching price.
- **Full capacity.** If demand exceeds supply, his first move is to raise price, before changing the delivery ratio or hiring. Fewer clients at a higher price means more money and freed time to build capacity.
- **Thin margin at full volume.** If a business barely profits even on repeat and word-of-mouth customers (free acquisition), either fixed costs are too high or prices are wrong. For services he wants at least 80% gross margin, with delivery at about one fifth of price.
- **You can't afford acquisition.** In his view, many "we need cheaper leads" problems are really problems of not making enough per customer.
- **Long time since the last increase.** A raise after three or four years is just catching up with inflation.

##### Testing price

He wants price answered by tests, not opinions. Edge the price up until people stop saying yes, then back off slightly. Test live on calls, moving from low to high, because going down means crediting recent buyers. Test the less price-sensitive segment first. Expect to make more money before you make less: in one example a 50% raise with 10% fewer sales is clearly worth keeping. The point of maximum profit sits beyond the point of maximum conversion.

##### Never discount the core offer

This is one of his most consistent rules across the sales trainings. When asked for a discount:

1. Say you could do it for more. This resets the anchor upward.
2. Change the **terms** (payment plan, prepay) or the **components** (remove a feature, downsell to a lighter version).
3. Add a **bonus** instead of a discount. He suggests giving sales teams a list of valued bonuses to use in place of price cuts.
4. If you do lower price, **trade it for something**: a longer commitment, a testimonial, reviews, a referral. That keeps you in control and makes the difference defensible to other customers.

Unplanned price cuts sound like begging and lower trust. Price tests are planned in advance, not improvised on the call.

##### Anchors and premium tiers

Always present the premium tier first and step down only if the buyer says they are on a budget. Keep a high anchor even if few take it: a visible top tier lifts the perceived value of everything below it. His arithmetic: if 10% take an anchor priced at 10x, revenue roughly doubles. One-on-one should cost about 5x one-to-many; if everyone pays the premium, raise it. If the anchor isn't meant to sell, make it nearly identical to the core with one or two extra features.

##### Surge pricing and fees

Supply and demand apply to small businesses too. Charge more at peak times, or frame it as an off-peak discount. Sell speed: a same-day or priority fee is pure margin on the same work (he suggests 10% rising to 20% in one hotseat). Pass on card-processing fees, which in a restaurant case could add about 15% to profit. Name fees after what customers accept (setup, equipment) and price them to what you need.

##### Competitors and the worst customers

Never cut price to fight a competitor who undercuts you or copies the cheap part of your model. Be in the value-surpassing game: show buyers variables they hadn't considered (risk, ease, speed) so the comparison is no longer apples to apples. Accept that a raise loses some customers. When he tripled prices at his gym and lost about a third of members, revenue roughly doubled and staff nicknamed it "the culling", because it cleared out the chronic complainers. Price complaints from non-ideal customers mean the price is filtering correctly.

#### Examples

*Cases are paraphrased and anonymised from Alex's videos. Any example marked as invented is for illustration only.*

##### Local moving company, ~$300K a year

<div class="ba" markdown="1">
<div class="before" markdown="1">
**Before.** Hourly pricing near the top of the market, losing about 40% of quotes on price. The owner thinks he needs more leads.
</div>
<div class="after" markdown="1">
**After.** A VIP tier presented first (master movers, packing, materials, insurance, a price cap about 50% above the estimate), standard as the fallback, and a same-day surcharge starting at 10%.
</div>
</div>

Alex diagnosed a commodity offer, not a lead problem. Selling by the hour invites comparison, so he broke the offer into elements buyers hadn't thought about.

##### Custom awards manufacturer, ~$1.7M revenue, 6% net

Prices were set cost-plus. With net margin near 6%, a roughly 6% price rise about doubles profit. His advice: move to value-based pricing, split-test higher prices on top sellers, start with the impulse-gift segment rather than price-sensitive B2B buyers, and judge by gross profit × units.

##### Solo attorney billing $250 an hour

Average client value was about $1,500 and the close rate was very high. He moved her to a $5,800 flat fee plus a small performance share, with a $25K no-commission anchor and a $2–3K do-it-yourself downsell close enough in price to make the core look best.

##### Invented: Qlik consultancy dashboard project

| | Today | Test |
|---|---|---|
| Price per project | €8,000 | €14,000 |
| Sales calls per month | 20 | 20 |
| Close rate | 85% | 55% |
| Projects sold | 17 | 11 |
| Revenue per call | €6,800 | €7,700 |
| Gross profit (delivery €4,000) | €68,000 | €110,000 |

An 85% close rate is his clearest underpricing signal. At the higher price fewer projects are sold, yet gross profit rises by 60% and six projects' worth of capacity is freed.

#### Common mistakes

- **Pricing from your own wallet.** You underprice because you can't imagine paying it yourself. Price from the value of the problem solved and the buyer's wealth.
- **Cost-plus or market-average pricing.** Your costs are irrelevant to what the buyer will pay, and the market average is mostly small businesses that are struggling.
- **Judging a raise by unit sales.** Fewer units at a higher price often means more profit. Look at conversion × price.
- **Cutting price to close or to fight a rival.** It lowers trust and trains discount seekers. Change terms, components or bonuses instead.
- **Cutting price to fix churn.** In one case a cut from about 3,000 to 2,500 a month changed nothing, and in his own business a cut didn't move churn either.

#### How Alex would apply it

- **He checks price before demand.** Thin margin, full calendar, very high close rate, or unaffordable acquisition all point him to price first, ahead of new channels or hires.
- **He runs the numbers in the room.** Margin, close rate and units turn the decision into arithmetic: a 20% cut at a 40% margin needs twice the sales to break even.
- **He raises, then backs it with value.** If the owner can't justify a higher price, he improves the offer (faster, easier, more certain) and adds a guarantee with teeth, then raises 20–40%.
- **He refuses to discount the core.** Terms, components and bonuses can move; the core price does not. Any discount is traded for something.
- **He builds a ladder of tiers.** Premium first, a 10x anchor above, a downsell below, priced so the core looks best.
- **He accepts losing the worst customers.** Watch what customers do, not what they say: if they keep buying while complaining, ignore the complaints.
- **Questions he would ask:** How do you set prices today, and who set them? What is your close rate at this price? When did you last raise prices? What is your gross margin? Are you full? Which segment is least price-sensitive? What revenue per call do you get at price A versus price B?

#### Worksheet

Answer for your main offer, with real numbers from the last three months.

1. **Close rate:** What share of qualified sales conversations close? Where does that put you in his table?
2. **Margin:** What is your gross margin per client? Is delivery at or below one fifth of price?
3. **Test plan:** Which next price will you quote on the next ten calls, and what revenue per call would make it a win?
4. **Discount script:** What will you say instead of a discount? List three bonuses and one term change you can offer.
5. **Anchor:** What would a version at 5–10x the price include? Which of those items cost you little?
6. **Fees and speed:** Where could you charge for priority, peak times or setup?

- [Make More Profit than 99% of People](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=41EvCgwPrDc&t=1355s) — revenue per call, testing from low to high (22:35)
- [NEVER lower your prices...](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=BZQtuK-ucDM&t=432s) — break-even math on price cuts, cuts don't fix churn (07:12)

- [KEYNOTE: Small To BIG. The Big 4 Customer Acquisition Models.](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=XwZH-lOKG9c&t=3822s) — the pricing genie: double price vs double customers (63:42)

- [Building a $3,500,000 Business for a Stranger in 51 Mins](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=BMF2fWHyDrg&t=1531s) — cost-plus to value pricing on a 6% margin (25:31)
- [I Blew Up a Business Through 3 Small Changes (Just Copy Me)](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=3yAiVjcImQ4&t=477s) — hourly to outcome pricing with anchor and downsell (07:57)

## Pull Cash Forward

- id: cash-forward
- book: Alex's Playbook / Money levers
- original term: Pull cash forward (30-day payback)
- key: Cash collected in the first 30 days > cost of delivery + cost of acquiring the customer (CAC).
- related: client-financed-acquisition, business-math, pricing-decisions, retention-and-ltv, money-models

Alex treats up-front cash as the master variable. If the first 30 days of a customer pay for acquiring and serving them, growth stops being limited by money, so he fixes cash flow through offer design before anyone talks about debt or investors.

#### The gist

In the notes, cash flow problems are almost never solved with a loan or a funding round. Alex solves them by changing how and when the customer pays: prepay discounts, setup fees, quarterly or annual billing, layaway, card on file, a planned ladder of payment options. The pattern recurs across hotseats, lectures and sales trainings, and up-front cash works as the master variable behind much of his advice.

The reasoning is simple. If a new customer returns more cash in the first month than they cost to acquire and serve, every customer funds the next one. He ties the 30-day window to the interest-free period on a credit card: it is about as long as a small business can carry the cost of acquisition without its own money.

<div class="formula" markdown="1">
**30-day rule:** cash collected in the first 30 days > fully loaded delivery cost + CAC.
**Stronger version (one keynote):** 30-day gross profit from one customer > cost to acquire and serve two customers.
</div>

#### How it works

##### The 30-day rules

- **Target CAC payback within 30 days.** With that in place he says there is no fixed marketing budget: you spend as long as it stays profitable. A fixed budget, in his framing, means you lose money up front.
- **Know your payback curve.** In one talk he asks for cash returned per dollar of ad spend at day 15, 30 and 60. Without it, marketing decisions are guesswork.
- **Testing budget.** Let a new campaign spend up to about twice the allowable CAC (based on 30-day cash) before switching it off.
- **When payback is too long,** he looks at setup fees, onboarding fees, bundled physical products, longer prepaid durations, buy-one-get-two offers, or last month paid up front.

##### Prepay and pay-in-full discounts

He wants at least a 10% discount for paying in full, and calls 10–20% worth it because a small business earns more on its capital than the markets do. A ladder he uses in one sales training: about 20% off for full prepay, about 10% for half down, list price on the payment plan. Two framing rules:

- **Show the highest price first** (the payment plan), then offer discounts for paying sooner. You become the one giving a benefit, not the one adding interest.
- **Say "prepayment discount",** not "paid in full". The second is seller language with nothing in it for the buyer.

He often prefers perks to discounts: faster delivery (skip the line), less risk (a longer guarantee), more ease (concierge). He quotes rough uptake in one lecture: buy-10-get-2 gets 15–20% to prepay, a moderate discount plus perks 30–40%.

##### Up-front fees

Use something up front as the vehicle for cash: a setup fee, onboarding fee or equipment. Build the first payment from hard costs up (delivery, hardware, commission) so it covers what you have to pay before the customer has been with you long. A waived setup fee works as an A/B close: waive it for an annual commitment, charge it for quarter-to-quarter flexibility.

##### Quarterly and annual prepay

Ask **new** customers, not existing ones, to prepay a quarter to offset CAC; changing billing for newcomers doesn't push current clients away. Annual can be the default or even the only option, with a status perk or a physical bonus attached only to annual. A typical annual discount is about 16% (pay ten months, get twelve). In one lecture he argues that even at half the conversion, annual prepay can bring in several times more cash up front, which funds aggressive acquisition. Quarterly is the middle ground when annual is too much for the buyer.

##### Layaway and payment ladders

Layaway means the buyer pays first and delivery starts when they are paid up. Buyers then speed up their own payments, collection is easier, and you carry no delivery risk. His standard step-down order on a sales call:

1. Full prepay with a discount
2. Third-party financing
3. Partial prepay with a smaller discount
4. Payment plan (with automatic renewal at the end, which he finds stickier than month-to-month)
5. A smaller commitment, or just booking the next visit

Financing can lift sales (he cites about 35% in one lecture) but won't save a bad product.

##### Card on file

A heavily discounted assessment or a small paid ticket (around $99 in one hotseat) mainly serves to get a card on file. He says a small up-front fee typically gives 70–80% show rates and makes the later sale and upsell smoother: the next purchase is charged to the card already stored.

##### When cash is short right now

His order for cash in 48 hours, from one live Q&A: current customers first (prepay, preferably for extra service rather than a discount), then past customers (an "I owe you" credit toward a bigger package), then the lead list (an attraction offer). In a cash crisis he picks one bet with the biggest cash potential in the window and sells the strongest prepaid offer to the existing list, then recycles the cash into ads. Short-term cash plays are acceptable if they fund fixing the real constraint.

#### Examples

*Cases are paraphrased and anonymised from Alex's videos. Any example marked as invented is for illustration only.*

##### Residential waste-collection startup, losing money

<div class="ba" markdown="1">
<div class="before" markdown="1">
**Before.** Monthly service fee only. Every signup costs labour, a bin and a sales commission before the customer has paid much.
</div>
<div class="after" markdown="1">
**After.** First payment built from hard costs: roughly $42 labour + $116 bin + $50 commission ≈ $208 at signup. An A/B offer: prepay a year and get the bin free, or the core plan with a bin fee. Equipment financing as a fallback, with a yearly price increase built in.
</div>
</div>

Cash at signup covers the cost of each customer, so commission-only door-to-door reps can scale without debt. He notes that even if only 20% take the anchor, average cash per sale goes up.

##### Indoor family park, weeks from running out of cash

Revenue around $4.8M, unprofitable, heavily indebted. Alex chose one bet only: a prepaid summer camp offer sold to the existing list through a giveaway with a claim deadline and phone follow-up, with the cash recycled into ads.

##### Couples coaching, slow payback

Two 12-month offers and ad-driven challenges with slow payback. He asked for about $9K (the first quarter) up front, offered layaway as the downsell, and tied fast-action bonuses to the up-front payment. His expectation was that the first payment, more than the total price, drives conversion.

##### Invented: analytics retainer for a manufacturer

| | Monthly billing | Setup fee + quarter up front |
|---|---|---|
| Cash in first 30 days | €3,000 | €4,500 + €9,000 = €13,500 |
| Delivery cost in first 30 days | €2,400 | €2,400 |
| CAC | €6,000 | €6,000 |
| 30-day position | −€5,400 | +€5,100 |

With monthly billing the agency waits almost four months to recover the cost of each client. With an onboarding fee and the first quarter prepaid, the client funds the next acquisition in month one.

#### Common mistakes

- **Reaching for debt or investors first.** He treats a cash gap as an offer-design problem. If a business with strong returns can't raise money, he blames the deal terms, not the pitch.
- **Discounting without asking for cash.** A discount that isn't tied to prepayment or commitment gives away margin and gets nothing back.
- **Calling it "paid in full".** Seller language. Frame it as a prepayment discount the buyer receives.
- **Changing billing for existing clients.** Apply new payment terms to new customers.
- **Paying sales on revenue booked.** He says pay rarely changes rep behaviour, with one exception: paying on cash collected raises cash collected.

#### How Alex would apply it

- **He asks for the 30-day number first.** What is a customer worth in gross profit within 30 days, and what does it cost to acquire and serve them?
- **He redesigns the offer before the financing.** Setup fees, prepay, annual default, layaway. Debt and capital come after the offer cannot be improved.
- **He anchors high and steps down.** Payment-plan price first, prepay discount second, then financing, partial prepay, plan, smaller commitment. Everyone buys something.
- **He uses perks before percentages.** Faster, safer, easier, or a bonus worth more to the buyer than it costs you.
- **He goes to existing customers when cash is urgent.** Prepay for extra service, credits toward bigger packages, one strong offer to the list.
- **He removes the money ceiling, then spends.** Once acquisition funds itself, the limit should be operational, not financial.
- **Questions he would ask:** How much cash do you collect in the first 30 days? What is your CAC, and how long until it's paid back? Do you offer prepay or financing, and what share takes it? Do you have a card on file? How much cash do you have, and can you deliver what you've already sold with it?

#### Worksheet

Fill in with your last ten new clients.

1. **30-day cash:** What did each client pay in their first 30 days, on average?
2. **30-day cost:** What did delivery cost in those 30 days, and what was CAC per client?
3. **Gap:** Is cash collected above or below cost? By how much?
4. **Vehicles:** Which up-front item fits your offer: a setup fee, onboarding, first quarter prepaid, annual default?
5. **Ladder:** Write your step-down sequence from full prepay to the smallest commitment, with exact prices.
6. **Urgent cash:** If you needed cash in 48 hours, what would you offer current and past clients?

## Retention & Lifetime Value

- id: retention-and-ltv
- book: Alex's Playbook / Money levers
- original term: Retention, LTV and LTGP
- key: LTV = price ÷ monthly churn. LTGP = LTV × gross margin. Revenue plateaus at new sales per month × LTV.
- related: continuity-offers, upsell-offers, pricing-decisions, cash-forward, enterprise-value

If Alex could give a business one advantage, it would be revenue retention. He fixes churn before scaling acquisition, and he reads churn as an onboarding, usage or expectation problem far more often than a sign the product is bad.

#### The gist

In several lectures Alex calls retention the most valuable property a business can have. Retained customers stack year on year and growth compounds. Without retention you are permanently in the business of replacing customers, and a business that depends only on new sales dies when its channel breaks. Since the cost of reaching a given customer is roughly the same for every competitor, the lasting edge is the lifetime value you build on top.

His order is consistent: plug the leaks, then pour in more. Scaling acquisition into a leaking bucket just scales the problems. And when he digs into churn, the cause is usually upstream of the product: weak onboarding, customers who don't use what they bought, or a sales promise that didn't match reality.

<div class="formula" markdown="1">
**Churn** = cancels this month ÷ active clients · **LTV** = price ÷ churn · **LTGP** (lifetime gross profit) = LTV × gross margin
**Ceiling:** new sales per month × LTV is roughly where revenue stops growing.
</div>

#### How it works

##### Why it is worth more than acquisition

- **The ceiling moves.** With 100 new sales a month and an LTV of about $7.7K, revenue tops out around $770K a month in one of his worked examples. Halve churn and the ceiling doubles without one extra sale.
- **You can pay more per customer.** At a fixed LTV:CAC target, doubling LTV doubles what you can afford to acquire a customer. In one portfolio company, LTV rising from about $2,000 to $3,800 took the business from about $2M a month to about $2M a week.
- **It is often easier.** He frequently finds dropping churn far easier than multiplying acquisition. He would rather keep the same 300 customers than sell 900 a year to stay at 300.

##### Fix the leak first, but read it correctly

If clients drop after a couple of quarters, fix that before spending more on the front end. Two cautions he adds: young programmes (under 90 days or six months) show inflated churn, so don't overhaul everything because month one looks bad; and if retention is already strong, put the effort into acquisition rather than reinventing the product.

##### Where churn happens

Across a large membership platform he describes a stable shape: over 20% churn in month one, a second drop around month three (about 10%), and about 2% a month after month six. So he reframes churn work as getting people past day 30, past day 90 and past month six:

| Window | What has to happen |
|---|---|
| Month one | Activation, onboarding, expectations set, the right promises made |
| Day 90 | A real outcome or a visible change in behaviour |
| Month six | Customers connected to each other |

##### Activation and onboarding

The activation point is what your longest-staying customers did early. If you don't know it, study them and point the whole early experience at that behaviour. His examples: a first $2,000 sale within seven days for a gym licensee, 5+ lbs lost in week one, a trainer's first five clients. If there is no onboarding at all, he adds it first: one-on-one where possible, restate the customer's goal and why they want it, deliver a quick win within 24 hours, introduce one named contact. In the lecture built on a SaaS case, he says onboarding can add two to three months to LTV in recurring businesses.

##### Churn as consumption and expectations

- **Usage predicts cancellation.** Low renewal is usually preceded by low consumption. Track usage and act on the first drop.
- **Overwhelm drives churn.** Paying for five things and using one feels like 20% value. Removing unused inclusions raises perceived value.
- **Promises set the gap.** High promises raise close rates and lower retention. Set modest expectations and over-deliver; fix the gap by rescripting sales rather than adding delivery.
- **Stated exit reasons mislead.** "We already got the value" usually hides poor onboarding and communication. He prescribes frequent updates that tell clients what you did for them, without asking for anything.

##### Billing cadence

At each charge, customers judge value over the period since the last charge. Longer billing periods smooth over bad months. He cites a study with about 10.7% monthly churn on monthly billing, about 5% on quarterly and about 2% on annual. So he makes annual the default (about 16% off, with status perks), charges the one-time value up front and keeps the recurring fee small ("big head, long tail"), and prices the recurring fee at what customers would still pay in their worst month.

##### Talk to the people who left and the people who stayed

Message every canceller and tag the reason (price, overwhelm, not using, missing feature, not as expected). Every ~20 cancels, fix the top one or two; don't react to a single answer. Ask the longest-tenured customers why they stay: that answer is your retention engine. Tell customers at signup that cancelling includes an exit conversation, then offer to credit what they've paid toward staying, toward a tier that fixes their real problem, or a lighter version without the feature they don't use.

##### Referral ask and upsell at the moment of need

Ask for referrals at the activation point. The customer's gratitude fades quickly while you remember the favour as bigger than it was, so ask early. After activation he wants a testimonial, a referral, then ascension. Selling customers the next thing is itself a retention tactic: if you don't meet their urge to buy again, someone else will. The two upsells that work almost anywhere are more of what they bought or more help with it, offered right after purchase. Renewals get pitched at several points (week four, milestones, halfway, last chance).

##### Benchmarks he states

Treat these as his rules of thumb, each from its own context: B2B annual client retention above 80% (accounting firms, private equity buyers); 80% annual retention ≈ five years of revenue per customer, 50% ≈ two; best-in-class prosumer 12-month retention 60%+; if fewer than 7 in 10 members stay each month, retention is your highest-return lever; service churn rarely goes below 1–2% a month; SMB services at $1,500–3,000 a month stick about four to six months.

#### Examples

*Cases are paraphrased and anonymised from Alex's videos. Any example marked as invented is for illustration only.*

##### Portfolio SaaS company, churn rising after a launch

<div class="ba" markdown="1">
<div class="before" markdown="1">
**Before.** Group onboarding with about 25 customers per call. Complaints such as "too techy", "not enough support" and "too slow" pointed to a gap between what sales promised and what customers got.
</div>
<div class="after" markdown="1">
**After.** One-on-one onboarding that restates the goal and fixes issues live, rescripted sales expectations, a separate escalations team, commission for support staff on saves.
</div>
</div>

He reports 61% fewer cancellations, ascensions up from 20% to 48% with one-on-one onboarding, and about $2M saved in six months. Lowering the promises did not hurt close rates.

##### Online communities and gyms

On his community platform, one group that deleted content cut monthly churn from about 30% to 5%. His own community cut three weekly calls to one after overwhelm topped the cancel reasons. In his gyms, seven stacked tactics (onboarding, unlockables, exit interviews, attendance tracking and others) took churn from 11% to 6% a month.

##### B2B consultancy with retainer churn

A ~$1.1M bar consultancy lost retainer clients after a couple of quarters. His diagnosis: value wasn't being shown. Fix churn first by showing clients their numbers every month, before putting more into the front end.

##### Invented: Qlik managed analytics service

30 manufacturing clients at €1,500 a month started the year; 18 were still paying twelve months later. That is 60% annual retention, about 2.5 years and €45,000 revenue per client. Interviews show the stayers ran a management meeting from the dashboard within the first month. Onboarding is rebuilt around that meeting, with the referral ask right after it. At 80% retention, lifetime revenue per client would be about €90,000.

#### Common mistakes

- **Scaling a leaking bucket.** More ad spend into high churn just buys more people to lose.
- **Measuring LTV as revenue.** He wants lifetime gross profit. Revenue LTV can make an unprofitable channel look healthy.
- **Cutting price to stop churn.** His own cut didn't change churn, and in one case a recurring price drop of about a sixth changed nothing.
- **Adding features to keep people.** Unused inclusions widen the gap between paid and consumed and raise cancellations.

#### How Alex would apply it

- **He calculates churn and LTV first.** Most owners don't know either. He works out the plateau before talking about growth.
- **He fixes retention before acquisition,** unless retention is already strong, in which case he sends the effort to acquisition.
- **He starts with onboarding.** If there is none, it is the first fix; then unlockables, exit interviews and usage tracking, one tactic at a time.
- **He defines activation from the best customers** and points the first 30 days at it.
- **He makes annual the default** and separates one-time value from the recurring fee.
- **He places the referral ask and the upsell at the moment of need,** right after activation and right after purchase.
- **Questions he would ask:** How many customers cancel each month, out of how many active? What is your price and gross margin? How many of last year's customers are still paying, and how much of last year's revenue? What does onboarding look like? What is your activation point? Why did the last 20 leave, and why do your longest customers stay? Where exactly does the referral ask happen?

#### Worksheet

Use your client list from the past 12 months.

1. **Numbers:** Monthly churn, LTV and LTGP. What is your revenue ceiling at today's new-sales rate?
2. **Cohort:** Of clients active a year ago, what share still pays? Compare with his 80% B2B rule of thumb.
3. **Activation:** What did your five longest-staying clients do in their first 30 days?
4. **Leavers:** List the reasons of your last 20 cancellations. Which one or two will you fix first?
5. **Billing:** Could annual or quarterly billing become the default for new clients?
6. **Ask:** At which moment will you ask for a referral and offer the next purchase?

- [How to Get Your Customers to Stay FOREVER](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=-j8_YCWZ05Q&t=335s) — tagging cancel reasons every ~20 cancels; platform benchmarks (05:35)
- [7 Ways to Get People to Buy More Times](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=sPkMHh8zTMI&t=1053s) — defining activation from the longest-staying customers (17:33)

## The Sales Machine

- id: sales-machine
- book: Alex's Playbook / Sales and people
- original term: Sales team system (speed to lead, feed the killers, drill and review)
- key: Availability × speed × show rate × close rate × cash collected. Fix around the call before you fix the call.
- related: closer, constraint, pricing-decisions, hiring-and-talent, downsell-offers

How Alex builds selling into a system: what surrounds the call, who gets which lead, a question-led call, a planned answer to every objection, and a team trained by daily drills and recorded calls.

#### The gist

Alex treats sales as a machine with many small levers, each raising the odds that someone buys. His most repeated claim across the sales trainings is that what happens *around* the call (availability, speed to lead, booking, reminders, lead routing) usually moves revenue more than what is said *on* it. He fixes those first because they are mostly one-time setup work.

<div class="formula" markdown="1">
**Sales** = opportunities (availability, speed, show rate) × conversion (diagnosis, short pitch, objections) × consistency (training, review, standards)
</div>

The call itself follows the [CLOSER framework](../sales/closer.html), which this entry does not repeat. Here the focus is the system around it: lead routing, rules for questions and objections, the handoff into delivery, and how he trains, ranks, pays and recruits reps.

#### How it works

##### Around the call: availability, speed, show rate

A rep's job has three parts: maximise opportunities, convert them, sustain it. Opportunities come first.

- **Speed to lead.** Contact every new lead within about 60 seconds, and call again if they don't pick up. In several hotseats he prescribes one person whose only job is calling new leads.
- **Availability.** Sell seven days a week, offer start times every 15 minutes, and offer only same-day or next-day slots. If that leaves you short of slots, hire another rep rather than open later dates. In his scheduling-software data, open slots predicted throughput more strongly than any close-rate change.
- **Pull appointments forward.** When someone books for later, call straight away and offer a slot today. One rep who did this outsold the next best about 4x.
- **Reminders.** Manual messages 24 hours before, the morning of, and an hour before, with something showing prep you did for them. He says this personal prep can lift show rates by about 30%.
- **Calendar load.** Keep reps around 70–75% booked. Overbooked pushes appointments out, underbooked breeds desperation.
- **Pre-call education.** A short video that answers the common questions. Deals that need three or four calls signal too little education beforehand.

Every rep gets two SOPs: what to say on calls and what to do between them. Every meeting ends with the next one booked.

##### Lead scoring and routing

Score leads on the one or two factors that predict a sale, then "feed the killers": the best leads go to the best closers, the worst to new or weak reps. His recurring story is a timeshare company where this lifted the top rep's income more than tenfold and, rolled out nationally, multiplied the business. New reps practise on poor leads, where losses are smallest. Complaints about lead quality go to the manager privately, never to the group.

##### The call: questions, diagnosis, short pitch

- **Question-led scripts.** About 12 questions with one or two follow-ups each. No paragraphs, because reps read statements aloud. A closer says only three things: questions, restatements, and short stories that break one belief.
- **Diagnosis before prescription.** Current state, desired state, the obstacle, and every past attempt, in the prospect's words. He does not pitch until he expects a yes. The prospect should talk about two-thirds of the time.
- **Three pillars as analogies.** Chunk the solution into three components and show that each past attempt lacked at least one. Each pillar gets a metaphor or a 30-second story, and the whole pitch runs about two to three minutes. Reps memorise only these stories and the objection handles.

##### Objection theory

Objections fall into three groups: circumstances (time, money, fit), other people (spouse, partner) and self (self-doubt). The deeper the group, the closer you are to the real reason.

| They say | It usually means | His direction |
|---|---|---|
| No time | Not a priority yet | Ask what changed since they said they could commit; put a number on waiting |
| No money | They don't see the value | Resourcefulness, not resources; check rep conviction |
| Spouse or partner | Wants support, not permission | Use past agreements: the partner already dislikes the problem |
| Detail questions | Preference: "your result, my way" | Ask what answer they need before giving one |
| "Let me think" | Avoiding the decision | Ask the main concern, then what they fear happening |

Raise likely obstacles before the price, when the stakes are low. After the price they become objections. On each one he acknowledges, associates (often through a past client as a third party) and asks the next question. His most-used close plays out best case and worst case.

##### Step-downs instead of discounts

He starts from the belief that everyone buys something. He never cuts price to close. Instead he moves down a planned ladder: prepayment discount, third-party financing, partial prepay, payment plan, then a smaller commitment. A lower price is traded for something (longer term, testimonial, referral), or a bonus is added. If about six step-downs fail, he concludes the problem was never money and the prospect checked out earlier.

##### The ethics guardrail

He frames selling as transferring conviction across a bridge of trust. If they would not buy knowing everything you know, a yes is deception. He turns away people the offer won't help. With a qualified prospect and honest facts, he considers asking again an obligation.

##### Handshake to customer success

Within the hour after the close, the next person sends a personal video restating the buyer's goal. It cuts cold feet. Sales and customer success review recorded calls together weekly so promises match delivery.

##### Training: drills, recordings, one fix at a time

- Daily role-play of one short script section, corrected immediately, repeated right several times.
- Script adherence first: if nobody follows it, you can't tell whether the script is the problem.
- Record every call. The manager listens at double speed, picks the one or two fixes with the biggest effect on close rate, gives feedback briefly, then role-plays the fix. One fix at a time.
- Conviction drives tone: testimonials read aloud at huddles, ideally by the rep who closed that client.

##### Leaderboards, cuts, pay ladders, recruiting

A daily leaderboard and competitions every four to six weeks, run in small teams so reps share what works. Cut the bottom performers regularly (he cites about 10% a quarter) after a short improvement plan. Pay attracts reps but rarely changes behaviour, except paying on cash collected. A visible career ladder (setter, qualifier, closer, with objective targets and relegation) gives entry roles a future, and a high top earner becomes the recruiting ad. Hire through group interviews and a coachability test, prefer believers over seasoned closers, and don't make the best closer the manager.

#### Examples

*Cases are paraphrased and anonymised from Alex's videos. Any example marked as invented is for illustration only.*

##### Webinar-funnel coaching business (hotseat, high six figures a month)

<div class="ba" markdown="1">
<div class="before" markdown="1">
**Before.** Webinar show rate about 18%, close rate 14% after the webinar, CAC about $5,800.
</div>
<div class="after" markdown="1">
**After.** Reminders across every medium, honest scarcity, a scripted call opening, role-play and per-rep metrics. From the same ad spend, booked calls went from 58 to 102, close rate to 35% and CAC to about $2,900.
</div>
</div>

The ads did not change. The gains came from shows, the call opening and training.

##### Multi-location service chain (portfolio company, 14 locations)

Staff sold one-off sessions. After secret-shopping the sales, Alex wrote a diagnostic process: a pre-sale questionnaire, the card taken while "setting up the profile", the customer choosing their own goal, the price set for the path to that goal, and a prepay step-down ladder built into the point-of-sale system. Revenue per customer rose from about $200 to about $800, and the chain grew to 32 locations in about 18 months. The service itself stayed the same.

##### Analytics consultancy (invented B2B example)

A Qlik consultancy answers demo requests the next day and books calls a week out. The fix: call within minutes, offer a slot today or tomorrow, send a 5-minute video of a finished dashboard. The pitch has three pillars (trusted data, one model for everyone, answers in seconds), each with an analogy. Price objections go down a ladder: prepaid project, phased payments, then a paid two-week pilot.

#### Common mistakes

- **Polishing the pitch while leads wait.** Slow response, week-out slots and no reminders lose more sales than weak closing.
- **Spreading leads evenly.** Equal routing wastes the best leads on the weakest reps.
- **Improvised discounts.** Unplanned price cuts sound like begging and lower trust.
- **Training by handing out scripts.** Without daily drills and call review, reps drift and nobody can diagnose the script.

#### How Alex would apply it

- **He fixes around the call first.** Speed to lead, same-day slots and reminders come before script changes.
- **He treats show rate and close rate as equal.** A 20% lift in either is worth the same, so he picks whichever is easier from where you are.
- **He routes leads deliberately:** best to the killers, worst to the trainees, no group complaints.
- **He never cuts price to close.** Terms or components change, never the price, and price tests are planned in advance.
- **He coaches one fix at a time** and judges reps on sub-metrics, not close rate alone.
- **He makes the founder sell until about $1M,** then checks the model still works when reps close at half his rate.
- **Questions he would ask:** How fast do you call new leads? What percentage book, show, get an offer and close? How booked are the calendars? Is the team following the script word for word? Which one fix would move this rep's close rate most? Would one more rep earn more than they cost?

#### Worksheet

Answer in writing using last month's numbers.

1. **Speed:** What is the median time from opt-in to first call? Who owns it?
2. **Funnel:** What share of leads booked, showed, received an offer and closed? Which step is furthest below the rest?
3. **Routing:** Which one or two lead traits predict a sale? Who gets the best leads today?
4. **Pitch:** What are your three pillars, and which analogy explains each?
5. **Objections:** List your three most common objections and the step-down ladder you use instead of discounts.
6. **Training:** When did you last record, review and role-play a call, and what was the single fix?

- [Sales Was Hard Until I Understood These 9 Concepts](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=cy2k1GdA-9o&t=1622s) — three rep jobs, pull-ups, circumstances/other people/self (27:02)
