## Hiring & Talent

- id: hiring-and-talent
- book: Alex's Playbook / Sales and people
- original term: Who, not how
- key: Who before how. Hire A-players for the smallest skill gap, pay them well, interview far more than feels reasonable.
- related: delegation-and-owner, constraint, sales-machine, stages-of-growth, business-math

Past the first stage, the speed of a company is the speed at which it recruits. Alex treats talent as the long-term constraint and runs hiring with the same funnel, metrics and standards as customer acquisition.

#### The gist

In almost every lecture on scaling, Alex ends up at the same place: once demand is solved, the business grows only as fast as it can find, train and keep good people. When an owner lists six problems, he often reads them as one: the company is missing around three senior hires. Above roughly $30M a year he describes his own bottleneck as nothing but talent.

<div class="formula" markdown="1">
**Scaling speed** = **speed of recruiting talent**. Ask "who" before "how": often the fastest answer is someone who has already done it.
</div>

He backs this with blunt economics. In his framing an A-player costs a bit more and produces several times as much, so paying properly for the right person is one of the largest arbitrages in business. His recurring rules of thumb: hire when the freed time or capacity returns several times the salary (he mentions 4 to 5x), and treat each new salary band as a new territory that reveals talent you did not know existed.

#### How it works

##### Recruiting is a second funnel

Across at least five videos he describes recruiting as a mirror of the customer pipeline: application generation, application nurture, the interview (which is a sales call), onboarding and training (fulfilment), then retention and ascension. You use the same channels you use for customers: ads, content, your own lists, direct outreach including poaching, referrals and word of mouth. You track the same kind of numbers: reach-outs per interview, finals per hire, cost and time to fill, net headcount after churn.

His rules of thumb: about 100 LinkedIn reach-outs per hire in one example, and strong candidates are gone within about 8 days, so a slow first reply loses them. Referral bonuses are sized against the gross profit a hire produces.

##### Two interview tests

These recur in several talks:

1. **Metrics.** How many relevant metrics does the candidate use to describe their job, and how well do they know them? Someone who cannot define the outcome cannot deliver it. A strong HR candidate once quizzed him on cost per hire, time to fill and two-way fit at 90 days.
2. **Money.** "How does what you do make the business money?" He rejects vague answers. A sales leader should talk about raising conversion across the team's leads; a finance lead about cutting costs and turning numbers into decisions.

For leadership roles he adds a third: if you are not learning from them in the interview, do not hire them. For specific roles he prefers a real problem over hypotheticals: show the actual stats and ask how they would attack it.

##### Interview volume is the method

He interviews far more people than feels sensible. Volume finds the outlier and trains your judgement: by the fiftieth interview for one role, you can tell. He cites a partner who interviewed about 600 developers to find a technical co-founder, and his own five sales managers before one worked. For front-line to manager roles he runs group interviews, where strong candidates speak clearly and help the nervous ones. For a role you do not understand, the interviews are your education.

##### Who to hire

- **Smallest skill gap.** Every skill is trainable; the question is the cost of training it. Prefer the candidate whose missing skill is quickest to teach.
- **Experience you can buy.** For senior roles, hire someone who has already taken a similar business from your size to the size you want, ideally with about ten years in the function. Their mistakes were paid for by someone else.
- **Leaders over managers.** He grades people on three levels: individual contributor (knows what), manager (knows how, teaches it), leader (builds the system that trains people). He says leaders become necessary from about $3M a year.
- **First hires.** The first five or so are extensions of you that take over repeated, low-value work: support and admin, then a setter or salesperson, marketing help, a fractional bookkeeper, a tech person. If you sell well, hire sales later.
- **Bar raiser.** Hire only people who raise the average of the team they join, because people tend to hire slightly worse than themselves.

##### Promotion risk

His most repeated warning: the best closer is rarely the best sales manager. Hunters rarely want to coach. Assess each level separately, show a top performer the manager's day before they ask for it, and keep early titles modest so there is room to hire above. If a promotion fails, redefine their role with someone above them, or part ways. Own your mistake and frame the senior hire as an investment in their career.

##### Standards, tolerance and culture

The weakest team has the manager who tolerates the most. A long list of rules is, in his reading, a sign of low standards and wrong hires. The person with the highest standards should be in charge. His sales-team culture system: visible daily leaderboard, clear KPIs, a competition every 4 to 6 weeks, and the bottom 10% cut each quarter. A high-performance culture keeps attrition low even while firing often, because the strong stay.

##### Firing and pay

Two weeks below KPI means a performance plan; two more weeks without improvement means exit, and new hires hear this on day one. He rarely moves an underperformer sideways (he says nine times out of ten it is the wrong call). On pay: change it only to fix a problem attracting or keeping talent, because raises cannot be taken back. Before approving a new hire, ask for the team's hour-by-hour utilisation. Do not hire ahead of growth, with commission sales reps as the exception.

#### Examples

*Cases are paraphrased and anonymised from Alex's videos. Any example marked as invented is for illustration only.*

##### Used-car dealership with too many priorities

<div class="ba" markdown="1">
<div class="before" markdown="1">
**Before.** A profitable dealership (about $500–600K profit) wants branding, social ads, a commercial-van line and a service shop, all at once, run by the owner.
</div>
<div class="after" markdown="1">
**After.** Alex reads it as a missing-people problem: about three senior hires. First a finance lead, told to find their own salary in savings within 90 days. Then operators for service and the used lot. Only then does the owner chase the van line with freed time.
</div>
</div>

The finance hire funds the next hire, and each senior person removes a whole problem from the owner's list.

##### Wholesale business after a bad promotion

<div class="ba" markdown="1">
<div class="before" markdown="1">
**Before.** Deals fall from about 70 to 40 a month after the sales manager and second-best closer leave. The top closer is promoted and coaches poorly. The owner wants to hire a sales director.
</div>
<div class="after" markdown="1">
**After.** The owner learns sales personally, simplifies one script, enforces it for everyone and trains through call review and drills.
</div>
</div>

The wrong personality was promoted, and a sales-led company whose owner cannot run sales stays hostage to whoever does.

##### Solo agency afraid of the first hire

A solo ads agency with about $90K real profit asked how to make its first hire. Alex's diagnosis: the model is broken, and hiring would only scale the problem. Before hiring, either 10x the price per client or 10x delivery efficiency, because there is no margin to pay anyone.

##### BI consultancy hiring a senior consultant (invented)

| Step | Weak | Strong |
|---|---|---|
| Sourcing | One job post, wait | Outreach to consultants at firms you admire, referral bonus, content aimed at candidates |
| Screening | CV and certifications | Group call, then "which metrics do you track on a project?" |
| Final | Chat about tools | Real client data model and question: "how would you cut report delivery time in half?" |
| Decision | First decent candidate | After 20+ interviews, only if they raise the team average |

#### Common mistakes

- **Hiring the first decent person.** Without volume you have no comparison and no judgement.
- **Promoting the best performer by default.** You can lose a great contributor and gain a weak manager.
- **Saving on salary for a key role.** A few thousand saved on a person who moves the company years forward is a false economy in his view.
- **Recruiting with no system.** No channels, no speed, no metrics, then concluding "good people don't exist".
- **Tolerating low standards to keep the peace.** The tolerance becomes the culture.

#### How Alex would apply it

- **He checks whether talent is really the constraint.** If the model has no margin, he fixes price or delivery before any hire.
- **He makes recruiting the owner's job when it is the bottleneck,** compressing it hard: many interviews in one week, tracked like a sales pipeline.
- **He buys experience for senior roles** and accepts a short-term profit dip to pay for higher-level leaders, framing it as skipping years of mistakes.
- **He interviews with the two tests** (metrics, how the role makes money) and passes on leaders he cannot learn from.
- **He sets standards visibly and enforces them on everyone,** including the top performer, with time-boxed performance plans.
- **He refuses** sideways moves for failing people, inflated titles in small teams, and pay raises that do not solve a recruiting or retention problem.
- **Questions he would ask:** If you had ten of your best person, would you make more money? What are you doing this week to recruit? How many candidates per hire, how fast is first contact? What metrics does this person use for their job? How does their role make money? Does this hire raise the team's average?

#### Worksheet

Answer in writing for your next or most important hire.

1. **Constraint:** Is talent the thing that stops you from doing more, or is it price, demand or the model?
2. **Return:** What will this person free up or produce, and is it at least several times their cost?
3. **Funnel:** Which channels will you use, how many candidates do you need per hire, and how fast will you reply?
4. **Tests:** Which metrics should a strong candidate name, and what real problem will you give them in the final round?
5. **Standards:** What will you no longer tolerate, and what is the written performance-plan rule new hires will hear on day one?

#### Sources

- [13 Years Of Brutally Honest Business Advice in 90 Mins](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KhFlD54nQrY&t=1098s) — the two interview tests, bar raiser, A-player arbitrage and his talent ROI numbers (18:18)
- [How to Build a Business That Runs Without You](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=TLpLmhWqKEU&t=1579s) — interview volume, group interviews, the 600-interview story (26:19)
- [How I recruit the best employees in the world...](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Lxp-e5NionA&t=97s) — recruiting as a mirror of the customer funnel (01:37)
- [The Talent Grid - How I Recognize Skill in Self & Team](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=gpKz22P84iM&t=143s) — IC, manager and leader levels (02:23)
- [The first 5 hires EVERY entrepreneur should make...or go broke...](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=7NqsFh5ej0w&t=47s) — order of the first hires (00:47)
- [How To Build Large Sales Teams (Starting from 0)](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=2lA_A8BGRRs&t=332s) — standards, tolerance, PIP and firing rules (05:32)
- [All Entrepreneurs Have Only 1 Problem...](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=-is6Z2T2h8o&t=378s) — spotting A-players and who should be in charge (06:18)
- [I Reverse Engineered the Perfect Business](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=HKbFUWJwEG0&t=669s) — paying more for A-players, modest titles, experienced operators (11:09)
- [Recruiting and Hiring Tips - How to Hire The Best Employees](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=cemduJKQl5w&t=1254s) — when to change pay and utilisation before hiring (20:54)
- [10 Millionaires Asked Me How to Get Richer](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6m6DCQMASEM&t=1167s) — missing senior hires and the self-funding finance hire (19:27)
- [Every business is limited by the constraint. Here's how to find it.](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=QtE6kk0158o&t=143s) — hiring funnel math and group interviews (02:23)

## Delegation & the Owner's Role

- id: delegation-and-owner
- book: Alex's Playbook / Sales and people
- original term: Buy back your time
- key: Time study → task list → process → decision rules with limits → KPIs → hand off at ~80% as good.
- related: hiring-and-talent, constraint, enterprise-value, stages-of-growth, focus-and-subtraction

Alex sees the founder as the default bottleneck. He buys back time in a fixed order and delegates through a written system, until the business keeps growing while the owner is away.

#### The gist

Early on, a business is a suit of armour that multiplies the owner but cannot move without them. Alex's goal is a business that creates value without you: only then is it worth something to someone else, and only then can you step away. Two companies with the same profit are worth very different amounts if one needs its owner eighty hours a week.

<div class="formula" markdown="1">
**Delegation** = time study → task list → **process** → decision rules with limits → **KPIs** → graduation test (about 80% as good) → shadow, supervise, support
</div>

He treats this as a sequence of giving up control. At each stage, the thing you hold on to is the next thing you must hand off, and it always feels uncomfortable. Handing over control never hands over accountability: you stay responsible for the result and close enough to know where the problems are.

#### How it works

##### The buyback order

Across several lectures the order is consistent:

1. Low-skill support and delivery tasks.
2. Admin: billing, collections, contracts. Then money tracking, once someone tracks it better than you (you still look at it).
3. Selling. He puts the ceiling for founder-led selling around $1M a year.
4. Running the sales team. He places this roughly between $3M and $10M.
5. Leading leaders. From about $10M to $30M you lead a small group, and culture travels through them.

The rule for each task: if the market will do it for a fraction of what your hour is worth, hand it off, even if you believe nobody does it as well. Freed time must go into higher-value work. Founders who free time and then fill it with nothing see margins fall.

##### The delegation system

1. **Time study.** Log what you do every 15 minutes for a week. List every task as granularly as possible. He uses the same study on any key employee the business depends on too much.
2. **Person, process or project.** Next to each task, write whether it needs a person, a process or a one-off project. Rate it green (can hand off now), yellow (you need to build a process first) or red (you lack the skill or the person). Clear greens first, then yellows, then reds.
3. **Decision rules.** Document decisions as well as tasks: if this happens, do that. The more repetitive the role, the more standard the rules. Give each person spending authority without approval; his examples include under $500 per decision with a $5,000 monthly cap, or an operator who can spend up to 1,000 without calling him. A monthly financial review catches waste.
4. **KPIs.** Ask each person how their role makes the company money. If they cannot answer, help them, for example by reversing the costs of doing the job badly into KPIs.
5. **Graduation test.** Can they get 100% of the result about 80% as well as you? At handoff he accepts 60 to 80% if there is a clear path to improve, and judges people by how fast they get better.
6. **Handoff.** They shadow you, then you supervise them, then you are available but not involved.

##### Document, demonstrate, duplicate

His training method recurs in hotseats and sales talks: write the checklist, do the task in front of them using only the checklist, then have them do it in front of you while you correct immediately. Keep the steps simple enough for someone with no experience. If you have no written process, record yourself doing the work: the structure is already there.

##### Know where the bodies are buried

Delegation succeeds only when the function's results hold or improve after the handoff. If they drop, you abdicated. He wants owners able to name each department's current problems. If a department only brings good news, you are too far away. The fix is more visibility, never taking control back.

##### Owner-independence tests

- You are off the company chat, one person can reach you, you meet that person once a week, and the company keeps growing.
- Basic pass: a month away and nothing burns down. Real pass: three months away (or six months in another video) and the business grows. He recommends actually taking the time off, fixing what breaks and repeating.
- A new operator should leave the owner with more time. If they mainly create tasks for the owner, it is not working.
- To train judgement, ask staff "what do you think I would say?" and tell them to do that.

##### When work isn't getting done

Two lectures give the same triangle. Before judging the person, check:

| Cause | Question | Fix |
|---|---|---|
| Expectation (what) | Did I state it clearly, in writing, and is it in their reporting? | Write it down with a deadline; review it often |
| Training (how) | Do they know how, given their workload? | Do it together, record it, define how to report back |
| Motivation (why) | Do they know why it matters to the company and to them? | Explain the why; check the comp plan rewards it |

He starts from "this is probably my fault" and checks expectation first. If communication and training are fixed and nothing changes, he names it as motivation and has the direct conversation. Moving the person to another role is usually wrong.

##### The founder hides the real constraint

His sharpest observation: founders keep working on what they enjoy and are good at, and hand off what they dislike. Their strengths become the bottleneck. A media person optimises media; a closer studies closing. If you dislike people work, recruiting and managing are probably your constraint. Growth requires an identity change: from best salesperson or best coach to the person who teaches and then builds the system. "I have trust issues" is not an acceptable reason in his view.

#### Examples

*Cases are paraphrased and anonymised from Alex's videos. Any example marked as invented is for illustration only.*

##### Field-service owner who is in everything

<div class="ba" markdown="1">
<div class="before" markdown="1">
**Before.** A high-end field-service company, about $1.5M revenue and $700K net. Each technician brings $180–300K a year. The owner insists on being involved in everything and has stopped recruiting.
</div>
<div class="after" markdown="1">
**After.** A one-week time study, the bottom half of his activities delegated, and the freed time spent recruiting technicians nationally with a budget of about $50K per hire.
</div>
</div>

Half the owner's time back could double the business, because a technician is worth far more than the cost to recruit one.

##### Dental practice with one expert doing every step

<div class="ba" markdown="1">
<div class="before" markdown="1">
**Before.** A founder-doctor handles every high-ticket case, capped at about 25–30 patients a month, with a booking delay of weeks.
</div>
<div class="after" markdown="1">
**After.** Associates take the simpler half of the visits. The founder becomes the architect and teacher. Combined with price and payment changes, the follow-up showed average ticket up from about $28K to $44K and booking within a week.
</div>
</div>

Halving the expert's workload doubles capacity. The founder's identity moves from best practitioner to best teacher.

##### Owner who closes best

An event company owner closed about 40% while two reps underperformed. Alex turned the reps into appointment setters for the owner, still on commission, with daily morning and end-of-day check-ins, until the process was documented well enough to hand back.

##### Analytics consultancy founder (invented)

A founder of a ten-person BI consultancy does presales, solution design and code reviews. A time study shows 12 hours a week on reviews. He writes a review checklist (green), records himself doing three reviews (document, demonstrate), then has a senior consultant review while he watches (duplicate). After a month the senior handles all reviews at about 80% of his quality, with a rule: escalate only changes to shared data models. The 12 hours go into presales.

#### Common mistakes

- **Abdication.** Handing off and never looking again. Results drop, and the owner concludes nobody can do it.
- **Delegating only what you dislike.** You keep the tasks you enjoy, and those become the bottleneck.
- **Handing over tasks without decisions.** Without if-then rules and spending limits, every exception still comes back to you.
- **Waiting for 100%.** Demanding your own quality on day one means you never hand anything off.
- **Wasting the freed time.** Time bought back has to go into higher-value work or margins suffer.

#### How Alex would apply it

- **He starts with a time study,** never with a guess. The log shows where the hours go.
- **He hands off from the bottom of the value list** and keeps going in the fixed order: support, admin, selling, the sales team, leaders.
- **He wants decisions on paper,** with if-then rules and a spending limit, so people can act without calling the owner.
- **He sets a graduation bar** at roughly 80% of the owner's quality and accepts less at first if improvement is visible.
- **He diagnoses before he blames:** expectation, then training, then motivation.
- **He tests independence for real** by having the owner leave for a month, then longer, and fixing what breaks.
- **Questions he would ask:** What did your last week look like in 15-minute blocks? Which tasks could someone do for a fraction of your hourly value? Can you name each department's current problems? Would the sales model still work if reps closed half as well as you? Did they know it was expected, do they know how, and do they know why? What are you holding on to because you enjoy it?

#### Worksheet

Run this over the next two weeks.

1. **Time study:** Log one week in 15-minute blocks. Which ten tasks took most of your time?
2. **Colour code:** Mark each task green, yellow or red, with a person, process or project next to it.
3. **Rules:** For your most frequent green task, write five if-then rules and a spending limit.
4. **Bar:** Define what 80% as good looks like and how you will measure it.
5. **Absence:** Pick a date for a week away. What must exist before then so nothing breaks?

- [How to Build a Business That Runs Without You](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=TLpLmhWqKEU&t=530s) — time study, decision rules, spending limits, graduation test (08:50)
- [If I Wanted to Create a Business That Runs Itself, Here's What I'd Do](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=mr4Pw66_490&t=383s) — person, process or project and the green/yellow/red rating (06:23)

- [The Talent Grid - How I Recognize Skill in Self & Team](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=gpKz22P84iM&t=381s) — the truly delegated business test and strengths as bottleneck (06:21)

## Stages of Growth

- id: stages-of-growth
- book: Alex's Playbook / Growth and value
- original term: Stages of growth (scaling roadmap)
- key: Nail it before you scale it: no second location, product or business until the first is profitable and runs without you.
- related: delegation-and-owner, hiring-and-talent, focus-and-subtraction, constraint, more-better-new

Each revenue stage has its own constraint and asks the founder to give up the job that got them there. Nail the current unit before you multiply it.

#### The gist

Alex treats growth as a series of levels, like a video game. Each one has a different boss, and the skills that beat the last boss don't beat the next one. That is why he places almost every hotseat business on his head-count-based scaling roadmap before giving advice: the right move at 5 people is often the wrong move at 50.

Two rules run through all of it. First, at each stage the thing you are holding on to is the thing you have to give up next: delivery, then selling, then marketing, then leading. Second, better before bigger. He refuses to help a business multiply a unit that doesn't work yet, because you only scale the problem.

<div class="formula" markdown="1">
**Nail it** (profitable unit, runs without you) → **squeeze it** (more of what works) → **only then multiply it** (new location, product, channel)
</div>

#### How it works

##### The stages and what changes

He uses revenue bands, with slightly different cut-offs across videos. These are his rough rules of thumb, not fixed laws.

| Stage | Founder's job | Typical constraint | What to give up |
|---|---|---|---|
| $0–100k | Sell something to someone | Getting any reliable flow of customers | Distractions; he notes founder personal issues often derail the $0–1M stage |
| $100k–1M | One product, one avatar, one channel | Founder's own selling and delivery hours | Fulfilment, so you can pass $1M |
| $1–10M | From doing to teaching | Founder-led selling hits its ceiling around $1M; leaders are needed from about $3M | Selling, then running the sales team |
| $10–30M | From managing to leading | Margin compression, channel dependency, lack of leaders | Marketing and direct leadership |
| $30M+ | Deciding and advising | Founder can no longer push growth personally | Leading the culture yourself |

In one lecture he describes the founder's role moving from doing to managing to leading to deciding to advising. The meta-skill in sales changes too: close, then teach others to close, then keep many closers, then recruit someone who can do all of that. He admits he got stuck in the mid-$30Ms for three years because past about three layers of hierarchy he could no longer drive growth himself.

On the head-count roadmap he mentions specific placements: around 10 to 19 employees is the "productize to optimize" phase, where service businesses typically struggle with churn (nothing else to sell) and expensive leads. He also notes that one company can sit at different stages for different functions.

##### First hires and the doer-to-teacher shift

The first five or so hires are extensions of the founder, not departments. He hires in reverse order of value: admin and support first, then a setter or salesperson, marketing help, a fractional bookkeeper and a tech person. The test is the gap between what your hour is worth and what replacing it costs.

The identity change is the hard part. A founder who is the best salesperson or best practitioner has to become the best teacher of that skill, judged by the team's results. His "Talent Grid" names the three levels: individual contributor (what), manager (how, by teaching) and leader (who, building the machine that builds the machine). Strong doers often fail as managers, and few managers become leaders.

##### Nail it before you scale it

This is one of his most repeated rules. Before a second location, product line or business, the first must be profitable and run without the founder. In one talk he sets the bar at six months of growth without you; in another, a three-month absence the business survives and grows through. Opening early doubles debt and overhead while halving talent, and he names over-expansion as a top cause of failure.

He also defines over-expansion precisely: selling to more people than you have hired and trained good people to serve. The fix is to slow sales, recruit and train, cut your own draw and accept a profit dip until delivery, churn and referrals recover.

##### Squeeze before you add

Before multiplying, check unused capacity. In several hotseats he shows that doubling revenue per existing location reaches the goal with almost no extra fixed cost. One retail chain grew existing locations about 4x before opening new ones. Up to about $1M he wants one offer, one avatar, one channel; below about $10M he doesn't worry about channel dependency, above it he wants a second channel.

##### Better before bigger

In mass-market models, rising ad costs, colder audiences and heavier infrastructure squeeze margin as you grow. His illustrative numbers: 40% margin falling to 30% from $1M to $10M, and toward 10% by $30M if nothing changes. The counterweight is word of mouth, so somewhere around $1M to $5M he wants the founder to pause scaling and fix customer experience.

#### Examples

*Cases are paraphrased and anonymised from Alex's videos. Any example marked as invented is for illustration only.*

##### Multi-location clinic chain (hotseat)

<div class="ba" markdown="1">
<div class="before" markdown="1">
**Before.** Six acquired locations, about $5.2M revenue and $1.2M profit. The owner wanted more locations to hit an earnings goal. Lead flow was uneven and standards differed by site.
</div>
<div class="after" markdown="1">
**After.** Alex's plan doubled revenue per location instead: more search spend, a cheap front-end offer with card on file, same-day sale after the assessment, and a sales checklist piloted by one manager at one site before rollout.
</div>
</div>

Fixed costs barely move when an existing site doubles, so new locations may be unnecessary. He also flagged "founder magic": the first site carried the founder's personal pull that the others lacked.

##### Retail service chain with a new sales process (lecture)

A low-wage, multi-location team got a simple process built into the point-of-sale system so no step could be skipped. Average revenue per customer went from about $200 to about $800, and only then did the chain grow from 14 to 32 locations in about 18 months. Packaging and selling were fixed before capacity was added.

##### Founder-dentist at capacity (hotseat)

One founder-doctor handled every high-ticket case, with a booking delay of two to four weeks and close rates of 80–90%. Associates took the simpler half of visits, the founder became the architect and teacher, prices rose and payment moved up front. In the follow-up, average ticket rose from about $28K to about $44K and no new location was needed.

##### Analytics consultancy at €700k (invented)

<div class="ba" markdown="1">
<div class="before" markdown="1">
**Before.** The founder sells every deal, leads every implementation and plans a second offer for a new industry.
</div>
<div class="after" markdown="1">
**After.** The second offer waits. The founder documents delivery, hires a senior consultant to own implementations, and stays on one avatar and one channel until revenue passes €1M and delivery runs for three months without him.
</div>
</div>

#### Common mistakes

- **Multiplying a broken unit.** A second location or product line on top of mediocre unit economics scales the problem and halves your talent.
- **Holding on to the old job.** Founders keep doing what they are best at, so their strength becomes the bottleneck.
- **Promoting doers into leadership by default.** Strong individual contributors often fail as managers; assess each level separately.
- **Starting something new to escape a plateau.** He sees most plateaus as a founder skill gap, not a vehicle problem. A new business caps at the same level.
- **Growing for ego.** Adding reps or locations because a revenue number feels important, without the margin and team to support it.

#### How Alex would apply it

- **He places the business first.** Headcount, revenue and who does what decide which stage's rules apply.
- **He names what the founder must give up next,** and frames it as the next level rather than a loss of control. Accountability stays with the founder.
- **He blocks expansion until the unit is nailed:** profitable, payback known, and growing for months without the owner.
- **He squeezes existing capacity first:** revenue per location, price, close rate, then new units.
- **He accepts a short-term profit hit to hire leaders** when the goal is beyond the founder's own hours, and treats each higher salary band as new territory.
- **He pauses scaling around $1–5M to fix experience** when CAC rises faster than ad costs, a sign of negative word of mouth.
- **Questions he would ask:** How many people work here? Does the core unit make money at full capacity? Does it run without you for three months? What are you holding on to? If you doubled customers, what would break? What do you actually want: many locations, one big one, or an exit?

#### Worksheet

Answer in writing for your business as it is today.

1. **Stage:** What is your revenue band and headcount? Which column of the table are you in?
2. **Holding on:** Which job (delivery, selling, marketing, leading) do you still do that the next stage needs you to give up?
3. **Unit test:** Is your core unit profitable, with known payback, and has it grown for three months without you?
4. **Squeeze:** How much more could the current unit produce before you add a new location, product or channel?
5. **Level check:** Which of your key people are doers, which teach, and which build systems? Who could move up?

#### Sources

- [How to buyback your time as a business owner](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=awOQDy_o9ww&t=238s) — order of buyback, the ~$1M ceiling on founder-led selling (03:58)

- [Business Was Hard Until I Understood These 4 Concepts](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=F84olnKkseM&t=926s) — over-expansion defined and how to fix it (15:26)

## Enterprise Value

- id: enterprise-value
- book: Alex's Playbook / Growth and value
- original term: Enterprise value
- key: Enterprise value = (customers × lifetime gross profit) ÷ risk
- related: stages-of-growth, delegation-and-owner, retention-and-ltv, risk-and-bets, continuity-offers

The difference between owning a well-paid job and owning an asset. A business is worth what it earns without you, multiplied by how little risk a buyer takes on.

#### The gist

Alex separates income from wealth. Income comes from work. Wealth comes from owning things that create value without you. If a business shrinks materially when the owner steps away, he calls it a high-paying job you can't quit, not an asset. Two companies with the same profit can be worth wildly different amounts: one needs the owner 80 hours a week and cannot be sold, the other runs without them and sells at a multiple.

He frames value as a fraction. The top is how many customers you have and how much gross profit each brings over their lifetime. The bottom is risk: everything that makes a buyer worry the profit won't survive the sale.

<div class="formula" markdown="1">
**Enterprise value** = (**customers** × **lifetime gross profit**) ÷ **risk**
</div>

Most of his enterprise-value advice is about shrinking the denominator: concentration risk, churn and founder dependency.

#### How it works

##### Income versus asset

His standard illustration: two businesses at $10M revenue and $2M profit. The owner-run one adds roughly $500k a year to the owner's net worth after tax and living costs. The owner-free one, at a 6x multiple, is worth $12M, and every extra $500k of profit adds about $3M of value against about $250k of take-home cash. In another talk he puts the wealth gap between a business sellable at 10x and one that can't be sold at roughly 20x.

Two guardrails: enterprise value only matters once personal cash needs are covered, and you should build it sellable whether or not you sell, because a sellable business is an asset you can borrow against.

##### Concentration risk

He treats concentration as the line between a job and an asset. It shows up in four places:

| Risk | What it looks like | His default fix |
|---|---|---|
| Key person | One person carries marketing, sales, delivery or the operational "glue" | Process plus people; vesting equity if the key person isn't you |
| Channel | All sales from one ad platform or one referral source | Below ~$10M don't worry; above it, build a second channel |
| Customer | One or two clients are a large share of revenue | Treat it as an acquisition red flag; open "more wells" |
| Vendor / partner | One processor, supplier or platform deal feeds the business | Redundancy in every dependency; keep backups approved |

Key-person risk counts double when the key person is the owner who leaves on a sale. The more areas one person covers, the worse it is. He names founder-as-acquisition-channel as the biggest single version. Single-channel risk is acceptable early; depending on a partner or platform is not.

##### Sticky, recurring revenue

Buyers pay for revenue that repeats. In his rules of thumb, private equity looks for roughly 80% annual customer retention, and licensing or software only earn attractive multiples (6x or more) above that level. A business that sells one product once with no back end is, in his words, closer to a job. Consulting and education are often non-recurring by nature: either find a consumable (such as staff turnover that keeps needing training) or accept that you can make good money with less enterprise value. For B2B he pushes expansion revenue, aiming for net revenue retention above 100%.

##### Brand and proof as moats

Without a defensible product, brand is the main differentiator, and he notes that acquirers buy brands, not products. He lists three measurable effects: higher click-through (lower CAC), more repeat buying (higher LTV) and premium pricing (higher gross profit). Without brand, a product business is "media arbitrage" that gets copied. He also counts capital requirements as a moat.

##### Founder dependency: the route from income to value

His owner-independence test is whether the company keeps growing for months without you stepping back in. The moves he recommends:

1. Time-study your week and hand off tasks to people, processes or projects.
2. Get yourself out of acquisition first, with systems that produce marketing without your face.
3. Replace the founder in delivery with several narrow specialists, each branded as the expert in one area, rather than one generalist clone.
4. Transfer a founder brand gradually: founder alone in ads, then founder plus successor, then each separately, then the successor alone. In his own case it took about 12 months.
5. Keep a personal brand separate from a business you intend to sell.

If the founder is bigger than the company, you can still sell a minority stake, but he calls that a liquidity event, not an exit.

##### Sellability, exits and M&A comps

His multiples, all rules of thumb: businesses under about $10M revenue typically sell at 1–4x earnings, with multiples rising above $10M revenue and $2M profit. Franchisors can get around 15x versus about 8x for local chains, because future openings are contracted. Before building for an exit, he checks M&A activity for that business type, studies how sold businesses differ from yours and closes the gap. He also asks bankers and PE firms what weakens a business; for his own the answers were churn, his name as the brand and a single channel.

##### Sell the eggs, not the goose

The goose is the asset that creates value, often founder-led and unsellable. The eggs are the sellable assets it produces. His advice is usually to keep the goose and build the business to produce and sell eggs. For an educator or consultant to a specific business type, that can mean standardising clients' businesses so the best ones can be rolled up and sold together.

#### Examples

*Cases are paraphrased and anonymised from Alex's videos. Any example marked as invented is for illustration only.*

##### His own licensing business (lecture)

<div class="ba" markdown="1">
<div class="before" markdown="1">
**Before.** A B2B licensing business for gym owners with very high profit, 100% of sales from paid social, and the founder in every ad. The founders wanted out. He judged it a guru brand, not a business.
</div>
<div class="after" markdown="1">
**After.** A competitor's outbound lead built an outbound department from scratch, and ads moved to the general manager and new faces. Within about 12 months outbound was roughly half of revenue, which reassured the acquirer.
</div>
</div>

##### Two businesses, one owner (hotseat)

A commercial builder at about $11M and 18% margin also ran a young elevator-service company at about $3M and 30% margin, with recurring, niche revenue. His own route: sell the construction business quickly, even at a discount, and go all in on the recurring one, because a quarter of focused recurring growth can match the old business's enterprise value.

##### Selling the eggs (lecture)

A consultant to accounting firms wanted to sell his consulting business. Nobody buys that, but buyers do buy accounting firms in groups. His advice: assemble roll-ups of 10 to 20 client firms every year or two and take a slice for arranging the deal.

| | Client firm sold alone | Firms sold together |
|---|---|---|
| Multiple of earnings | ~4x | ~12x |
| Firm owner's share after a ~30% slice | 4x | ~8.4x, about double |

##### BI consultancy with one vendor channel (invented)

<div class="ba" markdown="1">
<div class="before" markdown="1">
**Before.** €1.2M revenue, two clients are 60% of it, nearly all leads come through one software vendor's referrals, and the founder runs every key project.
</div>
<div class="after" markdown="1">
**After.** A direct outbound channel, a managed-service retainer after each project, and two named specialists who lead delivery. Value now depends on contracts and processes, not on one person and one partner.
</div>
</div>

#### Common mistakes

- **Confusing profit with value.** High profit that needs the owner every day has a low or zero multiple.
- **Chasing exit value before cash is secure.** He puts personal cash needs first.
- **Ignoring concentration until a buyer finds it.** Key person, one channel, one big client and one supplier are exactly what acquirers discount.
- **Trying to sell the goose.** A founder-led education or consulting business rarely sells well; the sellable asset is often elsewhere.

#### How Alex would apply it

- **He asks the goal first:** sell eventually, or just make money? If only money, he keeps it simple and runs the working playbook more times.
- **He audits concentration across all four risks** and fixes the one an acquirer would discount most.
- **He removes the founder from acquisition before delivery,** because founder-driven lead flow is the biggest key-person risk.
- **He prioritises retention over new revenue** when the goal is enterprise value, aiming for roughly 80% annual retention.
- **He prices hard problems in enterprise value terms:** losing $100k over six months to find a second channel is cheap if it adds $10–15M of value to a $2M-EBITDA business at 5x.
- **He invests surplus cash in talent and brand** in service businesses, and refuses to trade long-term brand for a short-term sale.
- **Questions he would ask:** Is anything in this business dependent on you? What would an acquirer see as its weaknesses? What share of revenue comes from your top client, channel and supplier? What is annual retention? Who buys businesses like this, and at what multiple? Which part is the goose, and what are the eggs?

#### Worksheet

Score each risk 1 (low) to 5 (high) for your business today.

1. **Founder test:** If you left for three months, which revenue, deals and decisions would stop?
2. **Concentration:** What share comes from your top client, top channel and top vendor or partner?
3. **Stickiness:** What is your annual customer retention, and what recurring or expansion revenue could you add?
4. **Moat:** What do people say about you when you aren't there, and does it let you charge more?
5. **Comps:** Who has bought businesses like yours recently, and how are they different from yours?
6. **Goose and eggs:** What creates the value, and what does it produce that someone else would buy?

## Cases: Local Businesses

- id: cases-local
- book: Alex's Playbook / Casebook
- original term: Hotseat cases
- key: Local businesses are usually underpriced and under-advertised, and their ceiling is the owner or one expert doing all the work.
- related: pricing-decisions, cash-forward, hiring-and-talent, focus-and-subtraction, diagnostic-sequence

Forty-two real situations from gyms, clinics, dental and chiropractic practices, salons, restaurants and home-service contractors, with what Alex diagnosed and what he told the owner to do.

#### The gist

Local owners almost always arrive saying they need more leads. When Alex looks at the numbers, the leads are rarely the first problem. Close rates of 70 to 90 percent, show rates near 100 percent and ads returning 10:1 or better tell him the business is underpriced and spending far too little on a channel that already works. His first moves are usually a price increase of 10 to 40 percent, a dedicated landing page and more ad spend on the channel that is already profitable. Small changes per ticket (menu order, an attached drink, a rebooking step) go straight to profit in a high-volume business.

The second pattern is capacity. Many local businesses are capped by one doctor, one founder in the van or a shortage of technicians. Alex treats recruiting as a core business function with its own funnel and budget, and he works out what one practitioner or rep is worth in gross profit to show the owner how cheap a large hiring bonus really is. He moves the expert to the hardest work and gives the simpler half to associates.

The third pattern is cash timing. Quarterly billing, pay-per-visit models and fronted equipment starve growing local businesses. He restructures the offer so more cash comes in on day one: a front-end assessment with the card on file, a prepaid year, an enrolment fee, a first quarter paid up front. Underneath all of it sits focus. He cuts extra avatars, side ventures and partner deals so the owner can push one model in one market before expanding.

#### Cases

##### Dental practice, multi-location · ~$2M/yr · one expert does every high-ticket case
- **Situation.** About 47% profit. The founder-doctor handles every high-ticket case (cap of ~25–30 patients a month), bills per visit and closes 80–90%; patients wait two to four weeks.
- **Diagnosis.** Supply constraint created by a single expert doing every step. The very high close rate signals underpricing.
- **Advice.** Associates take the simpler half of visits; the founder becomes architect and teacher. Payment up front, card on file at check-in. Raise price about 20%. A VSL before the treatment presentation and faster booking.
- **Why.** Halving the expert's load doubles capacity. In the follow-up the average ticket rose from about $28K to $44K and booking dropped to within a week, with no new locations.
- **Source.** [This Video Should Be Required Viewing for Business Owners](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=uaLNfijnp-8&t=329s) (05:29)

##### Residential trash collection · ~$640K/yr, losing ~$150K · growth starved of cash
- **Situation.** Under two years old, 2,500 customers, two avatars (homes and HOAs), five channels. Bins and commissions are paid out before quarterly billing brings cash in.
- **Diagnosis.** Too many avatars and channels, and an offer that collects too little cash up front to fund growth.
- **Advice.** Pick single homes as the avatar. Go all in on door-to-door with commission-only reps, trained by shadowing and role play. Restructure the offer: bin plus first quarter up front, free months spread later, and an anchor of a prepaid year with the bin free.
- **Why.** Each sale needs about $208 cash to cover labour, bin and commission. If about one in five takes the prepaid year, average cash per sale clears that bar and growth funds itself.
- **Source.** [Building a $1,000,000 Business for a Stranger in 69 Minutes](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Wr6n_zNKvMk&t=0s) (00:00)

##### Chiropractic chain, 6 locations · ~$5.2M/yr · inconsistent lead flow
- **Situation.** About 23% profit, CAC about $700 against LTV about $3,400, 80% show rate, 71% close, yet only $1.5–2K of ads per location per month. Insurance checks push the sale to day two, and each site sells differently.
- **Diagnosis.** Underspending on very profitable search ads, a weak social front-end offer, and selling after treatment instead of at peak pain.
- **Advice.** In order: raise search spend sharply until returns fall to about 5:1; a $29 front-end offer with card on file; collect insurance card on the booking call and print plans each morning; sell the same day after assessment; pilot a checklist sales process at one site; add local SEO.
- **Why.** Doubling revenue per location hits the profit goal without opening new sites.
- **Source.** [Building a $5,000,000 Business for a Stranger in 42 Mins](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=sBJppqCeFGI&t=2251s) (37:31)

##### HVAC and duct cleaning · ~$1.25M/yr · wants more and better leads
- **Situation.** About 38% profit, $7.2K a month on marketing, 99% show rate, 82% close, $60K of debt, a new van and new staff.
- **Diagnosis.** Demand-constrained with nothing badly broken: underpriced, weak landing page, under-spent ads, no reactivation, one productive referral partner.
- **Advice.** In order: raise price 10% and hold it while close stays above about 65%; keep paying down debt; a dedicated ad landing page; more creative variety, then more spend; retargeting and branded search; monthly reactivation campaigns; an affiliate offer where partners sell the dryer-vent service and keep the money while he upsells the core job; outbound to HOA events last.
- **Why.** A 10% price rise adds roughly 25% to net profit, ads already return about 13:1, and the order protects capacity.
- **Source.** [Building a $2,500,000 Business for a Stranger in 36 Minutes](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=OQf2Ba-Lp_4&t=234s) (03:54)

##### Roofing and gutter contractor · ~$20K/month, two months old · thinks salespeople are the constraint
- **Situation.** 59% margin, sales from door-knocking (about one per 20 doors) and referrals. Replacements bring about $7K gross profit, repairs about $1.5K at 64%.
- **Diagnosis.** Repairs are an overlooked, uncontested, fast-cycle product with no cash drag. The owner's door-to-door skill is the engine; average ticket and recurring revenue are the missed levers.
- **Advice.** Systematise repairs and lift the ticket toward $2.5–5K with a menu upsell at follow-up; a VSL for each step; scale doors with commission framed per door; a $180–240/year maintenance plan credited toward repairs; a paid priority fee of about 20%.
- **Why.** Three repairs a day can match a replacement in gross profit. Ninety days later monthly jobs rose from about 15–20 to 60–85.
- **Source.** [How I Would Build a $10M Service Business (If I Had to Start Over)](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=o64cI6tebnU&t=194s) (03:14)

##### Thai restaurant, one location · ~$3.5M/yr · opening a second, larger site
- **Situation.** 72 seats, 19% net, weekend queues, alcohol only about 1% of sales.
- **Diagnosis.** Great product, weak monetisation (pricing, menu layout, drinks, return of first-timers, review asks) and no staff training system.
- **Advice.** Add .99 to entrées; reorder the menu by profit with highlight boxes; drink pairings and a weekly drink special on a table tent; a first-timer napkin with free dessert and a manager card; a drink in exchange for a review at the check; a short daily staff skit for training; an influencer night to open the new site.
- **Why.** In high-volume, thin-margin businesses tiny per-ticket gains drop straight to profit and need almost no operational change.
- **Source.** [Building a $3,500,000 Business for Two Strangers in 52 Minutes](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=h9UyQiLjSHg&t=922s) (15:22)

##### Lash salon chain, 7 locations · ~$2.3M/yr · leads, staff turnover, partners not pulling weight
- **Situation.** About 28% net after ten years. Managers are trained inconsistently, three partners are misaligned, the owner is unpaid and staff leave.
- **Diagnosis.** The partnership drains all bandwidth; upsell and rebooking are untrained; marketing is badly underfunded.
- **Advice.** Fix partners first: an equity swap for the misaligned one, a buyout for the one leaving in exchange for helping replace her. Then 10–20% weekend pricing, rebooking before the service, membership as the default, prepay at checkout, a two-year career path with skill unlocks and a profit share for top staff, an attribution checklist at check-in, a dedicated lead caller, and higher ad spend once tracking works.
- **Why.** Nothing executes while the partnership drain continues; after that, quick high-impact items come first.
- **Source.** [Building a $2,300,000/yr Business for a Stranger in 57 Mins](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=-Koq14DPXC4&t=961s) (16:01)

##### Physical therapy and pain clinic, 3 locations · ~$4.2M/yr, ~zero profit · reimbursements falling
- **Situation.** Out-of-network, too many services and avatars, no ad-driven acquisition, can't afford senior talent. Wants $20M on a cash-pay model.
- **Diagnosis.** Margin squeeze plus no acquisition system; choosing cash pay means choosing to get good at marketing and selling.
- **Advice.** Drop the lowest-paying insurers to restore cash flow. Build a cash front end: local Meta ads in a 10-mile radius, a cheap discounted assessment that puts a card on file, call leads within 60 seconds, sell after the assessment. Settle on one to three front-end offers and cross-sell the rest. Upsell existing patients first.
- **Why.** Cash businesses win on an efficient front door plus ascension to the highest-LTV service.
- **Source.** [$100M Investor Answers The Most Important Business Questions for 1 Hour](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=--9kOXNCUdE&t=97s) (01:37)

##### Indoor family entertainment centre · ~$4.8M/yr, losing money · weeks from zero cash
- **Situation.** About 150 staff, $400K a month in revenue against $550K needed to break even, $180K a month of debt service, peak days at half capacity.
- **Diagnosis.** Cash is the immediate threat; the business needs about $600K extra before the slow season, and it tracks outputs instead of inputs.
- **Advice.** Bet on summer camp: hit the email list, run a 7–10 day giveaway (a whole summer as first prize, a big discount for everyone else), a 72-hour claim window, phone follow-up of every lead, then evergreen multi-week bundles. Later, more callers and the same giveaway for birthdays.
- **Why.** Summer is when parents spend big and book now. Reported result: about 3,700 leads and summer camp revenue up 325%, limited by too few callers.
- **Source.** [Can I Save This Failing Theme Park in 90 Days?](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=xRxVpjeHmeA&t=284s) (04:44)

##### Moving company · ~$300K/yr · loses ~40% of quotes on price
- **Situation.** 62% profit, hourly pricing near the top of the market. Leads from maps/SEO and realtors; realtor jobs average about $3K versus about $1K from search. Wants more locations.
- **Diagnosis.** A commodity offer and a weak sales process; leads are sufficient.
- **Advice.** A VIP tier (senior movers, materials, packing, insurance, price cap) beside standard; a same-day surcharge starting at 10%; name the differentiators on every call; free VIP upgrades for realtors who refer; personalised SMS and DM outreach to realtors; meet brokerage heads in person; a review bonus for foremen; before/after content.
- **Why.** One brokerage can bring dozens of moves a year, and differentiation wins the jobs currently lost on price.
- **Source.** [Give Me 42 Minutes, I'll Show You How To Sell Like The Top 1% | Scale or Fail Episode 1](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=zzleYxkf39k&t=2001s) (33:21)

##### Concierge longevity medical practice · ~$3M/yr · owner believes marketing is the bottleneck
- **Situation.** About $600K profit plus owner pay, ~475 patients, 16 staff. Growth from word of mouth and some SEO; one past event went badly.
- **Diagnosis.** The demand side is under-used; the sales process works for warm traffic; referrals happen but aren't systematised.
- **Advice.** Replace the small cash referral credit with a high-value treatment members can give a friend, and ask who they're bringing at each visit. A quarterly giveaway in the newsletter. Weekly events at local high-net-worth gatherings with a discounted scan and a same-day close. Leave the sales process alone.
- **Why.** Cash discounts mean nothing to wealthy clients, a guest treatment costs almost nothing, and high-trust sales close best in person.
- **Source.** [$100M Worth of Marketing Knowledge in 32 Minutes | Scale or Fail](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=B-ogfFiQpXg&t=238s) (03:58)

##### Online psychiatry practice, multi-state · ~$1.5M/yr · keeps 1.3% of it
- **Situation.** Seven providers, 89% insurance, practitioners paid half of collections, 85% of demand from one channel. Wants $5M.
- **Diagnosis.** One model problem instead of three constraints: insurers set the price, so margin can only come from lower cost or from cash pay.
- **Advice.** Ask a sorting question at intake (best possible outcome versus what insurance covers) and sell one simple one-time plan of roughly $300–600 on top of the insured visit. Push costs down too; a 1% margin is not a law of nature.
- **Why.** At 1.3% margin, every 10% of add-on revenue multiplies profit many times over, with little extra operational drag.
- **Source.** ["He Makes $1.5M a Year... and Keeps 1.3% of It" | Alex Hormozi Answers Live](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=lidFtb1Ga0o&t=141s) (02:21)

##### Short-let cleaning company · ~$30–40K/month · treated as a marketing business
- **Situation.** A former gym owner cleaning holiday rentals. LTV is high and CAC is about $6.
- **Diagnosis.** Customers are easy to get; the constraint is finding and keeping reliable cleaners. It is a recruiting and training business.
- **Advice.** Mirror the customer funnel for talent: generate applicants, interview, onboard, train and give people a path to advance.
- **Why.** Once the talent funnel worked, the business grew past $150K a month within about a year.
- **Source.** [Your Business Is NOT What You Think It Is](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=rj7rzOdj84A&t=334s) (05:34)

##### Family fence company · ~$20M/yr · hesitant to double the sales team
- **Situation.** Goal $50M, enough leads, five salespeople.
- **Diagnosis.** Sales capacity is the constraint, and the fear isn't backed by any cash-flow problem.
- **Advice.** Double the sales team and build a sales recruiting and training academy that runs parallel to the customer funnel.
- **Why.** Each rep brings about $1M in gross profit for about $200K in cost, roughly 5:1, and adds enterprise value.
- **Source.** [Watch This If You Have a Service Business](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jqo0lVveh98&t=2443s) (40:43)

##### Physical therapy for adult athletes, 2 locations · ~$3M/yr · more demand than practitioners
- **Situation.** Considering concierge pricing or an e-commerce side line.
- **Diagnosis.** Supply-constrained, and the owner hasn't worked out what a practitioner is worth.
- **Advice.** Treat recruiting as the business: a career path, outreach, large referral bonuses (around $20K instead of $500) and headhunters.
- **Why.** Each practitioner brings about $350K of gross profit a year, so paying $50–100K to land one is an easy return.
- **Source.** [Answering Your Top Business Questions for 1 Hour](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=QwEb78e5a8Y&t=1533s) (25:33)

##### High-end field service · ~$1.5M/yr · owner in everything, recruiting stopped
- **Situation.** About $700K net; each technician produces $180–300K a year.
- **Diagnosis.** The owner is the bottleneck by choice, and nobody is recruiting.
- **Advice.** Do a time study and delegate the bottom half of the owner's tasks. Spend about $50K per technician on national recruiting ads with a relocation bonus, and keep them as employees.
- **Why.** Getting half the owner's time back could double the business, and a technician is worth far more than the cost of recruiting one.
- **Source.** [Helping 6 Business Owners Scale in 33 Minutes](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0EqJD2o-Mnk&t=755s) (12:35)

##### Electrical contractor · ~$1.6M/yr · owner back in the van when crew are out
- **Situation.** About $650K kept, wants $5M. Work comes from six or seven HVAC companies.
- **Diagnosis.** Capacity constraint plus dependence on a handful of referral partners.
- **Advice.** Raise price 20–40%, backed by an on-time, on-budget guarantee. Use the extra cash to hire redundancy in the crew. Reach out to more HVAC firms for quotes.
- **Why.** Price funds the staff that removes the owner from the van, and more partners give negotiating leverage.
- **Source.** [Helping 6 Business Owners Scale in 33 Minutes](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0EqJD2o-Mnk&t=421s) (07:01)

##### Roofing, door-to-door · ~$3M/yr · bought leads and CAC overtook revenue
- **Situation.** Historically 100% door-knocking. After buying leads, reps stopped knocking; CAC reached about $7,500 against roughly $6,500 revenue per customer. Wants $10M.
- **Diagnosis.** Inbound and outbound mixed in one team.
- **Advice.** Send everyone back to the doors. One top closer works inbound leads while the company iterates on source, offer and process. Hire a strong in-house marketer.
- **Why.** Fewer new variables means faster learning, and inbound leads become a reward for the best sellers.
- **Source.** [Helping 6 Business Owners Scale in 33 Minutes](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0EqJD2o-Mnk&t=1083s) (18:03)

##### Nail salon in a mall · ~€600K/yr · half the seats empty in winter
- **Situation.** Average ticket €65, five of ten seats filled. One giveaway was run earlier without any back end to follow it.
- **Diagnosis.** A demand-generation problem rather than seasonality. The long-term constraint is keeping technicians.
- **Advice.** Rerun the giveaway now that the back end is fixed; alternate front-end offers and judge them by LTV:CAC. Build a career path for techs with skill levels and profit-share management roles.
- **Why.** Technicians leave once their book is full unless staying is the easiest path.
- **Source.** ["Should He Walk Away From His $1.4M Business To Start Over?" | Alex Hormozi Answers Live](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0RjIw9M1j28&t=6403s) (106:43)

##### Chiropractic practice · ~$2.4M/yr, flat for five years · no attribution
- **Situation.** About 30% margin. A new doctor shifted it from supply- to demand-constrained. Half the patients come by referral, about 20% from Meta ads, with no tracking.
- **Diagnosis.** No input-output equation, demand-constrained and short of cash.
- **Advice.** Fix pricing and packaging first to free cash; install attribution; scale ads, which work well for a practice with local reputation; produce more thought-leadership content to widen the radius people will travel.
- **Why.** Cash flow is the first need; a trusted local brand makes ads convert and supports premium price.
- **Source.** [Watch This If You Have a Service Business](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jqo0lVveh98&t=141s) (02:21)

##### Teeth-whitening chain · ~$500K per location · franchise or own the units
- **Situation.** About $50K to open a unit, about $250K profit per location per year.
- **Diagnosis.** Very strong owned-unit economics, roughly 5x return on capital each year.
- **Advice.** Run the numbers both ways. At the multiples he assumed, each owned location adds about $2M of value, each franchise about $300K; $100M of value needs about 50 owned units or about 333 franchises. Choose by achievable opening rate and founder type, and hire for the gap (for example a leader who has already scaled a chain).
- **Why.** With that return on capital, owning compounds wealth far faster.
- **Source.** [How to Grow ANY Local Business (my framework)](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=BHMeYaHEMpc&t=96s) (01:36)

##### Assisted living and memory care · private pay · profit arrives too slowly
- **Situation.** Sales are fine, residents stay a year or more, but profit only appears after two to three months.
- **Diagnosis.** A cash-timing problem; demand is fine.
- **Advice.** Three options: an up-front enrolment fee, the first three months paid up front, or a move-in bundle of services residents need anyway (transport, hair and beauty, furniture or bed upgrades) sold up front.
- **Why.** People resist monthly fees far more than one-time fees, and a life transition is a moment when they want everything handled.
- **Source.** [How to Build a Business That Runs Without You](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=TLpLmhWqKEU&t=2831s) (47:11)

##### Alex's own gym · boot camp at $99/month · a product he didn't believe in
- **Situation.** He thought semi-private training got far better results than the cheap boot camp.
- **Diagnosis.** A low price on the weaker product.
- **Advice.** He moved everyone to semi-private at $299 a month after a two-week free trial, with a hardship note for those who couldn't pay.
- **Why.** A third left and two-thirds stayed at three times the price, so revenue doubled with fewer clients and profit more than doubled.
- **Source.** [NEVER lower your prices...](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=BZQtuK-ucDM&t=46s) (00:46)

##### Alex's own gym at capacity · changed the product to fit more people
- **Situation.** Packed classes and members who loved them.
- **Diagnosis.** He merged and shortened sessions to squeeze in more people and lost about a third of members.
- **Advice.** When people love the product, replicate it in a new location or raise the price. Leave the product alone.
- **Why.** Product love is the hardest thing to earn; price is the easiest thing to change.
- **Source.** [How to Start a Business From Nothing (Thank Me Later)](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=unshZobTt6Q&t=3958s) (65:58)

##### In-home IV therapy · ~$1M/yr, ~50% EBITDA · thinking about consumer ads in new cities
- **Situation.** Growth comes from physician referrals.
- **Diagnosis.** Each referring affiliate costs about $500 to acquire but sends only about one $6K patient a year; nobody activates them or closes the loop.
- **Advice.** Assign an affiliate manager, systematise report-back calls to the referring doctor, choreograph the first referral. Then copy the same B2B playbook into new markets instead of switching to consumer ads.
- **Why.** The existing channel is far from squeezed; a couple of hundred activated affiliates referring regularly could reach the goal.
- **Source.** [$100M Investor Answers The Most Important Business Questions for 1 Hour](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=--9kOXNCUdE&t=3187s) (53:07)

##### Restaurant operator, 3 sites via partner deals · wants a large fast-casual chain
- **Situation.** Runs three restaurants through profit shares and partial equity; opening a fourth he fully owns.
- **Diagnosis.** Attention split across partner deals before the model is proven.
- **Advice.** Exit the other deals quickly, live in the fourth location, design it for radical simplicity, optimise return on capital and payback per site, nail grand openings and a manager career path. Decide franchise versus owned later.
- **Why.** Payback per location sets how fast the next one can open.
- **Source.** ["He Makes $1.5M a Year... and Keeps 1.3% of It" | Alex Hormozi Answers Live](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=lidFtb1Ga0o&t=6074s) (101:14)

##### Commercial construction plus elevator service · ~$11M + ~$3M/yr · owner splits time
- **Situation.** Construction at about 18% margin; a new elevator service business did about $3M at 30% in year one. Wants to sell within 5–10 years.
- **Diagnosis.** Elevator service is the far better business (recurring, niche); splitting attention slows it.
- **Advice.** The safe route: spend roughly $800K of profit on leaders to run construction. Alex's own route: sell construction quickly, even at a discount, and go all in on elevators.
- **Why.** A focused quarter of growth in recurring revenue can equal the whole value of the old business. He did the same with his gyms.
- **Source.** [Helping 6 Business Owners Scale in 33 Minutes](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0EqJD2o-Mnk&t=1700s) (28:20)

##### Permanent holiday lighting · ~$1M/yr, ~51% net · no repeat revenue
- **Situation.** One location, about $5K ticket, CAC about $300.
- **Diagnosis.** A one-time sale with nothing to buy afterwards.
- **Advice.** Add seasonal temporary decoration campaigns and maintenance checks, use every visit to offer upgrades, and track the share of customers who buy again each year.
- **Why.** Reliable repeat purchases make the business valuable even without a subscription.
- **Source.** [7 Millionaires Asked Me How to Get Rich(er)](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=bgBIO6nZawg&t=342s) (05:42)

##### Junk removal for property managers · ~$1M/yr · dabbling in every channel
- **Situation.** Mostly referrals, plus small tests of cold email, LinkedIn, social and networking. Wants $10M.
- **Diagnosis.** Effort spread across channels with no commitment.
- **Advice.** Any channel can work, so pick by skill. The owner is best in person: go from a few networking events a week to several a day, then use the extra cash to learn a second channel.
- **Why.** Doing more of what already works is the lowest-risk move and frees cash.
- **Source.** [Helping 6 Business Owners Scale in 33 Minutes](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0EqJD2o-Mnk&t=1415s) (23:35)

##### Roofing roll-up, 8 brands · ~$140M/yr · wants an irresistible offer
- **Situation.** Recently acquired by private equity; hard costs are high.
- **Diagnosis.** The offer is not the immediate constraint; centralising sales already gives a big gain and is needed for the exit.
- **Advice.** Centralise virtual sales first over six months or more and cut the bottom third of reps. Later add a profit guarantee (on time and on budget, or the company refunds its profit) instead of a money-back promise.
- **Why.** A business of that value shouldn't stack risky changes; centralisation alone might add about 25% to EBITDA.
- **Source.** [$100M Investor Answers The Most Important Business Questions for 1 Hour](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=--9kOXNCUdE&t=1593s) (26:33)

##### Used-car dealership · ~$500–600K profit · wants five initiatives at once
- **Situation.** Branding, social ads, a commercial-van line ($6–12K margin per unit against about $2K per car) and a service shop, all at the same time.
- **Diagnosis.** Too many priorities; underneath, a gap of about three senior leaders.
- **Advice.** Hire a finance or controller type first, partly paid by finding their salary in cost cuts within 90 days. Then operators for service and the used lot. Then the owner pursues the van line with the freed time.
- **Why.** Finance discipline alone could find about $250K a year, and paying for senior talent is the next level the owner has to break into.
- **Source.** [10 Millionaires Asked Me How to Get Richer](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6m6DCQMASEM&t=1217s) (20:17)

##### Garage-door services · ~$7M/yr, ~$1.5M cash flow · building marketing from scratch
- **Situation.** Most leads come from eight home-warranty companies.
- **Diagnosis.** A working partner channel plus no owned marketing.
- **Advice.** Keep adding warranty partners. Outsource local search ads and SEO to vendors first. Hire a young in-house buyer for local social ads with performance upside. Later, roll up competitors.
- **Why.** Search ads are stable and suit agencies; local social creative fatigues every two to three weeks, so it needs in-house output.
- **Source.** [10 Millionaires Asked Me How to Get Richer](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6m6DCQMASEM&t=2710s) (45:10)

##### Implant-focused dental practice · priced below market · half referrals, half search ads
- **Situation.** The owner believes they give more value than competitors for a slightly lower price.
- **Diagnosis.** Underpriced.
- **Advice.** Raise prices. Document the existing sales process from call recordings. Long term, buy other practices.
- **Why.** Price increases go straight to profit, and in mispriced businesses close rates often rise after the increase.
- **Source.** [10 Millionaires Asked Me How to Get Richer](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6m6DCQMASEM&t=3342s) (55:42)

##### Telehealth clinic · ~$550K/month · wants to start a consultancy for physicians
- **Situation.** Grew fast for two years, then stalled. The treatment can't be advertised on the major social platforms.
- **Diagnosis.** The owner doesn't know how to scale acquisition and wants to escape into a new venture.
- **Advice.** Drop the consultancy. Restate the problem as finding a permitted alternative channel to acquire more patients, and focus entirely on that.
- **Why.** The new venture would enter a saturated market with no track record and nothing unique, while the existing engine already works.
- **Source.** [Entrepreneurs, stop starting new businesses. Fix the one you've got.](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=s59jbVBprn0&t=48s) (00:48)

##### Roofing and exterior remodelling · ~$6M/yr · owner works 2–3 hours a week, wants $100M
- **Situation.** Held back by comfort, protecting family time and side investments in real estate.
- **Diagnosis.** A trade-off problem: he wants both outcomes, and growth requires giving something up.
- **Advice.** Give up short-term profit to hire senior leaders who drive growth. Hold firm on family time. Keep real estate passive.
- **Why.** Changing nothing changes nothing; profit can be earned back, family time cannot, and a career allows only a few big runs.
- **Source.** [Helping 6 Business Owners Scale in 33 Minutes](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0EqJD2o-Mnk&t=47s) (00:47); [Watch This If You Have a Service Business](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jqo0lVveh98&t=1723s) (28:43)

##### Health restaurant, first year · ~$1.5M pace · constant fires and a tired partner
- **Situation.** 100–200 regulars, operational chaos, and a partner who keeps saying they have suffered enough.
- **Diagnosis.** Demand is proven, so this is the normal kind of hard. The real issue is a partner whose attitude undermines the culture.
- **Advice.** Keep going. Have a direct conversation that sets expected behaviour, or buy the partner out on a payment plan and take all the risk and upside. Hire aggressively and state standards openly.
- **Why.** Push when demand exists; staff copy the least hard-working owner.
- **Source.** ["Should He Walk Away From His $1.4M Business To Start Over?" | Alex Hormozi Answers Live](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0RjIw9M1j28&t=5639s) (93:59)

##### Local business with 2–3 leads a day · slow follow-up
- **Situation.** A small daily flow of inbound leads, called back whenever someone had time (from Alex's gym years).
- **Diagnosis.** Lead response speed was the leak.
- **Advice.** Pay one full-time person whose only job is to call every lead the moment it arrives.
- **Why.** He closed about 55% of leads this way, against the roughly 10% he saw as typical.
- **Source.** [How to Start a Business From Nothing (Thank Me Later)](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=unshZobTt6Q&t=4103s) (68:23)

##### Alex's gym versus a bigger competitor · optimising the ad ratio
- **Situation.** He ran ads at about 10:1 while a competitor spent ten times as much at about 4:1.
- **Diagnosis.** He optimised the return ratio instead of total profit.
- **Advice.** Keep scaling spend for as long as absolute profit keeps growing.
- **Why.** The competitor netted about $45K a month against his $13.5K.
- **Source.** [How to Start a Business From Nothing (Thank Me Later)](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=unshZobTt6Q&t=3860s) (64:20)

##### Brick-and-mortar chain, two founders · plateaued
- **Situation.** A portfolio company whose growth had stalled under the founders.
- **Diagnosis.** It needed a seasoned operator.
- **Advice.** Bring in someone who had already taken a chain from about 100 to 2,000 locations.
- **Why.** Growth continued and profit expanded much faster; the operator even paused openings for a quarter to fix per-location profit, which also lowered risk for a buyer.
- **Source.** [I Reverse Engineered the Perfect Business](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=HKbFUWJwEG0&t=288s) (04:48)

##### Multi-location portfolio company · from ~$2M a year to ~$2M a month · plateaued
- **Situation.** The CEO didn't know where the profit went or how many new locations the business could afford.
- **Diagnosis.** No finance function.
- **Advice.** Build finance leadership, and treat that feeling of uncertainty as the signal that a function is missing.
- **Why.** Finance tells you what share of cash flow can fund new openings.
- **Source.** [I Reverse Engineered the Perfect Business](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=HKbFUWJwEG0&t=3497s) (58:17)

##### Alex's first gym · ~$6K left after build-out · rent ~$5K a month
- **Situation.** Almost no cash to pay for services the gym needed.
- **Diagnosis.** Cash constraint.
- **Advice.** Barter memberships for cleaning, IT, laundry, food and video production, and run cheap Facebook ads.
- **Why.** An extra member cost him almost nothing, so trading memberships was far cheaper than paying cash.
- **Source.** [How to Start a Business From Nothing (Thank Me Later)](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=unshZobTt6Q&t=95s) (01:35)

##### Alex's second gym · ~8x the investment of the first · same earnings
- **Situation.** About $250K went into a premium fit-out.
- **Diagnosis.** He spent on what he liked instead of what members valued: community, accountability and trainer quality.
- **Advice.** Learn the job the customer hires you for before reinvesting.
- **Why.** Spend only goes further where customers see value.
- **Source.** [How to Start a Business From Nothing (Thank Me Later)](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=unshZobTt6Q&t=1761s) (29:21)

#### Patterns

- **Underpriced, then a price rise first (about 10 cases).** Close rates of 70–90% and full calendars read to him as underpricing. Typical moves: 10–40% increases, VIP tiers, weekend or same-day surcharges, priority fees. He watches the close rate and holds the price while it stays healthy.
- **Underspending on a channel that already works (about 8 cases).** Search or social returning 5:1 to 13:1 on a tiny budget, an untapped door-to-door team or networking. He scales the winner before testing anything new, and judges success by total profit.
- **Capacity capped by one expert or too few staff (about 9 cases).** Founder-doctors, owners in the van, missing technicians, cleaners and practitioners. He prices out what one hire is worth in gross profit and makes recruiting a funnel with a real budget.
- **Cash collected too late (about 7 cases).** Quarterly billing, pay-per-visit, slow resident profit. He moves cash forward with card-on-file assessments, prepaid years, enrolment fees and up-front bundles.
- **Too many avatars, channels or ventures (about 8 cases).** Trash with two avatars and five channels, clinics with many services, owners with side ventures. He cuts to one avatar, one channel and one model before expanding.
