- **Weak front door and slow selling (about 6 cases).** No dedicated landing page, sales delayed to day two, leads called back late. He adds a cheap front-end offer, same-day selling and an instant call-back.
- **Missing back end and rebooking (about 6 cases).** No membership, no rebook, no maintenance plan, no second visit for first-timers. He adds a default membership or plan and an ask at every visit.
- **Owner, partner or leadership gap (about 8 cases).** Misaligned partners, missing finance function, no senior operators. He fixes partnerships first and pays up for experienced leaders, accepting lower short-term profit.
- **Leave a loved product alone (2–3 cases, from his own gyms).** When customers love it, replicate or raise price; spend where customers see value.

#### How Alex would apply it

**First questions.**
1. What are your show rate, close rate and return on ad spend? Very high numbers mean you are underpriced and underspending.
2. Are you limited by demand (empty chairs, open slots) or by supply (waiting list, owner or expert fully booked)?
3. How much cash do you collect on day one per customer, and does it cover the cost of getting and serving them?
4. How many avatars, services, locations and side ventures are you running, and which one makes the money?
5. What does one more technician, practitioner or rep bring in gross profit per year?

**Default moves by situation.**
- **High close rate, open capacity:** raise price 10–20%, build a dedicated landing page, then increase spend on the channel that already works until returns drop toward about 5:1.
- **Waiting list or expert at capacity:** move simple work to associates, make recruiting a funnel with a budget sized to a hire's gross profit, then raise price.
- **Cash squeeze:** restructure the offer so more cash comes up front (assessment with card on file, prepaid year, enrolment fee, first quarter up front), cut the lowest-margin payers or services, and stop new initiatives.
- **Lots of leads, poor conversion:** call leads within a minute, sell the same day after the assessment, add a VIP tier and name the differentiators.
- **One-time sale:** add a membership or maintenance plan, rebook before the customer leaves, and ask for a referral at each visit.
- **Owner wants a new venture or national expansion:** first dominate the current market and fix the current constraint; a new business rarely fixes the skill gap behind the plateau.
- **Multiple locations:** fix profit and payback per location before opening the next, and bring in a finance lead and a seasoned operator.

## Cases: Service & B2B Businesses

- id: cases-services
- book: Alex's Playbook / Casebook
- original term: Hotseat cases
- key: Service businesses are usually underpriced, sold by the hour, fed by one channel the owner doesn't control, and leaking clients in the first months.
- related: pricing-decisions, retention-and-ltv, sales-machine, focus-and-subtraction, enterprise-value

Thirty-six real situations from agencies, consultancies, law and accounting firms, staffing, B2B services and real-estate and insurance businesses, with what Alex diagnosed and what he told the owner to do.

#### The gist

Service owners usually come to Alex with a growth goal and a vague sense that they need more leads. In most of these cases the business is underpriced, priced by the hour or by cost-plus, and sold to anyone who asks. His first move is to repackage the work as an outcome with a clear price, often a fixed fee with a performance component, an anchor tier far above it and a cheaper downsell below. He reads close rates near 100% as proof the price is too low, and he expects some fall in close rate when the price rises.

The second pattern is acquisition that depends on something the owner doesn't control: vendor referrals, a handful of partners, the founder's network, one ad platform. He adds an owned channel, usually outbound to a defined list (top agents by volume, high-spending advertisers, partner types), and keeps doing more of whatever already works before trying something new. Partnerships and affiliates get real attention in his advice; he wants them activated, measured per partner and given an offer that changes their behaviour.

The third pattern is churn. Agencies and recurring services lose clients in months three to six, and he traces it to the wrong clients and a weak start more than to the work itself. He tightens the avatar to those who activate, adds qualification friction before the sale, makes the first 30 days deliver a visible win and keeps communicating what was done. For exits he looks at what a buyer would pay for: recurring revenue, multiple channels and no dependence on the founder, sometimes reached by rolling up the clients instead of selling the service firm.

#### Cases

##### B2B hospitality consultancy · ~$1.1M/yr · $30K programme sold cold, LTV:CAC 1.7
- **Situation.** About 11% net after 4.5 years. A $30K 90-day programme is sold to strangers from paid ads at about $11.8K CAC. Retainer clients churn. Goal: triple.
- **Diagnosis.** A high-ticket offer pushed at cold traffic, weak ads, a high-friction funnel, and churn because value isn't shown.
- **Advice.** Call-out ads with pain hooks and a lead magnet; a two-page funnel with a VSL above the scheduler; a $5.8K audit as the front end, closed in one call by the founder; roll that credit into a quarterly retainer, with the big fee waived for annual commitment; show clients their numbers monthly.
- **Why.** A lower first step turns strangers into customers, who are far more likely to buy again; a fast win funds the bigger purchase.
- **Source.** [Building a $3,000,000 Business for a Stranger in 57 Mins](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=n6SHLmKcY0E&t=287s) (04:47)

##### Event planning and AV company · ~$44K average deal, ~1 deal a week · thin margins
- **Situation.** The owner closes about 40%; two reps underperform; line-item pricing is invented case by case.
- **Diagnosis.** Mispriced, ad hoc offer (a margin problem) plus a demand problem because the reps produce little.
- **Advice.** Build a cost-based quote generator at 5–10x cost with a price lock and a prepay discount. Turn the reps into setters for the owner, with daily check-ins and role play. VSLs at three touchpoints. Ask for multi-event commitments. Go after B2B conferences via booths and speaking. Max out LinkedIn outreach plus a dialer.
- **Why.** Don't pour demand into thin margins; doubling appointments for an owner who closes 40% changes the business fast. Follow-up: pipeline up about 200%, one-year clients converted to three years, one booth produced a roughly $500K deal.
- **Source.** [How to Get Rich for Service-Based Businesses (Thank Me Later)](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=mRlSb0O5QNU&t=908s) (15:08)

##### Music-industry law firm with side lines · ~$300K/yr, losing ~$100K · six months of cash
- **Situation.** Legal work at $250 an hour (about $1,500 per client), plus a $500 course and contract software burning about $7K a month. Large social audiences, but no clear call to action.
- **Diagnosis.** Demand is sprayed across three offers, hourly billing underprices the work, and the software loses most of the money.
- **Advice.** Kill or sell the software. Make education free to feed services. Route every call to action to DMs and qualify new followers. Sell an outcome package at about $5,800 plus a small share of deals, with a $25K anchor and a $2–3K DIY downsell, sold on a scripted call.
- **Why.** Services already earn; about 800 new followers a month at 5% close and $6K could mean roughly $240K a month.
- **Source.** [I Confronted a Stranger With a Failing Business...](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6Fg4VXjRphQ&t=0s) (00:00); [I Blew Up a Business Through 3 Small Changes (Just Copy Me)](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=3yAiVjcImQ4&t=0s) (00:00)

##### Luxury personal styling · ~$309K/yr, 42% margin · strong economics, low volume
- **Situation.** About 40 recurring clients, about one churn a year; $8.5K onboarding, then $2K a month. About 1,800 paid clicks to a form with no headline gave 8 sales. Six partners, found with about 40 cold emails, bring a third of revenue.
- **Diagnosis.** Conversion is the bottleneck, and the partner channel is barely tapped. No price anchor, continuity isn't the default.
- **Advice.** A style-assessment lead magnet, a short VSL and BANT questions; qualified leads get a call, the rest a freebie. Send 20 partner emails a day and hold partner events. Add a 10x top-tier anchor and default continuity after onboarding.
- **Why.** The product already converts on calls, so the cheap gains are upstream; partners earned about $2,500 per email sent, better than ads.
- **Source.** [I Blew Up A Secret Business To Prove It's Not Luck](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=SmiOK8Yun4s&t=47s) (00:47); [Building a $1,000,000 Business for a Stranger in 56 Mins](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=nrounb8NlFQ&t=479s) (07:59)

##### Agencies serving local businesses · $1,500/month retainer · ~0.5:1 return in the first 30 days
- **Situation.** The offer looks the same as every competitor's retainer.
- **Diagnosis.** A commoditised offer.
- **Advice.** Restructure it: one payment up front, the agency works the leads, then the client pays per person who shows up.
- **Why.** Response rate rose 2.5x, appointments went from 40 to 100, close rate from 16% to 37% at four times the price, and cash collected rose about 22x on the same ad spend.
- **Source.** [GROWTH LEVERS: Big Business Owner Explains Real World Tactics That Work](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=F3NyhOtRpOE&t=576s) (09:36)

##### New marketing agency for home-service SMBs · $0 to ~$500K in four months · goal eight figures
- **Situation.** About $2K a month per client, about ten sales a month.
- **Diagnosis.** The price sits in the small-business churn zone (clients stay four to six months on average). Growth will stall near $100K a month as churn catches new sales, CAC rises and margins shrink.
- **Advice.** Choose one: go much cheaper and automated ($300–500 a month with visible deliverables such as map rankings and review management), or move up-market to sophisticated clients.
- **Why.** Cheap, visible services can stick 30–40 months with low CAC; high-touch work only sticks with clients who already have a working model.
- **Source.** [Watch This If You Have a Service Business](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jqo0lVveh98&t=523s) (08:43)

##### Subscription service · ~$400K/month · 100 new sales a month, didn't know churn
- **Situation.** 380 clients at $1,000 a month, growing fast; churn and LTV untracked.
- **Diagnosis.** About 13% monthly churn caps the business near $770K a month; growth will flatten.
- **Advice.** Put the effort into cutting churn toward about 3% before adding acquisition.
- **Why.** At 3% churn LTV rises to about $33K and the ceiling to about $3.3M a month, which is far easier than 10x lead flow. At 13% a month, under 20% of clients remain after a year, and buyers won't value that.
- **Source.** ["My business has stopped growing..what should I do?"](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=f_ceDZqhKbw&t=96s) (01:36)

##### Website-as-a-service for SMBs · ~$20M/yr, ~$3.6M EBITDA · afraid AI will kill the product
- **Situation.** About $450 a month per client, 100% of growth from outbound cold calling, clients stay about 29 months.
- **Diagnosis.** The fear isn't in the metrics. The real risks are a single channel and a thin margin from being people-heavy.
- **Advice.** Don't rebuild the product. Add paid inbound. Spend about six months rebuilding workflows with AI and cut headcount by about half to roughly double margin, then accept a negative first quarter on CAC, knowing the 29-month back end.
- **Why.** Margin funds acquisition without touching price, and customers who would build their own site with AI were never buyers.
- **Source.** [Watch This If You Have a Service Business](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jqo0lVveh98&t=907s) (15:07)

##### CFO and tax advisory · ~$2.9M/yr · paused new sales, wants to sell courses
- **Situation.** Growing 30–35% a year on organic demand alone, but sales are paused because delivery is hard. The owner has made courses and books and wants to sell them.
- **Diagnosis.** Supply-constrained. A course business would be a new and equally hard business.
- **Advice.** Resume selling. Add offshore staff for two to three times leverage per person. Build a data layer, then AI. Keep courses and books as future marketing assets.
- **Why.** Zero new sales guarantees zero growth; fix the constraint you have before creating demand you can't serve.
- **Source.** [Watch This If You Have a Service Business](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jqo0lVveh98&t=1284s) (21:24)

##### Virtual CPA firm · ~$3M/yr, ~$1.1M net · ~70% annual retention
- **Situation.** Quarterly fees from about $1,950. Departing clients say they have learned the strategies.
- **Diagnosis.** Bad onboarding and too little communication; value isn't really being lost.
- **Advice.** Audit the handoff from sales to onboarding, keep notes on each client, communicate often about the work done and train accountants on client touchpoints. Then hire a top tax specialist.
- **Why.** He targets retention above 80%; visible care signals competent work.
- **Source.** [7 Millionaires Asked Me How to Get Rich(er)](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=bgBIO6nZawg&t=1014s) (16:54)

##### Enterprise software-implementation consultancy · ~$1.4M/yr · thinking of starting over
- **Situation.** About 12 clients, almost all from vendor referrals as a subcontractor: capped hourly rate, about 17% margin against about 83% when selling direct. Considering a new healthcare business with a spouse.
- **Diagnosis.** Fuzzy avatar, no owned acquisition channel, underpriced for very large clients. The real risk is split focus, and the owner mostly wants permission.
- **Advice.** Commit fully to the new venture (the spouse could run the old one) or stay. Plan which work to remove from the old business before deciding. If staying: add a direct acquisition channel and raise enterprise prices.
- **Why.** Straddling two businesses makes the new one grow far slower.
- **Source.** ["Should He Walk Away From His $1.4M Business To Start Over?" | Alex Hormozi Answers Live](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0RjIw9M1j28&t=47s) (00:47)

##### Remote real-estate transaction coordination · ~$1M/yr · 70% of new business from the owner's networking
- **Situation.** Thin cash after a seasonal reserve; 73 of 87 appointments closed.
- **Diagnosis.** No leverage in acquisition, and an 84% close rate that points to underpricing.
- **Advice.** Pull top agents by sales volume from the listings database and run outbound (call, email, text) into small-group presentations. Later, local ads by city radius with high production thresholds. Raise prices.
- **Why.** Outbound is cheaper and safer with thin cash; ads will lose money while they learn.
- **Source.** ["He Makes $1.5M a Year... and Keeps 1.3% of It" | Alex Hormozi Answers Live](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=lidFtb1Ga0o&t=5507s) (91:47)

##### Offshore content-editor placement · ~$800K/yr · all clients from referrals
- **Situation.** $6.5K placement fee plus $2.5K a month; about 3% annual churn.
- **Diagnosis.** A pure demand problem; retention is excellent.
- **Advice.** Use the in-house team to make content about content (the process up to the sale) and turn the best pieces into ads. Partner with communities where the avatar gathers. Possibly raise prices and grandfather referrers; set a three-month minimum.
- **Why.** The content doubles as ad creative, and a couple of community partners could triple the business.
- **Source.** ["He Makes $1.5M a Year... and Keeps 1.3% of It" | Alex Hormozi Answers Live](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=lidFtb1Ga0o&t=3856s) (64:16)

##### Lead and sales service for new real-estate wholesalers · CAC ~$5K, LTV ~$35K · very low close rate
- **Situation.** Many prospects can't afford the first payment.
- **Diagnosis.** Unqualified lead flow drains the sales team and the close rate.
- **Advice.** Define the avatar from the highest-spending, longest-staying customers (demographics, quantifiables such as income, geography, behaviour in the sales process). Put that friction into ads, landing pages, form drop-downs and the VSL. Send the best leads to sales and the rest to an automated self-liquidating offer.
- **Why.** Qualification raises cost per call but lowers CAC and raises LTV. He couldn't recall a case where it lost him money, and selling to people spending their last dollar hurts ethics, morale and reputation.
- **Source.** [How to Build a Business That Runs Without You](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=TLpLmhWqKEU&t=3357s) (55:57)

##### B2B licensing for gym owners (Alex's own) · 100+ new gyms a month · poor-fit clients
- **Situation.** Non-owners, part-time owners, owners without a signed lease and owners with no staff took the most team time and got the worst results.
- **Diagnosis.** No minimum client standard.
- **Advice.** Require at least 25 members, a signed lease, a full-time owner and ideally one employee. Return to a programme with a defined end and market average results.
- **Why.** Better-fit clients pay more, earn more, need less work and stay longer. Sales fell from 100+ to 40–50 a month, and escalations went from 22 to zero in four weeks.
- **Source.** [Pricing Lessons From a Private Equity God](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=hnfh2jqNVEQ&t=336s) (05:36)

##### Lead-gen and sales coaching agency for financial advisers · ~$6.6M/yr · churn is the main problem
- **Situation.** Paid ads to a VSL to a phone team. Goal: $20M+.
- **Diagnosis.** Activated clients stick, so this is an activation and avatar problem.
- **Advice.** Find the segments (demographics, quantifiables, behaviours) that activate and sell only to them, possibly adjusting price. Run daily group role play for clients' teams. Reverse-engineer activation and deliver an early win.
- **Why.** Bad-fit clients can't be helped however hard you try; he saw the same with gym owners too small to pay.
- **Source.** [Helping 4 Educational Business Owners Build a $1M Business in 25 Minutes](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=BYpTRiRqS1Y&t=1106s) (18:26)

##### DM-funnel agency for creators · clients churn in 3–5 months
- **Situation.** Clients who hit a record month stay; the rest leave.
- **Diagnosis.** The first 30 days decide retention, and client selection is too loose.
- **Advice.** Require prior selling experience, make the offer more turnkey, set expectations up front, then scale outbound volume.
- **Why.** Fewer variables to get wrong; churn is mostly about getting clients to month six.
- **Source.** ["He Makes $1.5M a Year... and Keeps 1.3% of It" | Alex Hormozi Answers Live](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=lidFtb1Ga0o&t=5016s) (83:36)

##### Wholesale deal business · deals fell from ~70 to ~40 a month · top people left
- **Situation.** The sales manager and second-best closer left; the top closer was promoted to manager and coaches poorly. The owner wants to hire a sales director.
- **Diagnosis.** The founder doesn't know sales, nothing is enforced, and the wrong personality was promoted.
- **Advice.** The owner gets into sales personally: takes calls, simplifies the script, enforces it for everyone, and trains through call review and drills.
- **Why.** A sales-led company whose CEO can't run sales is held hostage by whoever does.
- **Source.** [10 Millionaires Asked Me How to Get Richer](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6m6DCQMASEM&t=781s) (13:01)

##### Solo paid-ads agency, regulated niche · ~$90K/yr profit · anxious about the first hire
- **Situation.** One person serving a hard-to-advertise niche.
- **Diagnosis.** A broken model; people aren't the issue.
- **Advice.** Either charge ten times more per client or become ten times more efficient in delivery before thinking about hiring.
- **Why.** There is no margin to hire from, and scaling the current model would scale the problem.
- **Source.** [10 Millionaires Asked Me How to Get Richer](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6m6DCQMASEM&t=2422s) (40:22)

##### B2B licensing for gym owners (Alex's own) · ~$1M a week · founders want out
- **Situation.** 100% of sales from paid social, and the founder was the face of every ad.
- **Diagnosis.** A personal brand more than a business: founder dependency, one channel and churn would cap any exit price.
- **Advice.** Hire someone from a competitor that did outbound and have him build an outbound department from scratch; persist through slow early months as long as each funnel stage improves. Take the founder off the door: specialists, former clients as product leads, new faces in ads, ads handed to the general manager.
- **Why.** Within about a year outbound was roughly half of revenue, which reassured the acquirer; the business also became worth keeping.
- **Source.** [How I Scaled My Sales Team \[Compensation, Recruiting & Training\]](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=okA9Yt2KZuk&t=139s) (02:19); [I Reverse Engineered the Perfect Business](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=HKbFUWJwEG0&t=1297s) (21:37)

##### Consultant to accounting firms · wants a sellable business
- **Situation.** Nobody buys a consulting practice like this one.
- **Diagnosis.** Buyers do buy accounting firms, especially in aggregate.
- **Advice.** Assemble roll-ups of 10–20 client firms every year or two and take a slice for arranging the deal.
- **Why.** Volume earns a premium: firms that sell at about 4x alone may sell at about 12x together, so even after giving up 30% they roughly double.
- **Source.** [How to Build a Valuable Company You Can Sell Someday](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=VxKwz6hBVZU&t=812s) (13:32)

##### Done-for-you marketing for medical clinics · ~$5M/yr · serves ~160 of ~2,000 clinics
- **Situation.** A healthy agency with a small share of a defined niche.
- **Diagnosis.** It captures a small fee from clients who together generate far more revenue.
- **Advice.** Consider rolling up 10–20 clients into a national group for a large exit, taking 20–30%.
- **Why.** Consolidation lifts the multiple, and being the one who did it changes pricing and positioning.
- **Source.** [7 Millionaires Asked Me How to Get Rich(er)](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=bgBIO6nZawg&t=0s) (00:00)

##### Architecture firm · asking how to measure revenue retention
- **Situation.** Projects are one-off, so client retention looks naturally low.
- **Diagnosis.** Thinking at the project level.
- **Advice.** Measure retention one level up, at the referral partner (contractors and others who send repeat work), and build acquisition around winning partners, each with a known value and cost.
- **Why.** The same happened in his software company: agencies stayed even though their end clients churned.
- **Source.** [How to Build a Business That Runs Without You](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=TLpLmhWqKEU&t=3839s) (63:59)

##### Insurance agency, 29 agents · owner as the face or more recruits
- **Situation.** Most leads come from referrals and networking, not from the owner's content.
- **Diagnosis.** Demand-constrained.
- **Advice.** Recruit agents and build training so they generate their own leads. Alternate content calls to action between buyers and recruits.
- **Why.** Lead getters compound on the same owner effort.
- **Source.** [$100M Investor Answers The Most Important Business Questions for 1 Hour](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=--9kOXNCUdE&t=2753s) (45:53)

##### Paid-ads agency · wants clients spending $100K+ a month · all business from referrals
- **Situation.** No owned acquisition; growth depends on who happens to refer.
- **Diagnosis.** Waiting for the right clients instead of going after them.
- **Advice.** Outbound to high-value targets, niche content, and their own ads with blunt call-outs that exclude small spenders, plus LinkedIn. Build a consulting phase into contracts for when clients take ads in-house.
- **Why.** You have to go and get the customer you want.
- **Source.** [10 Millionaires Asked Me How to Get Richer](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6m6DCQMASEM&t=3099s) (51:39)

##### Enterprise web development agency · two clients are ~90% of revenue · outbound failing
- **Situation.** Two 50/50 co-founders both doing client work.
- **Diagnosis.** A partner alignment problem: the partner who owns sales isn't doing the activity. Skill isn't the issue.
- **Advice.** Have a direct, unsoftened conversation that separates criticism (the gap between what was said and what was done) from insult. Agree whether both want growth; if not, split responsibilities or work on separate things.
- **Why.** When they lost a big client earlier they replaced it quickly, so the capability exists.
- **Source.** [10 Millionaires Asked Me How to Get Richer](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6m6DCQMASEM&t=1702s) (28:22)

##### Niche lead-gen agency plus software · ~$2M + ~$0.55M/yr · founder unmotivated
- **Situation.** About 15–20% share of a small market, goal $5M in two years. Operations are delegated, but the founder can't make himself do maker work.
- **Diagnosis.** Not real burnout: the work no longer rewards him and the stakes are too low. A hunter stuck farming.
- **Advice.** Raise the goal and shorten the timeline, hire a leader to run day-to-day, and go back to selling and finding bigger deals. Don't worry about the main ad channel changing; move budget gradually when needed.
- **Why.** Burnout comes when reward is delayed or no longer satisfies; 20–30% growth changes nothing in his life, 300% would.
- **Source.** ["Should He Walk Away From His $1.4M Business To Start Over?" | Alex Hormozi Answers Live](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0RjIw9M1j28&t=1198s) (19:58)

##### Music law firm, later stage · ~$100K/month and growing · choosing between units
- **Situation.** Law firm growing, education flat at about $200K, contract software alongside.
- **Diagnosis.** Two avatars, large labels and semi-professional musicians. About 40% of software users still paying after a year signals fit with the smaller buyers.
- **Advice.** Go downmarket, use education to offset CAC, and focus everything on lifting month-12 retention from 40% to 60–70%.
- **Why.** Getting these buyers is easy for them, keeping them is the hard part, and retention is what makes the model compound.
- **Source.** [$100M Investor Answers The Most Important Business Questions for 1 Hour](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=--9kOXNCUdE&t=2467s) (41:07)

##### House-flipping operation · ~$15M sales, ~$2.5M after team · 95% of deals from foreclosures
- **Situation.** Dominates a four-hour driving radius; no advertising at all.
- **Diagnosis.** No control over deal flow.
- **Advice.** Start advertising (search, mail, social) in the current market, which might double it. Study the largest national buyer and copy and improve their model before expanding.
- **Why.** The model is proven with eight years of learning; changing the business would reset the curve.
- **Source.** [7 Millionaires Asked Me How to Get Rich(er)](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=bgBIO6nZawg&t=628s) (10:28)

##### Done-for-you photo booths in venues · ~$1M/yr, 80 booths · cash-constrained
- **Situation.** About $950 gross per booth a month, about 17-month payback, goal $3M.
- **Diagnosis.** A financing problem: depreciating machines bought with cash.
- **Advice.** Test leasing from the manufacturer instead of buying; otherwise sell a small stake for capital.
- **Why.** The value is the spread from placing booths, not owning a depreciating asset.
- **Source.** ["He Makes $1.5M a Year... and Keeps 1.3% of It" | Alex Hormozi Answers Live](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=lidFtb1Ga0o&t=2360s) (39:20)

##### Bleacher rental · ~$4.5M/yr, ~$1M profit · leaders don't delegate
- **Situation.** The leadership layer holds on to work.
- **Diagnosis.** Vague expectations and weak training.
- **Advice.** Hire for the smallest skill gap; define the behaviours you want; train by document, demonstrate, duplicate; give stop/start/keep feedback.
- **Why.** People can't change behaviour that is described only in abstract words.
- **Source.** [Answering Your Top Business Questions for 1 Hour](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=QwEb78e5a8Y&t=769s) (12:49)

##### Holding company, 14 unrelated businesses · ~$35M combined revenue · goal ~$50M value
- **Situation.** Owns a scattered portfolio with no common thread.
- **Diagnosis.** Stuck between a concentrated strategy and a roll-up, committed to neither.
- **Advice.** Decide which day-to-day work you prefer. Then either 80/20 the portfolio and double down on the best six or so, or go vertical with real synergies.
- **Why.** Either path reaches the goal; the lack of focus is the problem.
- **Source.** [Answering Your Top Business Questions for 1 Hour](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=QwEb78e5a8Y&t=1292s) (21:32)

##### Media company with a talent roster · wants to be sellable
- **Situation.** Its stars generate large audiences.
- **Diagnosis.** Stars produce impressions, and impressions can't be sold.
- **Advice.** Route the impressions into owned products (an incubator of products) or sell ad inventory; raise a small stake to fund launches.
- **Why.** The audience is the goose; owned products are the eggs a buyer will pay for.
- **Source.** [How to Build a Valuable Company You Can Sell Someday](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=VxKwz6hBVZU&t=621s) (10:21)

##### Labour-intensive service business, 19-year-old owner · ~$20K/month profit · ~$700K saved
- **Situation.** Lost a $100K/month-profit software business to a platform change; lives on $2–3K a month.
- **Diagnosis.** Talent wasted on a small service business and thinking only one step ahead.
- **Advice.** Pick a target of $2–10M a month, check which business could actually support it (market size, churn, acquisition) and go all in, living on savings rather than the service income.
- **Why.** Very low downside at that age with no dependants; the savings exist to make bets.
- **Source.** ["Should He Walk Away From His $1.4M Business To Start Over?" | Alex Hormozi Answers Live](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0RjIw9M1j28&t=4544s) (75:44)

##### Agency selling ~$50K done-for-you packages · financing earns more than the service
- **Situation.** Arranging customer financing made more money than delivering the work.
- **Diagnosis.** The money was in the financing.
- **Advice.** The owner switched to business lending and made financing the business.
- **Why.** Follow the easy cash flow. Look at where the profit actually comes from before deciding what business you are in, because it is often a different one from the one you started.
- **Source.** [Your Business Is NOT What You Think It Is](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=rj7rzOdj84A&t=2066s) (34:26)

##### Resume-improvement service · strong process · the business failed
- **Situation.** A friend's service for unemployed people: real pain, easy to target, a growing market.
- **Diagnosis.** The market couldn't pay.
- **Advice.** A lesson rather than a fix: choose a market that can afford you.
- **Why.** A good process can't overcome customers without money.
- **Source.** [GROWTH LEVERS: Big Business Owner Explains Real World Tactics That Work](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=F3NyhOtRpOE&t=1153s) (19:13)

#### Patterns

- **Underpriced, hourly or cost-plus (about 9 cases).** Hourly legal work, invented line items, capped subcontractor rates, close rates of 84–100%. He moves to an outcome price, often a fixed fee plus a performance share, with a high anchor and a cheaper downsell.
- **Acquisition depends on something the owner doesn't control (about 9 cases).** Vendor referrals, a few partners, the founder's network, one ad platform. He adds an owned channel, usually outbound to a defined list, and keeps scaling what already works.
- **Partners and affiliates under-used (about 6 cases).** Referral partners bringing a third of revenue from a few emails, community partners, partner-level retention. He scales partner outreach, gives partners an offer that changes behaviour, and measures value per partner.
- **Churn from the wrong clients and a weak start (about 7 cases).** Agencies losing clients in months three to six, CPA clients leaving at 70% retention, a subscription at 13% monthly churn. He tightens the avatar, adds qualification, engineers an early win and communicates the work done.
- **Offer mismatch with cold traffic (about 4 cases).** High-ticket offers pushed at strangers, commodity retainers. He lowers the first step (audit, front-end offer, pay per show) and ascends later.
- **Price in the churn zone (2–3 cases).** Mid-priced SMB services churn fast; he says go cheap and automated with visible deliverables, or go up-market.
- **Founder or partner as the sales problem (about 4 cases).** A founder who doesn't know sales, a partner who doesn't sell, an unmotivated hunter stuck farming. He puts the owner back into selling or forces the partner conversation.
- **Sellability (about 5 cases).** Consulting and talent businesses are hard to sell; he points to recurring revenue, multiple channels, no founder dependency, or rolling up the clients.
- **Supply before demand (2–3 cases).** Paused sales or a solo operator without margin; he fixes delivery leverage or price before adding leads.

#### How Alex would apply it

**First questions.**
1. How do you price: hourly, cost-plus, or by outcome? What is your close rate?
2. Where does each new client come from, and how much of it do you control?
3. How many clients reach month six, and what do the ones who stay have in common?
4. What happens in the first 30 days, and when does the client see a visible win?
5. If you sold tomorrow, what would a buyer actually be paying for?

**Default moves by situation.**
- **Close rate very high, hourly billing:** repackage as an outcome with a fixed fee, add a performance component, an anchor tier above and a downsell below; accept a lower close rate.
- **All work from referrals or one partner:** build outbound to a defined list (top performers in the target market, big spenders), and run a partner programme with an offer partners care about.
- **Churn in months three to six:** define the avatar from your best long-stay clients, qualify on the form and the call, set expectations up front, make onboarding personal and show the results regularly.
- **Selling high-ticket to cold traffic:** add a cheaper front-end step (audit, assessment, pay per show) and roll its value into the main offer.
- **Too many offers or units:** keep the one with demand and margin, turn the others into marketing assets or close them.
- **Delivery capacity full:** raise price first, add offshore staff and AI to the workflow, and keep selling.
- **Planning an exit:** add a second channel, remove the founder from the front, raise retention, or roll up the clients you serve.

## Cases: E-commerce & Products

- id: cases-products
- book: Alex's Playbook / Casebook
- original term: Hotseat cases
- key: Most product owners have a pricing, focus or brand problem dressed up as a traffic problem; fix margin and the back end, then spend harder on the channel that already works.
- related: focus-and-subtraction, pricing-decisions, retention-and-ltv, enterprise-value, paid-ads

Real hotseat situations from e-commerce brands, DTC labels, consumer goods and physical-product makers, with what Alex diagnosed and told the owner to do.

#### The gist

Product owners usually arrive asking for more traffic or a new line. In most of these cases Alex finds the traffic is fine or under-tested, and the real gaps sit elsewhere: prices set by cost-plus habit, too many products or avatars competing for the same attention, no email or repeat-purchase back end, broken attribution, and a business that buys clicks profitably without ever becoming a brand anyone would pay a premium for or acquire.

His prescriptions follow a steady order. First subtract: cut the losing location, the side SKUs, the new venture that is really an escape. Then fix money per customer: test higher prices, add repeat-purchase offers and email flows, and make the most profitable segment easy to buy from. Only then scale acquisition, and when he does, he pushes owners to spend more and test more than feels comfortable on the channel that already returns well, treating profit as an experiment budget.

Two longer-term themes recur. Unbranded direct-response winners get copied and margins collapse as spend rises, so he steers owners with traction toward one product they believe in and a real brand, often built through creators and affiliates. And when an owner wants to bolt on something new (a SaaS, a character universe, an acquisition), he asks whether the existing engine is exhausted and who carries the risk; usually the answer is to keep the engine running and fund the new thing from its profits, or not do it at all.

#### Cases

##### Hair-extension wholesaler with a salon · ~$3.5M/yr combined · wants to build salon-booking software

- **Situation.** Extensions wholesale and e-commerce at about $2.6M and 30% margin, growing fast, plus a salon at about $900k and 50% margin. The owner planned to spend about $500k building booking software for salons.
- **Diagnosis.** An escape from solvable problems (margin and acquisition) into a much harder business. Extensions are already a repeat purchase.
- **Advice.** Do not build the software. Improve margins and acquisition in the core: content, ads aimed at stylists, a front-end offer that helps salons earn more by selling extensions, and separate hunter and farmer reps.
- **Why.** Software means years without profit against focused, funded competitors; the current business is cash-positive and calmer.
- **Source.** [Helping E-Commerce Business Owners Scale](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8C_6qojTA78&t=1151s) (19:11)

##### Custom trophy and awards manufacturer · ~$1.7M/yr, 6% net · cost-plus pricing and no focus

- **Situation.** Multi-generation maker with 40% gross margin, about $110k profit, two locations (one barely breaking even), and sales spread across its own site, SEO, marketplaces and a viral gag-gift line on a video-shopping platform.
- **Diagnosis.** Mispricing, two very different avatars (price-sensitive repeat B2B buyers and impulse gift buyers) and no email back end.
- **Advice.** Close the losing location. Move to value-based pricing with regular tests, starting on gag gifts. Max out SEO and add paid search, including brand terms. Commit to the gag-gift media path with one to three posts a day, affiliates and ads behind organic winners. Add an in-box repeat offer, email flows and a weekly nurture.
- **Why.** At thin margins a ~6% price rise can roughly double profit, and e-commerce profit comes from repeat purchases.
- **Source.** [Building a $3,500,000 Business for a Stranger in 51 Mins](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=BMF2fWHyDrg&t=1051s) (17:31)

##### E-commerce railing maker · ~$2.5M/yr, 15% net · attribution broken, best segment under-monetised

- **Situation.** About $384k profit, 81% of customers from Google, about 70% DIY buyers and 30% custom, around 10% repeat. Reported LTV:CAC was 1:1 while dashboards showed 0.41.
- **Diagnosis.** With attribution broken nothing can be optimised. The least price-sensitive segment was under-monetised and the custom option was hidden.
- **Advice.** Fix attribution first. Reorganise around DIY plus custom, build a call funnel for custom orders with a VSL and qualifying texts, raise prices, show the custom option on every page and send email nurture twice a week.
- **Why.** Doubling close rate and margin per customer compounds. A year later revenue was reported up 44%, custom orders were half the business and budget had moved from break-even campaigns to winners.
- **Source.** [Building a $1,000,000 Business for a Stranger in 26 Minutes](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=HGZOxBfnF-E&t=679s) (11:19)

##### Luxury gaming-table maker · ~$2.5M/yr, 20% margin · extra ad spend brought worse leads

- **Situation.** Google Ads at $15–20k a month; the owner wanted 4x growth, but raising spend lowered lead quality.
- **Diagnosis.** The market was far from saturated. The real limits were keyword and media-buying skill and a fear of spending; ads only reached the most aware buyers.
- **Advice.** Treat profit as an experiment budget and accept losing a few hundred thousand while testing keywords to find more winners. Move up in awareness with broader keywords and advertorial bridge pages.
- **Why.** The keywords already found returned around 10:1, so more winners likely exist, and colder clicks are cheaper and far more numerous.
- **Source.** [Helping E-Commerce Business Owners Scale](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8C_6qojTA78&t=96s) (01:36)

##### Direct-response e-commerce operator · ~$3M run rate after four months · four unbranded SKUs, founder buried in chores

- **Situation.** Four products under separate brands, aiming for $15M and a portfolio exit. The founder was overwhelmed by small tasks.
- **Diagnosis.** The chores are a complaint; the constraint lies elsewhere. The model itself caps out: pure media arbitrage tends to stall near $10M with collapsing margins and nothing a buyer wants.
- **Advice.** Hand niche tasks to contractors and assistants. Pick the one product the team truly believes in, cut the rest, and build a brand through influencer-affiliates before worrying about legal protection.
- **Why.** He calls it the direct-response doom loop: revenue up, margins down, team becoming a liability. Buyers pay for brands, and copycats kill unbranded winners.
- **Source.** [Helping E-Commerce Business Owners Scale](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8C_6qojTA78&t=578s) (09:38)

##### DTC menswear brand · ~$10M/yr, ~$3M profit · considering womenswear and formal lines

- **Situation.** Contract-manufactured fashion brand in a large Asian market, strong at buying traffic, thinking about new categories.
- **Diagnosis.** Excellent at media arbitrage, but without a brand.
- **Advice.** Stay in menswear and aim for $100M there. Build a real brand, for example around an endorser, instead of imitating fast fashion.
- **Why.** A brand creates premium pricing and makes the company saleable; new categories would split focus before the first one is exhausted.
- **Source.** [7 Millionaires Asked Me How to Get Rich(er)](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=bgBIO6nZawg&t=1833s) (30:33)

##### Live-shopping reseller of designer accessories · ~$6M/yr · wants ~$38M

- **Situation.** Sells designer bags and sunglasses on a live-shopping app, limited to about five live hours a day, runs its own fulfilment and keeps hitting cash crunches.
- **Diagnosis.** The constraint is on-camera sellers. The warehouse is a commodity, and cash runs short because all gross profit goes into inventory.
- **Advice.** Add two more on-camera sellers paid a share of profit, recruited from creators already live-selling and signed to exclusivity. Hand the warehouse to a capable leader, hire a finance person for forecasting, train sellers in concrete behaviours and keep the star seller selling.
- **Why.** Each show is profitable, so more selling hours scale revenue directly; the founder's leverage is teaching others to sell.
- **Source.** [Helping E-Commerce Business Owners Scale](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8C_6qojTA78&t=1728s) (28:48)

##### Solo board-game publisher · $1.3M from one crowdfunded game · four new games started before the first shipped

- **Situation.** About $150k cash left, another $150k already spent developing four more games, the first game not yet delivered, and the owner overwhelmed.
- **Diagnosis.** Self-made complexity. Starting four games before finishing one removed priorities and created the anxiety.
- **Advice.** Raise cash by offering past buyers a buy-one-get-one deal on upcoming games. Make delivering game one the only objective. Hire an assistant. Learn ads with a fixed learning budget split roughly 70/20/10.
- **Why.** Feedback from the first delivery will change the later games, and the business itself is simple: ship a box.
- **Source.** ["Should He Walk Away From His $1.4M Business To Start Over?" | Alex Hormozi Answers Live](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0RjIw9M1j28&t=2389s) (39:49)

##### Premium canned-cocktail brand · ~$1.5M/yr, zero profit · wants a billion-dollar brand

- **Situation.** In about 1,000 stores with weak sell-through, founder alone after letting staff go, no branding or direct-response experience. She also ran a proven property-development business on the side.
- **Diagnosis.** No repeat-purchase data, no capital for a celebrity-style brand play and no appetite for a 10–15 year regional grind.
- **Advice.** One last focused bet: go all in on travel retail contracts (airlines, trains, cruise lines), where a small can is an advantage. If no contract closes, shut it down. Otherwise put her time into property and learn to hire and train people.
- **Why.** It is the only path that needs no capital and fits her skills; learning fast beats losing three more years.
- **Source.** [Your Business Is NOT What You Think It Is](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=rj7rzOdj84A&t=1488s) (24:48)

##### Robotic vending-machine seller · ~$15k machines at ~50% margin · outbound works but is tiny

- **Situation.** Entrepreneurs want the machines but lack locations. Cold email gets two or three replies per 100 and nearly all of them close. Four closers, one setter; paid ads had not worked.
- **Diagnosis.** A validated, very profitable outbound motion that has never been scaled.
- **Advice.** Go from about 100 to about 5,000 emails a day using several warmed domains, AI personalisation and automated sends in reps' names. Route replies straight to closers, who also keep prospecting. Consider SDRs or AI dialers.
- **Why.** The unit economics are extreme. Once a process works, the limit is volume and headcount.
- **Source.** [How to Build a Business That Runs Without You](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=TLpLmhWqKEU&t=5998s) (1:39:58)

##### Licensed-drop apparel brand · ~$6M/yr, ~10% net · conversion swings 5–25% by license

- **Situation.** Drops with TV and anime licenses, all traffic from paid social to waitlists; the owner wanted a 4–5x EBITDA exit.
- **Diagnosis.** No predictive data on which license will perform. The exit thesis is weak: large acquirers rarely write cheques this small, and the model is easy to copy.
- **Advice.** Build quantitative predictors of drop performance. Create an ambassador program of niche creators who push every drop and get drops of their own, and use their pull data to choose license partners.
- **Why.** Creators bring audiences without ad spend, negotiate softly and give early signal on who really moves product.
- **Source.** [How to Build a Business That Runs Without You](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=TLpLmhWqKEU&t=4082s) (1:08:02)

##### E-commerce clothing brand with a community · size not stated · apparel or an original character universe?

- **Situation.** A brand with about 14,000 community members, torn between scaling apparel and building an original character universe.
- **Diagnosis.** A false either/or.
- **Advice.** Keep the clothing engine running. Fund the universe from its profits and treat it as marketing. Measure whether it brings more buyers, and study how large studios monetise characters.
- **Why.** Building intellectual property costs capital. Someone's money has to be at risk, and ideally it comes from your own working engine.
- **Source.** [10 Millionaires Asked Me How to Get Richer](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6m6DCQMASEM&t=1991s) (33:11)

##### Custom railings shipped nationwide · ~$420k/yr · wants architects and contractors to refer big projects

- **Situation.** Inbound comes through SEO. The owner wanted trade partners to send projects of $50k and more.
- **Diagnosis.** Hunting large B2B deals is a strategy change with real risk, and partners need a compelling reason to refer.
- **Advice.** Start with the lowest-risk moves: add paid search and more SEO articles. For partners, use direct outreach and ideally let them resell and keep the margin; otherwise offer a pool (for example 20%) split between partner cut and client discount. Raise direct prices.
- **Why.** Generic kickbacks do not change behaviour; what you give away simply becomes your acquisition cost.
- **Source.** [$100M Investor Answers The Most Important Business Questions for 1 Hour](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=--9kOXNCUdE&t=1018s) (16:58)

##### Trophy manufacturer eyeing an acquisition · target ~2x its size · 70% of target revenue from one customer

- **Situation.** The same awards maker considered buying a local competitor about twice its revenue, whose owner was retiring.
- **Diagnosis.** Key-person risk from the retiring owner on top of severe customer concentration, and no clear strategic reason for the deal.
- **Advice.** Do not buy. If the supplier relationships are the attraction, try to get them another way.
- **Why.** If the retiring owner holds the big relationship, the buyer could lose most of the revenue the day the deal closes.
- **Source.** [Building a $3,500,000 Business for a Stranger in 51 Mins](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=BMF2fWHyDrg&t=813s) (13:33)

#### Patterns

- **Subtract before adding (about 7 cases).** Losing location, extra SKUs, unfinished games, new categories, a SaaS escape: his first move is usually to cut or refuse something.
- **The new venture is an escape (about 4 cases).** Software, womenswear, a character universe, an acquisition. He checks whether the core is exhausted; it rarely is.
- **Prices set by habit (about 4 cases).** Cost-plus or below-market pricing; he prescribes value-based tests and direct price rises, noting that small increases move thin-margin profit a lot.
- **Missing back end (about 4 cases).** No email flows, no repeat offer, past buyers never re-sold. E-commerce profit comes from the second and third purchase.
- **Under-spending and under-testing the winning channel (about 4 cases).** Paid search at 10:1 that the owner is afraid to push, PPC never tried, outbound left at 100 emails a day.
- **Media arbitrage without a brand (about 3 cases).** Profitable click-buying that caps out, gets copied and is hard to sell. Pick one product and build a brand, often through creators.
- **Creators and partners as a channel (about 4 cases).** Influencer-affiliates, ambassador programs, trade partners who resell; give partners something they can earn on.
- **Data before optimisation (about 2 cases).** Broken attribution or no predictive model means spend cannot be steered.
- **Cash and inventory traps (about 3 cases).** Gross profit locked in stock or prototypes; he raises cash from past buyers and refocuses on shipping.

#### How Alex would apply it

**First questions.**
1. What are gross margin and net profit, and how were prices set?
2. What share of customers buy again, and what do you send them after the first order?
3. Which single channel and product make most of the profit, and can you prove it with attribution?
4. What happens to returns if you double spend on the best channel?
5. Is this a brand people would pay more for, or a product you sell because the ads work?
6. What new thing are you about to start, and why now?

**Default moves by situation.**
- **Margin under ~10% or cost-plus pricing:** run price tests first, starting with the least price-sensitive line; close or cut anything losing money.
- **No repeat purchases:** add in-box offers, email flows and a weekly nurture before buying more traffic.
- **A channel returns well but growth stalls:** raise spend and widen targeting deliberately, with profit set aside as a learning budget; move into less-aware audiences with broader terms and bridge content.
- **Attribution broken:** fix it before any other optimisation.
- **Several SKUs or brands on media arbitrage:** keep the one the team believes in and build a brand around it, using creators or affiliates.
- **Wanting a new line, a SaaS or an acquisition:** default answer is no. If the itch is real, keep the core engine running and fund the new thing from its profits as a marketing bet; refuse acquisitions with key-person risk or one-customer concentration.
- **Cash squeeze:** sell to past buyers first (pre-orders, bundles), narrow to delivering the current product, and outsource or lease what is commoditised.
- **A validated outbound or selling motion:** scale its volume and headcount before changing strategy.

## Cases: Education, Coaching & Software

- id: cases-education-software
- book: Alex's Playbook / Casebook
- original term: Hotseat cases
- key: Education is transactional, so growth equals acquisition; raise value per customer and fix activation before chasing a new avatar, product or platform.
- related: retention-and-ltv, pricing-decisions, focus-and-subtraction, continuity-offers, enterprise-value

Real hotseat situations from coaches, course and community businesses, event and creator brands, and software companies, with what Alex diagnosed and told the owner to do.

#### The gist

Coaching, course and creator businesses tend to bring Alex the same three complaints: ads stopped scaling, customers churn, and the founder has run out of hours. Underneath, he usually finds that the owner wants to switch something big (platform, avatar, B2B, a new product) when the cheaper fix is inside the current funnel. Creative volume is too low, the weakest funnel step has never been tested, value per customer is too low to absorb rising acquisition costs, and the back end is sold too late or not at all. Many founders also over-deliver: Slack access, long unstructured calls and group sessions nobody attends, which caps capacity without raising retention.

His prescriptions are consistent. Keep the channel that works and feed it far more creative. Raise lifetime value first, through upsells, renewal pitches timed to milestones and events, annual prepay, performance components or a diagnostic sale of the outcome. Fix churn at the start: qualify for the customers who activate, onboard one-on-one, and deliver an early win. On price he moves both ways, but he warns hard against cutting price to fix churn, citing his own costly mistakes. When a founder is the bottleneck, he adds structure (a scripted webinar, a self-serve checkout, clipped content) before adding people.

Software and sellability form a separate thread. For software he treats retention as the whole game: find the cohort that stays, then grow. For education businesses that want an exit, he notes that founder-led education is hard to sell, and points owners to the sellable assets their students or audiences create.

#### Cases

##### Couples-coaching program · ~$480k/yr, ~$206k profit · founders at capacity, challenge attendance falling

- **Situation.** Two 12-month offers ($5k group, $25k high-touch with chat access), a five-day challenge funnel on paid social, slow payback.
- **Diagnosis.** A supply constraint caused by over-delivery (chat access, custom plans, group calls nobody valued). The cheap tier was built instead of fixing capacity, and event renewals failed because nobody pitched.
- **Advice.** One ~$25k offer: deep one-on-one onboarding, quarterly calls in groups of four, two or three in-person getaways, a curated community. Kill chat access and weekly calls; sunset the $5k tier. Collect ~$9k up front with layaway as a downsell. Test a short date-night event in place of the challenge, run ~100 image ads, and pitch renewals in person with a fast-action incentive.
- **Why.** Capacity rises from ~60 to 120–150 clients without hiring; the first payment drives conversion more than total price.
- **Source.** [If I Wanted To Scale A Service Business In 2026, Here's What I'd Do](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=N5MExtki_VI&t=281s) (04:41)

##### Online program for parents of neurodivergent children · ~$770k/yr, ~$555k profit · founder spends ~30 hours a week in DMs

- **Situation.** $2,500/year offer, 9–10% conversion of live attendees, 3–4 hour unstructured live sessions, selling only to a warm audience.
- **Diagnosis.** The founder is the bottleneck, held back by beliefs that selling and higher prices are bad; the one-on-one tier is mispriced.
- **Advice.** $2,500 per six months, recurring. A structured 45-minute webinar with slides and FAQ. Checkout at $2,500 plus bonus versus $3,000 to talk to someone first. A three-day guarantee and next-day onboarding. Reprice coaching ~5x or drop it. Clip Q&As into 30–50 posts a week, all pointing to DMs; winners become ads.
- **Why.** Structure lifts conversion and frees founder time. Follow-up: DM time fell about 90%.
- **Source.** [If You Hate Selling, Please Watch This | Scale or Fail](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8VqSFBMMS4M&t=98s) (01:38)

##### Online tech courses for teens · from ~$700k to ~$150k a month · ROAS fell from 2–3x to ~1x

- **Situation.** Sold to mothers through paid social: $297 front end plus a $189 upsell taken by 30–40%. The owner was considering a high-ticket coaching offer.
- **Diagnosis.** Most likely a creative and pixel problem; the model is fine. The upsell is underpriced.
- **Advice.** In order: more and better creative aimed at the highest-LTV avatar; fire the pixel on the post-upsell purchase; test a ~$1,500 one-time offer (all courses or a light group bootcamp). Only if those fail, add a sales team for a $5–10k back end.
- **Why.** Even a 10% take on $1,500 adds ~$150 per order, more than the current upsell, without building a heavy back end.
- **Source.** ["Should He Walk Away From His $1.4M Business To Start Over?" | Alex Hormozi Answers Live](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0RjIw9M1j28&t=3581s) (59:41)

##### Online craft-skills membership · ~$800k/yr, 30–35% profit · owner self-limits ad spend

- **Situation.** $27/month or ~$217/year membership sold through a $10 five-day bootcamp three times a year; ~$37 cost per ticket, 22% of buyers join, LTV ~$300. Only four ads running.
- **Diagnosis.** Pure demand constraint: too little creative, hooks aimed at already-aware buyers, low front-end cash.
- **Advice.** Follow the launch spend cadence; produce 80+ ads (re-shoots, member content unlocked by a secret bonus, clipped lives, AI statics); add pain-aware hooks and call-outs; accept up to ~$100 cost per buyer; sell annual-only during launch with a physical starter kit, monthly after cart close; then test the bootcamp as a $10 seven-day trial with tool upsells.
- **Why.** If clicks cost less than they earn, it scales; ~55% trial conversion beats 22%.
- **Source.** [Building a $3,000,000 Business for a Stranger in 31 Minutes | Scale or Fail](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=j2TZMFkj71Q&t=335s) (05:35)

##### Executive career coaching · ~$7.2M/yr, ~$2M profit · wants to add enterprise B2B

- **Situation.** B2C on paid social at $3–5k a day.
- **Diagnosis.** The new avatar is an escape from a sales-efficiency problem that would follow him into B2B anyway.
- **Advice.** Stay B2C and aim to scale spend 10–20x. Fix ROAS levers one at a time. If he needs something new, add a channel and keep the avatar.
- **Why.** The B2C market is vastly larger, and doing more of what works carries the least risk.
- **Source.** [Answering Your Top Business Questions for 1 Hour](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=QwEb78e5a8Y&t=434s) (07:14)

##### Women's weight-loss program · ~$4M/yr toward a $12M goal · nervous about a big lead-magnet launch

- **Situation.** Webinar funnel plus sales team; show rate about 30%.
- **Diagnosis.** The weakest step is the opt-in at ~5%, where 20%+ is possible; show rate is already above benchmark.
- **Advice.** Test many names for the lead magnet, break it into content, and focus all testing on the opt-in page and session availability. The booking rate could double too.
- **Why.** The step with the biggest gap to benchmark gives the biggest multiple on the whole funnel.
- **Source.** ["He Makes $1.5M a Year... and Keeps 1.3% of It" | Alex Hormozi Answers Live](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=lidFtb1Ga0o&t=1576s) (26:16)

##### Online weight-loss coaching · $3.2M/yr, wants $10M · churn after ~8 months, founder takes all sales calls

- **Situation.** About 15 sales calls a week by the founder; new ads at ~$135 CAC.
- **Diagnosis.** Churn is structural in weight loss, so the game is distribution and value per customer.
- **Advice.** Pitch renewals at milestones, at the halfway point and as a last chance; offer a buy-six-get-six deal on the back end; consider adjacent sticky products; then spend hard on ads, since CAC is tiny.
- **Why.** Higher LTV and gross profit fund more aggressive acquisition.
- **Source.** ["He Makes $1.5M a Year... and Keeps 1.3% of It" | Alex Hormozi Answers Live](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=lidFtb1Ga0o&t=3279s) (54:39)

##### Coaching back end with group calls · size not stated · ~30% churn, ~7% attend calls

- **Situation.** Buyers were placed straight into group calls after the sale.
- **Diagnosis.** Low consumption caused by weak onboarding.
- **Advice.** Have the closer book onboarding. Add four to six one-on-one sessions (start with two) before the group. Book the next session at the end of each call. Use automated and manual reminders. Offer a one-on-one start as a priced option.
- **Why.** Comfort and buy-in raise attendance, and attendance drives renewal.
- **Source.** [Answering Your Top Business Questions for 1 Hour](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=QwEb78e5a8Y&t=145s) (02:25)

##### Alex's gym-owner licensing business · 100+ new gyms a month · poor-fit clients swamping the team

- **Situation.** Non-owners, part-time owners, owners without a signed lease or staff consumed team time and got poor results.
- **Diagnosis.** A fit problem at the front door.
- **Advice.** Require at least 25 clients, a signed lease, a full-time owner and ideally one employee. Return to a program with a defined end. Market average results.
- **Why.** Better-fit clients pay more, earn more, need less work and stay longer. Sales fell from 100+ to 40–50 a month while escalations dropped from 22 to zero in four weeks.
- **Source.** [Pricing Lessons From a Private Equity God](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=hnfh2jqNVEQ&t=336s) (05:36)

##### Coaching for affiliates who want brand deals · ~$1.5M/yr · a price jump crushed close rate

- **Situation.** Price moved from $4k/month to $20k for three months plus a back-end share. Close rate fell from ~96% to 20–30%, margin from 80% to ~37%.
- **Diagnosis.** The jump hurt sales velocity and the owner's morale; the avatar is fine and unsaturated.
- **Advice.** Lower the upfront fee (for example to $15k or $10k) and raise the back-end percentage. Keep the avatar and make small tweaks.
- **Why.** Two levers allow experiments and restore velocity, while changing avatar is a huge move.
- **Source.** ["He Makes $1.5M a Year... and Keeps 1.3% of It" | Alex Hormozi Answers Live](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=lidFtb1Ga0o&t=1239s) (20:39)

##### Alex's high-ticket program · ~$42k/year · team pushed to cut price to reduce churn

- **Situation.** Price was about $800 a week.
- **Diagnosis.** In hindsight, price was never driving churn.
- **Advice.** What happened: price was cut to ~$600 a week and services costing ~$100k a month were added. The lesson he draws is to leave price alone when churn has another cause.
- **Why.** Churn stayed flat, topline fell ~25% while costs rose, and about $5M a year of profit was lost.
- **Source.** [NEVER lower your prices...](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=BZQtuK-ucDM&t=92s) (01:32)

##### Coaching business with paid coaches · 40 clients at $400/month · 75% gross margin

- **Situation.** The coach was paid $4k a month to serve 40 clients.
- **Diagnosis.** Gross margin of 75% sits below his 80% rule of thumb for this kind of business.
- **Advice.** Raise price by about $20 per client per month, or cut coach pay to about $3,200.
- **Why.** A few points of gross margin produce a large relative gain in net profit.
- **Source.** [If You Don't Understand Margin, You Don't Understand Business](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=9xAMe0QBFhU&t=632s) (10:32)

##### Men's self-development brand · ~$500k/yr · strong acquisition, no renewals, losing money on retreats

- **Situation.** $2k/year community plus four retreats; webinars close 41%.
- **Diagnosis.** Nothing is sold at the events, retreats are not full, and renewals are offered too late.
- **Advice.** Package the most valued items (in-person plus online) at ~$5–6k and let members pick two or three of the four events. Fill the retreats, and sell renewals and premium upgrades at the second event.
- **Why.** Events become profitable only when you sell at them; people buy while they are in the moment.
- **Source.** ["He Makes $1.5M a Year... and Keeps 1.3% of It" | Alex Hormozi Answers Live](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=lidFtb1Ga0o&t=1003s) (16:43)

##### Real-estate education with large events · ~$1.3M/yr · nothing to buy after the mentorship

- **Situation.** Two 600-person events a year; 20% buy a $12k one-year mentorship. The owner wanted more income and had no plan to sell.
- **Diagnosis.** Customers have nothing else to buy.
- **Advice.** Double the events by adding another region. Optionally downsell graduates into a ~$3–5k annual network and access membership.
- **Why.** Running the same playbook more often has almost no operational risk; a recurring back end would let him break even on the front and outspend competitors.
- **Source.** [$100M Investor Answers The Most Important Business Questions for 1 Hour](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=--9kOXNCUdE&t=676s) (11:16)

##### Fitness coaching sold on professional-network ads · LTV ~$3k · ads stop working above ~$1,500/day

- **Situation.** $2k/$3k/$5k packages for three, six and twelve months.
- **Diagnosis.** Selling time where the goal should be sold; LTV too low to absorb diminishing returns at scale.
- **Advice.** Switch to a diagnostic sale priced weekly over the weeks needed to reach the goal, with a conditional "until you get there" guarantee. Add affiliate supplements sold at a nutrition consult about 48 hours after start. Then make more creative.
- **Why.** Selling the goal converts better and extends LTV; supplements can add about 50% revenue with no delivery work.
- **Source.** [10 Millionaires Asked Me How to Get Richer](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6m6DCQMASEM&t=95s) (01:35)

##### Executive-coaching style offer · ~$10k/month ad spend at ~3:1 · wants to switch platforms over low-salary leads

- **Situation.** Paid social brings leads with lower salaries than desired.
- **Diagnosis.** The issue is LTV; the platform is fine.
- **Advice.** First add the planned performance-based back end to lift value per customer from about $3k to $10–12k. Keep the current platform running, then test others.
- **Why.** Starting from roughly 12:1, diminishing returns at higher spend still leave a very profitable channel.
- **Source.** [10 Millionaires Asked Me How to Get Richer](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6m6DCQMASEM&t=386s) (06:26)

##### Weight-loss coaching for mothers · ~$288k/yr, needs ~$600k · tempted to change channel

- **Situation.** Tried local organic and local ads (CAC over $1k); now national ads to Instagram DMs at ~$750 CAC and ~4x ROAS.
- **Diagnosis.** He has a working channel; the risk is switching away from it.
- **Advice.** Alex first suggested a local in-person hybrid, then corrected himself: double spend on the 4x funnel and add DM setters. Do not run both.
- **Why.** When something works, put more into it. Local is only a fallback for speed to cash, and a winner stays.
- **Source.** ["He Makes $1.5M a Year... and Keeps 1.3% of It" | Alex Hormozi Answers Live](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=lidFtb1Ga0o&t=4393s) (1:13:13)

##### Travel-rewards education · ~$6.4M/yr, ~30% margin · LTV:CAC 1.4:1, more spend adds little

- **Situation.** ROAS 4.5:1, ~6 months to break even per customer, 85% of buyers through a book funnel; raising spend 50% added 10% sales; prospects find it too good to be true.
- **Diagnosis.** Demand-constrained by a creative ceiling; the outbound team is under-resourced and not prioritising leads.
- **Advice.** Ads: a member trip-video loop, variations on winners, a young short-form operator, diverse avatars, AI avatar videos. Conversion: score leads on travel and card spend, grow to ~6 callers on a parallel dialer, admit limitations early on calls, ask for a phone number in exchange for a free assessment.
- **Why.** Better creative lowers CAC; better conversion of ~300 daily prospects lifts LTV:CAC.
- **Source.** [Building a $12,000,000 Business for a Stranger in 25 Minutes](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=MH-IMJxbUY4&t=715s) (11:55)

##### Coaching for real-estate agents · ~$2.5M/yr · five-day challenge straight to checkout, wants a brand manager

- **Situation.** Delivery could handle four times the volume.
- **Diagnosis.** A pure advertising and demand play.
- **Advice.** Poach an experienced in-house brand manager. Add paid ads together with a book-a-call sales motion, because cold traffic will not convert straight to checkout.
- **Why.** Brand is a core function, and warm and cold audiences need different selling.
- **Source.** [Helping 4 Educational Business Owners Build a $1M Business in 25 Minutes](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=BYpTRiRqS1Y&t=821s) (13:41)

##### Done-with-you YouTube growth program · ~€700k/yr pace at €10k per three months · volatile months

- **Situation.** Nearly all sales from cold email; their own channel produces little.
- **Diagnosis.** Volatility comes from organic. The bigger warning sign is not succeeding with the very channel they sell.
- **Advice.** Scale cold email to about $1M, rework content to target the buying avatar (and get good at it), then add ads.
- **Why.** Education is transactional, so growth equals acquisition; views from the wrong avatar do not matter.
- **Source.** ["He Makes $1.5M a Year... and Keeps 1.3% of It" | Alex Hormozi Answers Live](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=lidFtb1Ga0o&t=2743s) (45:43)

##### Front-end sales for new real-estate wholesalers · CAC ~$5k, LTV ~$35k · prospects cannot afford the first payment

- **Situation.** Close rate very low; an unqualified lead flow drains the sales team.
- **Diagnosis.** Missing qualification tied to a defined avatar.
- **Advice.** Derive the avatar from the highest-spending, longest-staying customers (demographics, income and other quantifiables, geography, behaviour in the sales process). Put that friction into ads, landing pages, form drop-downs and the VSL. Send the best leads to sales and the rest to an automated self-liquidating offer.
- **Why.** Qualification raises cost per call but lowers CAC and raises LTV; he cannot recall it ever losing him money. Being willing to invest in yourself is no qualifier.
- **Source.** [How to Build a Business That Runs Without You](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=TLpLmhWqKEU&t=3357s) (55:57)

##### Content-template membership for real-estate agents · ~$20k/month at $49 · 12% monthly churn, thinking of a new niche

- **Situation.** About 300 members, ~50 sales a month; a similar product in another niche grew 5x.
- **Diagnosis.** Sales velocity is too low for the market, and churn likely clusters in part-time agents. Too early to judge the niche.
- **Advice.** Stay and learn. Find the two or three traits that separate churners from stayers; target agents with a recent sale. Push annual prepay (with ~8-month LTV, four months free is equivalent and pulls cash forward). Add a $197–397 offer on the thank-you page, test ads to learn CAC, and consider a $500–1,000/month AI-assisted managed upsell.
- **Why.** The market leader has a tiny share of the market; blaming the avatar is the easy answer.
- **Source.** [How to Build a Business That Runs Without You](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=TLpLmhWqKEU&t=5187s) (1:26:27)

##### Motocross training company · a few million a year · doubled one-day tour dates, fears a copycat

- **Situation.** One-day tours grew from 70 to 140 dates with heavy operational drag; each five-day camp netted $100k+.
- **Diagnosis.** The bottom ~40 tour dates were negative or negligible, and the business was underpriced.
- **Advice.** Shift to five-day camps, raise prices, and let the competitor copy the low-value format.
- **Why.** He tied it to his own costliest mistake: matching an undercutting competitor by cutting price for an existing recurring base, which cost a large share of monthly revenue, several million in annual profit and tens of millions in exit value, with no change in churn.
- **Source.** [Helping 4 Educational Business Owners Build a $1M Business in 25 Minutes](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=BYpTRiRqS1Y&t=435s) (07:15)

##### Local house-flipping ecosystem with a coaching program · ~$4M/yr · wants national scale for impact

- **Situation.** Contracting, trades companies and local coaching; the owner is stuck in operations with a new COO.
- **Diagnosis.** The local market is not yet dominated, so national ambition is premature, and prices are far too low.
- **Advice.** Ignore national for now. Use spare capacity to sell many more people locally through paid ads and bigger workshops. Move to standard tiers (around $15–25k done-with-you, ~$100k turnkey). Use content for impact.
- **Why.** The local market could support a very large business; expanding too early collapses, and content scales impact for free.
- **Source.** [Helping 4 Educational Business Owners Build a $1M Business in 25 Minutes](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=BYpTRiRqS1Y&t=145s) (02:25)

##### Weight-loss info-product creator · ~$4M/yr · planning apparel, supplements and creams

- **Situation.** A large following and a plateaued core offer.
- **Diagnosis.** The same "I don't know how to grow the core" problem, disguised as diversification.
- **Advice.** Put all effort into growing the existing offer toward $10M instead of launching new lines.
- **Why.** A new line does not fix the skill gap that caused the plateau.
- **Source.** [Entrepreneurs, stop starting new businesses. Fix the one you've got.](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=s59jbVBprn0&t=287s) (04:47)

##### Solo founder of book-writing retreats and media · ~$1M last year · keeps starting new things

- **Situation.** Runs luxury retreats, a publishing house, a TV show and a business school, and gets bored easily.
- **Diagnosis.** Energy split across too many businesses; demand and margin clearly sit in the book business.
- **Advice.** Pick the book business and stop pushing the school. Use the TV show as media to sell book-writing and make more seasons. Put creative energy into marketing and keep a private list for new ideas.
- **Why.** Concentrated effort compounds, the market is already voting with money, and every change has a guaranteed cost.
- **Source.** [Why Doing More Is Actually Making You Less Money](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=hfwZwPGsbIo&t=242s) (04:02)

##### Alex's gym-owner licensing business · ~$1M a week · founder-led brand on one ad channel

- **Situation.** 100% of sales came from paid social and the founder appeared in every ad; the founders wanted an exit. Ads underperformed during the pandemic.
- **Diagnosis.** A guru brand more than a business: founder dependency and single-channel risk would cap the price.
- **Advice.** Hire someone from an outbound-led competitor to build an outbound department from scratch and stick with it through slow early months (keep going as long as each funnel stage improves). Take the founder's face off the door with specialists, ex-client leads and new faces in ads.
- **Why.** Within about 12 months outbound was roughly half of revenue, which reassured the acquirer.
- **Source.** [How I Scaled My Sales Team \[Compensation, Recruiting & Training\]](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=okA9Yt2KZuk&t=139s) (02:19); [I Reverse Engineered the Perfect Business](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=HKbFUWJwEG0&t=1297s) (21:37)

##### Education business teaching people to run event venues · size not stated · founder wants to sell it

- **Situation.** The founder sold his own three venues and now teaches others to do the same.
- **Diagnosis.** He is trying to sell the goose (founder-led education); the sellable assets are the venues his students run.
- **Advice.** Standardise students' systems, branding and prices, and offer the top 10% a path to be rolled up in groups of 20–50 and sold periodically.
- **Why.** Venues are faceless, turnkey and proven sellable. He does the roll-up work while keeping the education business.
- **Source.** [How to Build a Valuable Company You Can Sell Someday](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=VxKwz6hBVZU&t=48s) (00:48)

##### Education business teaching marketplace sellers · size not stated · founder wants to sell it

- **Situation.** A former online-store owner now teaches others to build stores.
- **Diagnosis.** The education business is not recurring, carries key-person risk and volatile acquisition: few buyers, low price.
- **Advice.** Bundle the students' stores into a sellable asset.
- **Why.** Scaling something already sellable is much easier than fixing something inherently unsellable.
- **Source.** [How to Build a Valuable Company You Can Sell Someday](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=VxKwz6hBVZU&t=193s) (03:13)

##### Media company with a stable of talent · size not stated · wants to become sellable

- **Situation.** Revenue depends on on-screen stars.
- **Diagnosis.** Stars generate impressions and cannot be sold.
- **Advice.** Route impressions into owned products (an incubator of products) or sell ad inventory; raise a small stake to fund launches.
- **Why.** Impressions are the goose and products are the eggs; products can be valued and sold.
- **Source.** [How to Build a Valuable Company You Can Sell Someday](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=VxKwz6hBVZU&t=621s) (10:21)

##### Enterprise building-efficiency software · same revenue for ~8 years · plans to double the sales team

- **Situation.** About 10 demos a week, about two closes a year, ~98% revenue retention.
- **Diagnosis.** Leads are fine. The pitch goes to owners while building managers hold the real decision power and have no incentive to take on more work.
- **Advice.** Do not hire more reps. Rescript the demo to raise the manager's status and make it about them.
- **Why.** Turning hundreds of demos into many more closes is a far bigger lever than doubling reps.
- **Source.** [How to Grow Your Business So FAST it Makes Your Accountant Nervous](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=sGv2BTUCcCM&t=1240s) (20:40)

##### Early-stage SaaS that reduces sales-call no-shows · pre-scale · founder from high-ticket education

- **Situation.** The founder knows marketing and sales from education.
- **Diagnosis.** A different game: a product and retention business.
- **Advice.** Use early marketing to learn, find the cohort that stays, retarget ideal customers, and validate the co-founder's track record.
- **Why.** If users do not stick, nothing else matters.
- **Source.** [7 Millionaires Asked Me How to Get Rich(er)](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=bgBIO6nZawg&t=1495s) (24:55)

##### Creator tools, agency and label · ~$4.3M/yr · 40,000 creators, ~1,000 earning, considering SaaS

- **Situation.** Several business lines serving creators plus brand-side revenue.
- **Diagnosis.** Too many businesses at once; marketplaces usually fail unless they first work as SaaS.
- **Advice.** Either charge a subscription with a free tier, or go high-ticket with white-glove onboarding for selected creators. Keep the brand-side revenue, then go all in on one path.
